# Risk Rank 04 — Original Profile 08

**Severity:** CRITICAL — LOAD-BEARING  
**Primary defendant exposure:** The same treating neurologist knew the unresolved relationship, received the Protest, authored the durable account, continued management and later defended the history.

## Profile 08 — Profile of the Treating Neurologist's Knowledge

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-140053).md -->

<!-- Original source heading: Profile of the Treating Neurologist's Knowledge -->

### The Protest, the Record, and Eight Years of Treatment Inside an Incomplete Representation

Thomas Monaghan occupies a unique position in this case.

He is not a later clinician trying to infer what happened in July 2017 from somebody else's notes.

He was the treating neurologist.

He received the patient.

He examined him.

He recognised the Parkinsonian syndrome.

He recorded the dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenon.

He knew the longstanding foot history.

He knew the feet remained problematic.

He was the neurologist whom the patient caused to be summoned at discharge specifically because the feet had not been resolved.

He then authored the definitive account of the admission.

And he remained involved in the patient's neurological management for the following eight years.

That means the proper question is not merely:

> **What did the record say?**

It is:

> **What did Monaghan know at each point, what representation of that knowledge did he create, and which representation subsequently governed his treatment of the patient?**

### 1. What came into his hands

The information arriving at Neurology was already integrated.

The GP had been considering Neurology for:

> **“gait, feet and ‘muscle tension’ issues.”**

The patient had bilateral painful abnormal feet, movement-initiation difficulty, increased tone, toe-clawing, gait abnormality, deteriorating dexterity and threatened occupational function. The referral did not separate these into independently settled diseases.

So before Monaghan had interpreted anything, his knowledge base already contained:

> **feet + gait + tone + toes + movement + function.**

That is the incoming phenotype.

### 2. The admission: one direct examination of the feet

On the project reconstruction, the physical count is stark:

> **Focused direct examination of the feet during the eight-day admission: one.**

That is the first-day examination.

The surviving account records longstanding:

-  corns and calluses;
-  fallen arches;
-  years of foot treatment;
-  continuing foot problems;
-  and a history of significant pain.

The physical findings include structural abnormality such as pes planus and hammertoes.

There is no second focused foot examination during the admission.

There is no return on day three.

No return after the MRI.

No return after the complex-case discussion.

No return after PRKN enters the differential.

No return before physical discharge.

That is the first knowledge asymmetry.

### 3. One explicit neurological mapping of the lower-limb/foot phenomenon

The neurological examination was entirely different in intensity.

It became extremely detailed.

And critically, Monaghan himself records:

> **“The gait is strikingly abnormal.”**

The patient walks:

> **“almost with a footdrop appearance somewhat suggestive of a dystonic posture - NOT FOOT DROP.”**

There is therefore at least **one explicit neurological mapping of the presenting lower-limb/foot territory** during admission:

> apparent footdrop
>
> → not weakness/footdrop
>
> → possible dystonic posture.

The examination also identifies severe rigidity, reduced arm swing, Parkinsonian posturing, bradykinesia and other signs. It was sufficiently significant to be video-recorded.

So the admission count becomes extremely revealing:

> **Direct focused foot examinations: 1.**
>
> **Explicit neurological mapping of the lower-limb/footdrop phenomenon: 1.**
>
> **Documented repeat focused foot examinations after that: 0.**
>
> **Documented repeat attempts during the admission to partition structural foot disease from dynamic dystonic foot/lower-limb behaviour: 0.**

That is the knowledge state before the Protest.

Monaghan does not lack the structural fact.

He does not lack the dynamic neurological fact.

He lacks the completed **relationship between them**.

### 4. Meanwhile everything around the feet expands

Across the eight days, Monaghan's knowledge of the *rest* of the case grows dramatically.

There is:

-  severe young-onset Parkinsonism;
-  CT;
-  MRI brain;
-  MRI cervical spine;
-  extensive laboratory testing;
-  metabolic and genetic reasoning;
-  complex-case discussion;
-  video documentation;
-  consideration of recessive disease;
-  PRKN/PARK2;
-  treatment planning;
-  Physiotherapy;
-  Occupational Therapy;
-  long-term work planning;
-  and senior advice regarding dopaminergic treatment.

Monaghan then writes externally to Prof Dan Healy asking about genetic testing and a possible second opinion. He says:

> **“Myself and Tim are keen to hold back on Levodopa insofar as maybe possible unless you'd advise otherwise?”**

and sends the video.

The Parkinsonian problem is therefore repeatedly elaborated.

The foot relationship is not.

### 5. What Monaghan knows immediately before the Protest

By the end of the admission Monaghan knows all of these propositions simultaneously:

The patient has genuine severe Parkinsonism.

The patient is extremely young.

PRKN is plausible.

The gait includes a phenomenon that may be dystonic.

The feet are genuinely structurally abnormal.

The feet have been painful for years.

The GP sent the feet inside the neurological presentation.

The feet have only been directly examined once.

The structural-dynamic relationship has not been established.

And the evolving disposition is effectively:

> **possibly separate / perhaps neuropathy / “Not clear” / Podiatry.**

That is already a fragile information state.

Then the Protest happens.

### 6. The Protest changes Monaghan's personal knowledge

This is where the case becomes actor-specific.

The patient does not merely mutter dissatisfaction.

He deliberately causes the treating neurologist to be fetched.

He confronts Monaghan with the unresolved feet.

The NCT/bald-tyres analogy says, in substance:

> **You have performed an extraordinary overall investigation but are discharging me while the obvious presenting problem remains unresolved.**

The later patient account records the exchange in precisely those terms.

After that encounter, Monaghan's knowledge is materially different.

Before:

> **The relationship is unresolved.**

After:

> **The relationship is unresolved, and the patient has personally told me that he regards that unresolved state as evidence that the admission has not dealt with the presenting feet.**

That second proposition cannot be produced from the first.

It is new information.

And it belongs specifically to Monaghan because the objection was made directly to him.

### 7. The Protest therefore becomes part of the doctor-patient relationship

This is the point I think you are reaching.

The Protest is not merely an external “patient perspective.”

Once spoken directly to the treating neurologist, it becomes a fact of the clinical relationship.

Monaghan thereafter has two sources of knowledge about the case.

#### The clinical model

> Parkinsonism + structural feet + possible separate neuropathy + “Not clear” + Podiatry + cautious dopaminergic strategy.

#### The relational fact

> **The patient has expressly challenged me that this model has failed to resolve the presenting feet.**

Both are real.

Neither automatically eliminates the other.

But both bear on the future treatment relationship.

### 8. Then Monaghan creates a record containing only one of them

This is the critical transformation.

The definitive synthesis preserves:

-  Parkinsonism;
-  the dystonic-looking gait;
-  PRKN reasoning;
-  structural feet;
-  “Not clear”;
-  Podiatry;
-  treatment caution;
-  senior input;
-  extensive diagnostic reasoning;
-  the patient's intelligence;
-  his ability to question;
-  his understanding of the levodopa policy;
-  “firmer footing”;
-  “clear plan”;
-  and “As per above.”

It does **not** preserve:

> **The patient expressly challenged the treating neurologist before discharge that the feet had not been adequately dealt with.**

So from this point onward there are two histories.

#### Encounter history

Monaghan knows the patient challenged the sufficiency of the foot disposition.

#### Documentary history

The admission produced a highly intelligent, appropriately questioning patient who understood the treatment rationale and left under a clear plan in which the feet remained “Not clear” and were routed toward Podiatry.

Those histories overlap enormously.

But they are not identical.

### 9. The discharge summary is therefore not “ultimate reality”

This distinction is fundamental.

A clinical document can be highly authoritative.

It can guide GPs.

It can guide future specialists.

It can guide its own author.

It can accurately contain enormous amounts of information.

But it remains a **representation produced by a human actor**.

It does not acquire the power to erase events that occurred outside its selected contents.

The fact that the Protest is absent from Monaghan's letter does not make the Protest absent from Monaghan's 2017 knowledge.

The letter is downstream of his knowledge.

It cannot retrospectively become the source of all that he knew.

That is why the discharge document must never be allowed to become **ultimate reality** in the reconstruction.

It is an information object.

The bedside relationship preceded it.

### 10. The next clinical development is particularly important

The patient improves markedly on neurological treatment.

Then procyclidine is stopped.

Monaghan's later clinical documentation records:

> **“the first thing he noticed changing and changing rapidly was the return on the foot, of the outside left corn.”**

The patient also becomes:

> **“more ‘shifty’ ‘unrelaxed’ off the Proclyidine.”**

This is actor-specific evidence.

This is not merely something another service knew.

It appears in Monaghan's own neurological material.

So Monaghan now possesses another piece of information bearing directly upon the unresolved 2017 boundary:

> **neurological medication state alters the focal foot territory.**

And because Monaghan also knows the Protest occurred, the new information does not arrive into a neutral historical background.

It arrives into a case in which the patient had already told him:

> **you have not resolved my feet.**

That is what changes the significance of the 2017–18 treatment observation.

### 11. Yet the discharge architecture remains usable

The new fact does not require the existing model to collapse.

That is the remarkable property of the discharge architecture.

The outside-left-foot phenomenon can be neurologically modifiable.

The Parkinsonian syndrome can be treatment responsive.

The feet can remain structurally abnormal.

And the underlying painful-foot relationship can still remain:

> **Not clear.**

This is the beginning of the pattern we have been identifying:

> **local update without global reconstruction.**

The record learns something new.

The foundational relationship does not need to be rewritten.

### 12. The importance of Monaghan being the same neurologist

This would be a different case if every later observation were received by a succession of clinicians with no access to the originating encounter.

But Monaghan remains in the chain.

That means the information is accumulating around an actor who was present at the origin.

He does not have to infer that there once was an unresolved-foot problem.

He was there.

He does not have to infer that the patient cared about it.

The patient told him directly.

He does not have to infer that the lower-limb presentation may have been neurological.

He himself documented a possible dystonic posture.

He does not have to infer later that neurological medication can alter the foot territory.

His own later record captures exactly that.

This makes his knowledge state qualitatively different from that of a later reader of the chart.

### 13. March 2023: the original question returns directly to him

By March 2023 the feet become catastrophically painful.

Brennan's urgent referral is addressed to **Thomas Monaghan**.

It says the patient has severe bilateral burning feet and lower-leg pain, describes extreme distress and a “death wish,” says:

> **“I am worried about him”**

and recalls an earlier episode where pains settled with better control of Parkinsonism.

That information therefore enters Monaghan's treatment environment directly.

And again, for Monaghan, it has a history that another clinician might not possess.

He knows this is the patient who challenged him at discharge over unresolved feet.

That is part of the actor-specific context whether or not it appears in the referral.

### 14. September 2023: Monaghan receives an explicit OFF-state interpretation

The later Movement Disorders correspondence becomes even more direct.

David Folan emails Monaghan that the patient is again complaining of severe pain in his feet, reduced mobility and shuffling, and that he can be worse in the morning or later in the day if Sinemet has not been taken.

Folan's conclusion is:

> **“It sounds like his Parkinson's is being undertreated and the leg pain and shuffling is off related.”**

He asks about restarting Stalevo.

Monaghan answers:

> **“I think that it is reasonable to try please.”**

So by September 2023 the knowledge chain in Monaghan's hands includes:

2017 dystonic-looking lower limb;

2017 unresolved painful structural feet;

2017 direct patient Protest about those feet;

2017/18 neurological medication altering the outside-left-foot phenomenon;

2023 catastrophic foot pain;

2023 pain and shuffling interpreted by Movement Disorders as OFF-related;

and Monaghan himself approving the dopaminergic response to that interpretation.

Again, the original “Not clear” relationship is not globally reconstructed.

### 15. March 2025: Monaghan states the mature frame himself

At the 10 March 2025 clinic encounter, Monaghan knows the feet improve when Sinemet takes effect.

His later rebuttal quotes the contemporaneous formulation:

> **“He knows very well that once the Sinemet hits his system that the feet feel better from a pain perspective.”**

Yet the same account retains:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

The response expressly states that the team had:

> **“a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.”**

That is the fully mature descendant of the 2017 information state:

> **neurological modulation: accepted;**

> **underlying structural/peripheral possibility: retained;**

> **overarching relationship: still not globally reconstructed.**

And Monaghan remains the same actor who received the bedside Protest.

### 16. Then the patient complains to the same neurologist

The 19 March 2025 complaint formally challenges the history.

QPS sends it to Monaghan.

His 25 March response is therefore especially revealing.

He does not say the 2017 letters are obsolete.

He does not say the historical frame must now be rebuilt.

He says:

> **“I feel my own clinical reasoning will be evident to the reviewer…”**

He says the long letters exist to provide:

> **“clear documentation in terms of records for knowing how decision were made and when and by who.”**

And:

> **“I hope that the information contained within (In this account and in all of the letters from 2017) will be able to deliver the answers that Mr Moloney requests.”**

That is the moment the relationship between Monaghan and his own record becomes explicit.

The 2017 representation is no longer merely a historical document.

It is being used by its author as a **memory and justification device**.

### 17. This produces an extraordinary closed loop

Consider the sequence.

At the bedside:

> **patient tells Monaghan the foot disposition is inadequate.**

In the final record:

> **that challenge is absent.**

Across the years:

> **Monaghan manages the patient within the documentary architecture created by that record while incorporating later observations locally.**

In 2025:

> **the patient challenges the resulting history.**

Monaghan responds:

> **look at the old letters; the reasoning will be evident there.**

But those letters are the very representation from which the original challenge is absent.

So the record begins to validate itself.

That is not merely a rhetorical problem.

It is an informational one.

> **An omitted fact cannot challenge a document if the document later becomes the principal evidence used to reconstruct whether that fact mattered.**

### 18. “I cannot claim to remember everything” actually increases the importance of the omission

This sentence in the 2025 response needs to be treated carefully.

Monaghan says:

> **“I cannot claim to remember everything.”**

That prevents us from proving continuous conscious recollection of the Protest at every appointment.

But it makes the record problem more important, not less.

Because if human memory naturally fades, the clinical record becomes the mechanism by which the past is carried forward.

And Monaghan says exactly that: the letters are designed to document decisions, bridge the gap between what was intended and what was understood, and allow later readers to know what happened.

Therefore:

> **if the Protest was omitted from the record, fading human recollection does not neutralise the omission; it magnifies its downstream effect.**

The clinician can eventually become dependent upon his own incomplete representation.

The omission can become self-blinding.

### 19. The relevant fact is notice, not permanent conscious recall

This distinction gives us the strongest evidential formulation.

We do **not** need to say:

> Monaghan consciously remembered the NCT analogy every day from 2017 to 2025.

That cannot be shown.

What is fixed is:

> **Monaghan was personally put on notice in 2017 that the patient regarded the unresolved feet as an inadequately completed presenting problem.**

Nothing later makes that historical notice cease to have occurred.

The subsequent question is:

> **Did the clinical information system preserve that notice sufficiently for it to bear on future management?**

The answer from the discharge synthesis is no.

And because Monaghan later relies on that synthesis as the history of his reasoning, the omission becomes longitudinally material.

### 20. Two simultaneous realities govern the doctor-patient relationship

From July 2017 onward there are therefore two legitimate information objects.

#### Bedside reality

The patient came with integrated feet/gait/tone problems.

The feet were examined once.

A dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenon was recognised.

The structural-dynamic relationship remained unresolved.

The patient directly challenged Monaghan over that unresolved state before leaving.

#### Documentary reality

Young-onset Parkinsonism was diagnosed and extensively reasoned.

The painful feet may be separate or neuropathic.

Their relationship is “Not clear.”

Podiatry will address the structural component.

The dopaminergic policy is carefully justified.

The patient is highly intelligent, asks appropriate questions and understands the strategy.

There is a clear plan.

“As per above.”

The second is a representation of the first.

It is not superior to it merely because it is typed, signed and transmitted.

### 21. Monaghan then treats the patient within documentary reality

This needs one refinement from the strongest version of the proposition.

It would be too absolute to say Monaghan never treated on anything other than the discharge summary.

He clearly acquired new information.

He changed medications.

He accepted new symptom observations.

He approved OFF-related treatment changes.

He recognised that Sinemet affected pain.

So the record **does update**.

But those updates occur **within the architecture established by the discharge synthesis**.

That is the factual pattern.

The foundational propositions remain available:

> Parkinsonism is the principal disease model;

> dopaminergic treatment must be restrained;

> the painful feet may still have another substrate;

> peripheral neuropathy remains conditional;

> and neurological modulation of pain does not necessarily reorganise its underlying cause.

Thus new facts are added without restoring the omitted proposition:

> **the patient challenged from the beginning that the foot relationship itself had not been adequately resolved.**

That is much more exact than saying the record never changed.

### 22. This explains why the Protest is materially relevant to every later event

Once Monaghan has personal notice of the Protest, later events do not occur in a historical vacuum.

When procyclidine changes the outside-left-foot phenomenon, it bears upon a relationship already challenged.

When bilateral foot pain becomes severe again, it bears upon a relationship already challenged.

When Brennan reconnects pain to Parkinsonian tone, it bears upon a relationship already challenged.

When Movement Disorders says pain and shuffling are OFF-related, it bears upon a relationship already challenged.

When Sinemet improves the feet, it bears upon a relationship already challenged.

When Sinemet CR gives profound relief, it bears upon a relationship already challenged.

The later observation does not need to prove what the patient said in 2017.

The Protest supplies the continuity proposition:

> **this was never merely a new foot problem appearing years later; the adequacy of the original foot disposition had been disputed to the treating neurologist before the foundational document was completed.**

That changes the longitudinal meaning of every subsequent integrating signal.

### 23. And yet the Protest is absent from the interpretive machinery

This is the material omission.

Not simply:

> **a patient complaint wasn't written down.**

But:

> **the information needed to tell later clinicians that the foundational “Not clear” was itself contested as inadequately investigated was absent from the very document that governed how later information would be interpreted.**

That is why the omission bears directly upon care.

The record told future Monaghan, future GP, future Movement Disorders clinicians and future reviewers:

> what Neurology concluded.

It did not tell them:

> **that before that conclusion became final and portable, the patient directly told its author that the unresolved-foot component had not been satisfactorily investigated.**

### 24. The 2025 rebuttal proves the discharge summary remained operative

The treating neurologist himself gives us the final control.

He goes back to 2017.

He bolds and underlines the old levodopa passage.

He describes the later history as continuation of the same Sinemet-sparing strategy.

He says the old letters show why decisions were made.

He states that Sinemet has long been known to help the pain.

He retains:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

and concludes that he is not sure the history amounts to:

> **“Diagnostic Anchoring, Bias, and Failure to Reassess.”**

This is not merely evidence that the discharge summary existed.

It is evidence that **its core logic was still being actively used by its author eight years later to explain the patient's care.**

### The profile of Monaghan's knowledge

> **Thomas Monaghan received an integrated feet–gait–tone presentation in July 2017, directly examined the structurally abnormal feet once, and neurologically mapped the striking lower-limb gait as resembling footdrop while being suggestive of dystonic posture. Across the remainder of an eight-day admission the Parkinsonian, genetic and treatment questions expanded extensively, while no second focused foot examination or documented structural–dynamic partition followed. By discharge the painful-foot relationship remained “Not clear” and was practically routed toward Podiatry. The patient then personally caused Monaghan to be summoned and challenged that exact incompleteness before leaving. From that point Monaghan possessed knowledge that the residual uncertainty was not merely residual: the patient regarded it as evidence that the presenting problem had not been completed. Monaghan subsequently authored the later-finalised consultant synthesis that preserved “Not clear,” Podiatry, the dopaminergic strategy, patient-understanding language, “firmer footing,” a “clear plan” and “As per above,” but not the contest. He then remained the treating neurologist while accumulating further information bearing on the same boundary: his own record captured rapid return of the outside-left-foot phenomenon on procyclidine withdrawal; severe foot pain later returned to his care; Movement Disorders directly told him that foot/leg pain and shuffling sounded OFF-related and he approved dopaminergic adjustment; by 2025 he himself stated that Sinemet had long been known to help the patient's pain while peripheral neuropathy remained only an “if.” When formally challenged, he expressly returned to the 2017 letters as the documents in which his clinical reasoning should be evident. The longitudinal problem is therefore not that Monaghan never learned new facts. He repeatedly did. It is that those facts were incorporated within a documentary architecture that omitted his own prior notice that the adequacy of the foot disposition had been contested before finalisation. The discharge document governed the continuing representation of the doctor-patient relationship, but it was never the entirety of that relationship.**

And I think the irreducible formulation is this:

> **Monaghan knew two histories.**
>
> **One was lived at the bedside: the feet were unresolved and the patient directly challenged him about it.**
>
> **The other was the history he authored: “Not clear,” Podiatry, clear plan, patient understanding, “As per above.”**
>
> **Only the second history became portable. He then treated, remembered and ultimately defended the case through that portable history.**

The deepest point is not that the discharge summary was “wrong” in every sentence.

It is:

> **Monaghan created a representation of his relationship with the patient that omitted a material event known only because that patient had personally put it into the relationship. Once that representation became the record, later care could be faithful to the record while becoming progressively less faithful to the full history that produced it.**

That is where the Protest becomes longitudinally material rather than merely historical.

# Risk Rank 05 — Original Profile 38

**Severity:** CRITICAL — LOAD-BEARING  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Shows that the record carries not only the plan but a compatible patient knowledge-state; the founding contrary patient state is omitted while understanding, counselling and prior knowledge are repeatedly preserved.

## Profile 38 — The Patient as a Documentary Knowledge-State: Selective Construction, Attributional Inflation and Epistemic Management

<!-- SOURCE: synthesis developed in project conversation on 4 September 2026. Grounded principally in PRIMARY_2017_REFERRAL.pdf, PRIMARY_2017_DISCHARGE.pdf, the longitudinal clinical corpus transcribed in SRC_COMPLETE_RECORD_FORENSIC.md, PRIMARY_2025_MONAGHAN_SUPPORT.pdf, and the patient-evidence Protest material. This profile analyses documentary function. It does not assume that every patient-state statement is inaccurate or that recording patient understanding is itself improper. -->

### The hypothesis

For the purpose of analysis, treat the patient's **state of mind** as a recurrent clinical-documentary object.

Ask, each time the record says that the patient:

> knows;
>
> understands;
>
> is aware;
>
> has been counselled;
>
> remembers;
>
> prefers;
>
> wishes;
>
> fears;
>
> accepts;
>
> is happy;
>
> has already been told;
>
> or has a shared understanding,

what documentary work that proposition performs.

Under this lens, a striking pattern emerges.

The record does not merely document symptoms and treatments. It repeatedly stabilises management by documenting a corresponding **patient knowledge-state**: what the patient is said to understand about the diagnosis, risks, causation, treatment restraint, available options, and the reasons for decisions.

This is not inherently improper. Good medicine requires communication and informed patient participation.

The evidential question is narrower:

> **How faithfully does the authored patient knowledge-state reproduce the patient's actual position, and when does it add separation, continuity, prior knowledge, assent or ownership beyond what the underlying evidence establishes?**

### 1. The primitive patient position was not a clean partition

The April–June 2017 primary-care record presents:

> **feet + pain + abnormal gait + function.**

The 29 June referral states that the patient was attending with bilateral foot issues and:

> **“blamed his gait at that time on callouses and corns (his feet are not in a good state).”**

`SRC_COMPLETE_RECORD_FORENSIC.md`, CLIN-000851–000854.

The admission history then records a genuine phenomenological distinction.

After leaving bar work in 2012, the foot pain reduced substantially while the internal leg tension continued. The history says:

> **“he began to realise that the foot pain and the leg tension were not the same problem.”**

CLIN-000971–000976.

These propositions are compatible.

A patient can accurately say:

> **my weight-bearing foot pain and my internal leg tension behave differently**

while also saying:

> **my feet and my gait are related and remain part of the same unresolved clinical problem.**

Distinguishing symptom experiences is not the same as establishing causal independence.

### 2. “Not the same problem” becomes “markedly divorced”

The later impression states:

> **“However he has in his own mind - markedly divorced the feelings of tension in the legs … from the ‘corns and callouses’ issues and pains in the feet.”**

CLIN-001146–001149.

The source material therefore supports a patient-derived distinction.

But the formulation adds two things.

**“Divorced”** is stronger than the patient's recorded phrase **“not the same problem.”**

**“Markedly”** is an authored intensifier.

The underlying evidence is patient-derived.

The degree and documentary meaning of separation are clinician-constructed.

This is the first clear example of what this profile terms **attributional inflation**:

> **a genuine patient statement or event is retained, but the record attributes to the patient a stronger state of separation, prior knowledge, assent or continuity than the recoverable underlying evidence establishes.**

### 3. The construction is selective because it supports the emerging partition

The phrase does not appear in isolation.

Immediately around it the record says that the corns, callouses and burning may have been:

> **“completely separate or perhaps a secondary peripheral neuropathy”**

and concludes:

> **“Not clear.”**

CLIN-001142–001149.

The patient's own mind is therefore made to supply support for the same directional separation that the clinician is considering.

This does not make the phrase fabricated.

It makes it **selective and constructive**.

A real patient distinction is selected, intensified and deployed in a way that supports the clinician's developing partition.

At the same time the same admission has already recognised a lower-limb phenomenon suggestive of dystonic posture, severe young-onset Parkinsonism and a live PRKN question.

Those facts could have supported integration of the lower limb, gait and painful structural feet.

They do not receive an equivalent patient-state formulation.

### 4. The “good understanding” does not establish understanding of the foot partition

The discharge synthesis later says:

> **“[Patient] has a good understanding of why we wished to delay the commencement of levodopa or dopamine agonist type treatments at least for now.”**

CLIN-001601–001602.

This statement is precise.

It certifies understanding of the **dopaminergic treatment strategy**.

It does not say that the patient understood or accepted:

> the painful feet as clinically separate;
>
> peripheral neuropathy as the correct explanation;
>
> Podiatry as a complete disposition;
>
> or the structural/dystonic relationship as resolved.

The distinction matters because later documentary continuity can make “good understanding” sound broader than the proposition actually recorded.

The patient's understanding of treatment restraint must not be silently converted into assent to the entire case partition.

### 5. The record repeatedly certifies the patient's capacity to receive the clinician's account

The same 2017 synthesis describes the patient as:

> **“particularly very intelligent”;**

having:

> **“a very prescient understanding”;**

able to identify when too much information is being supplied;

and someone who will:

> **“question very appropriately with exceptional insight.”**

CLIN-001583–001588 and related duplicated discharge-text anchors.

The admission is said to have:

> **“suited us and [Patient] very well”**

and to have allowed him to learn a great deal.

CLIN-001433 onward / duplicated CLIN-002178 onward.

This creates a documentary picture of a patient who is not merely informed but unusually capable of understanding the management account.

That can legitimately strengthen evidence that real counselling occurred.

It also means that omissions of contrary patient reasoning become more important, because the record itself has established that this patient's questions are clinically meaningful.

### 6. The record expressly manages how future information should be interpreted

The counselling section asks the patient not to:

> **“over interpret”**

possible adverse-effect information for procyclidine and says:

> **“He has been warned of the ‘nocebo’ effect as well.”**

CLIN-001596–001600.

This is explicit prospective management of interpretation.

The clinical purpose may be entirely reasonable: prevent an intelligent patient from over-reading adverse-effect lists that may not apply well to him.

But under the present lens it is notable that the record is not merely giving information.

It is instructing the patient how information should be cognitively weighted.

### 7. “Fully counselled” repeatedly functions as a documentary completion state

The 2017 treatment plan contains formulations such as:

> **“If [Patient] is happy to start (fully counselled on its nature and role and limitations and possible adverse effects…)”**

for treatment choices.

CLIN-001191 onward.

The record later repeatedly says the patient has been:

> **“fully counselled on all of the above”**

and notes that he will have a copy of the letter at home.

In 2021, immediately before the DBS paragraph:

> **“[Patient] has been fully counselled on all of the above and indeed presently will have a copy of this directly at home.”**

CLIN-002359.

This phrase performs more than a narrative function.

It certifies an information state:

> **the relevant explanation has been delivered and the patient possesses it.**

That certification can later become evidence of informed continuity.

### 8. Risk-awareness is repeatedly placed inside the patient's mind

Across the longitudinal record, management risks are not only stated as clinician concerns.

They are repeatedly documented as things the patient knows or is aware of.

Examples include:

> **“[Patient] was well aware of what might be happening and he was on the lookout for it”**

in relation to an anticipated treatment risk — CLIN-002124;

> **“[Patient] knows that he had this tendency to have the ‘swinging’ (~Choreoform) on the higher doses of the dopamine agonist”** — CLIN-002138;

> **“He is well aware of the impulse control disorder concerns”** — CLIN-002347;

and cognitive effects of Kemadrin which:

> **“he has always been aware of”** — CLIN-002354.

Again, these may all be accurate.

The pattern is what matters.

Risk becomes a **shared knowledge object**, not merely a clinician's private justification.

That shared state then supports restraint.

### 9. “As I have reminded him” is the second clear attributional-inflation specimen

The 27 October 2021 note says:

> **“He mentioned about DBS today. As I have reminded him, the DBS is only ever for the tremor element. It will not help with anything else.”**

CLIN-002360.

The patient raising DBS is a recoverable event in that consultation.

The phrase **“reminded him”** adds a distinct historical proposition:

> **this is not new information; the patient has previously been told and already knows it.**

The searchable corpus identified in this project presently contains the DBS term only in that paragraph. That does not prove no earlier oral discussion occurred under another wording or outside the recovered corpus.

It does mean that the prior knowledge asserted by **“reminded”** presently lacks recovered documentary provenance.

So the documentary progression is:

> **patient raises DBS → clinician supplies a categorical meaning → record characterises that meaning as already resident in the patient's mind.**

That is structurally similar to:

> **patient distinguishes two experiences → record characterises the distinction as a marked divorce already resident in the patient's mind.**

One constructs stronger separation.

The other constructs prior knowledge.

### 10. The same DBS passage then transfers practical initiative back to the patient

The paragraph continues:

> **“He will likely always remain a candidate for DBS and we can look into this at any time if he wishes to proceed with that.”**

CLIN-002360.

So the patient-state architecture of the paragraph is:

> **you already know what DBS means;**
>
> **it only concerns tremor;**
>
> **you will probably remain a candidate;**
>
> **we can pursue it whenever you wish.**

Within the same record, prominent tremor is repeatedly absent or minimal.

Thus an alternative management route is simultaneously narrowed by the meaning given to it and left nominally available as something awaiting the patient's wish.

This is a particularly clear example of how information and preference can combine to make non-progression look patient-owned.

### 11. Patient preference repeatedly becomes part of the reason for deferral

The same longitudinal record contains multiple decisions in which the patient's stated preference or confidence is incorporated into why a treatment route is not immediately taken.

Examples include:

> **“he is adamantly clear that the foot pain is not a problem currently”** — CLIN-002490;

> **“He isn't confident that the extra Sinemet in the evenings would help”** — CLIN-002492;

and:

> **“he would prefer to wait and see until after the DaTscan too”** — CLIN-002493.

There is nothing inherently improper about respecting patient preference.

The documentary significance is that the record frequently represents management decisions through a patient-state proposition:

> **the patient does not want / is not confident / prefers to wait / wishes to proceed.**

The resulting plan can therefore appear not merely clinician-selected but jointly or patient-selected.

### 12. The letters explicitly aim to create shared understanding

A later extended consultation contains an unusually revealing explanation of why the letters are written in real time:

> **“so that we have a shared understanding of what I mean.”**

CLIN-002035.

The 2025 rebuttal expands the same documentary philosophy. As preserved elsewhere in this booklet, Monaghan says that the long letters exist so that clinician and patient can have a **shared understanding**, so the patient can understand both **facts and nuances**, and to bridge the difference between what is said/intended and what is heard/understood.

This makes the patient's knowledge-state an explicit objective of the documentation.

The letters are therefore simultaneously:

> **clinical records;**
>
> **communication instruments;**
>
> **interpretive guides for the patient;**
>
> **and later evidence of what was supposedly understood.**

That combination is legitimate in principle but evidentially powerful.

The same document that helps create the patient's understanding can later be cited as proof of the understanding it helped create.

### 13. By 2025 treatment response is itself converted into a shared knowledge-state

The March 2025 clinic note says:

> **“He knows very well that once the Sinemet hits his system that the feet feel better from a pain perspective.”**

CLIN-002787.

Six days later the complaint rebuttal broadens the proposition:

> **“As the letters show, we have had a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.”**

CLIN-003247.

The clinical observation is no longer merely:

> **Sinemet appears to help the feet.**

It becomes:

> **he knows it; we have understood it together; and the letters show that this shared understanding has existed for some time.**

This is a mature form of the same documentary mechanism.

A treatment-state fact is integrated into a historical account through a patient/clinician knowledge-state rather than necessarily through reconstruction of the original structural/dynamic relationship.

### 14. The 2025 rebuttal reuses the 2017 patient knowledge-state as evidence

The rebuttal deliberately pulls the 2017 sentence forward:

> **“[Patient] has a good understanding of why we wished to delay the commencement of levodopa or dopamine agonist type treatments at least for now.”**

CLIN-003261 quoting the founding record.

It then uses the historic treatment strategy to explain the later course.

That is one of the strongest reasons this profile matters.

A patient-state proposition recorded in 2017 does not remain a local piece of counselling history.

Eight years later it becomes **retrospective evidential support for the reasonableness and continuity of the management architecture.**

The patient's documented mind becomes part of the defence of the record.

### 15. The negative control is the Protest

The pattern would be much less significant if the record preserved patient states indiscriminately.

It does not appear to.

Immediately before discharge he held and directly communicated a contrary state:

> **the feet remained part of the unfinished clinical problem and the admission should not simply close without returning to them.**

That state is analytically different from every category above.

It is not:

> understanding;
>
> risk-awareness;
>
> preference within the offered plan;
>
> willingness;
>
> prior knowledge;
>
> or satisfaction with the process.

It is a challenge to the adequacy of the governing plan itself.

Yet the surviving synthesis preserves:

> the patient's intelligence;
>
> his appropriate questioning;
>
> his good understanding of treatment restraint;
>
> the productive character of the prolonged admission;
>
> firmer footing;
>
> and a clear plan,

while the reported question that directly contests the sufficiency of that plan does not travel.

This is the crucial negative control.

### 16. The asymmetry is therefore directional, not merely incomplete

The patient-origin information that supports separation is preserved and strengthened:

> **“not the same problem” → “markedly divorced.”**

Patient knowledge supporting treatment restraint is expressly certified:

> **“good understanding.”**

Risk knowledge is repeatedly attributed:

> **“well aware”; “always been aware”; “he knows.”**

A patient-raised alternative is recorded as already known:

> **“as I have reminded him.”**

Deferrals are partly attached to patient preference:

> **“he isn't confident”; “he would prefer to wait.”**

Later treatment response becomes a historical shared understanding:

> **“we have had a clear understanding for quite some time.”**

The most important patient-origin state challenging the management architecture is absent from the founding synthesis.

That is why **selective construction** is more precise than merely saying the record contains many patient-state statements.

The selection has a detectable documentary direction.

### 17. This does not require an allegation that every statement is false

The strongest version of this profile does not say:

> every counselling statement was invented;
>
> every patient preference was misrepresented;
>
> every “he knows” statement was false;
>
> or the clinician consciously manipulated the patient's mind.

Those propositions are not established by the available evidence.

The more powerful claim is documentary and observable:

> **patient knowledge, understanding, preference and prior awareness repeatedly perform stabilising work for the management account.**

Some statements may be exact.

Some may be ordinary shorthand.

Some may be stronger than the recoverable underlying evidence.

The proper audit is therefore phrase-by-phrase.

For each statement ask:

1. What is the primary patient-origin evidence?
2. What additional meaning does the authored phrase add?
3. Does it construct separation, assent, prior knowledge, preference, continuity or shared understanding?
4. Is the proposition independently corroborated?
5. What contrary patient-state evidence existed at the time?
6. Did that contrary state enter the portable record?

### 18. Two clean specimens establish the mechanism

At minimum, two examples are unusually clear.

#### Specimen A — separation

Primary patient state:

> **foot pain improved with reduced standing while leg tension persisted; they were “not the same problem.”**

Authored state:

> **he had “in his own mind - markedly divorced” them.**

Added meaning:

> **strong conceptual separation supportive of the clinical partition.**

#### Specimen B — prior knowledge

Primary recoverable event:

> **the patient raises DBS and is given a categorical explanation in the 2021 consultation.**

Authored state:

> **“As I have reminded him…”**

Added meaning:

> **the proposition is represented as prior patient knowledge, despite no recovered earlier DBS provenance in the searched corpus.**

These two examples do not prove that every patient-state phrase is inflated.

They prove that **the mechanism exists and warrants systematic audit.**

### 19. The controlling proposition

> **Across the longitudinal record, the patient's state of mind repeatedly functions as a documentary control surface for management. Genuine patient observations are interpreted into stronger states of separation; treatment restraint is stabilised by recorded understanding; risk is converted into shared awareness; counselling is certified as complete; alternatives are represented as already known; deferral is partly attached to patient confidence or preference; and later treatment response is converted into a longstanding shared understanding. The record itself later explains that its letters are designed to produce shared understanding and bridge what is said or intended with what is heard or understood. None of this is inherently improper. The evidential problem arises when authored patient states are stronger than their recoverable basis, when they consistently support the management architecture, and when the most important contrary patient state — the reported pre-discharge Protest challenging that architecture — is absent from the portable synthesis.**

The irreducible version is:

> **The clinical subject of the record is the patient's body, but a recurrent documentary control surface is the patient's mind: what he understands, knows, fears, accepts, prefers, has supposedly already been told, or wishes to do. Those states repeatedly make the management plan intelligible and continuous. The critical exception is the state of mind that directly challenged the completeness of the plan before discharge.**

And the shortest formulation is:

> **The record repeatedly documents a patient who understands the plan. The missing patient-state is the one in which the patient says the plan is not complete.**

# Risk Rank 06 — Original Profile 19

**Severity:** CRITICAL — LOAD-BEARING  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Later documentary continuity is used as evidence of the adequacy of the process that produced it, creating the core self-validation problem.

## Profile 19 — Self-Validating Record

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-142952).md; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 8 of 12 — The Self-Validating Record -->

### When the Document Becomes the Evidence for Its Own Adequacy

The discharge summary began as a clinical document.

Over time, it became something more powerful.

It became:

- the account of what happened;
- the explanation of why decisions were made;
- the source later clinicians inherited;
- the historical framework through which later evidence was interpreted;
- and, by 2025, the document its own author relied upon to show that his clinical reasoning had been sound.

That transformation is central to the case.

The problem is not merely that the record was influential.

Clinical records are supposed to be influential.

The problem is that **a selective representation of the encounter eventually became the principal evidence used to assess the adequacy of the encounter it selectively represented.**

### 1. The record begins as a selection from a larger reality

The bedside encounter is larger than the final document.

By the time the patient leaves hospital, Monaghan knows at least two different things about the feet.

He knows the clinical proposition:

> **their relationship to the neurological syndrome remains “Not clear.”**

And he knows the relational proposition:

> **the patient has directly challenged him that the feet have not been adequately dealt with.**

Both are facts of the encounter.

The final synthesis carries the first.

It does not carry the second.

From the beginning, therefore, the document is not identical to the event.

It is a **selection from the event**.

### 2. Selection is not automatically a problem

No clinical record contains everything.

A discharge summary necessarily compresses.

The important question is whether the omitted information is material to the meaning of what is retained.

Here, the omitted Protest bears directly upon:

> **“Not clear.”**

It tells the reader something about the status of that uncertainty:

> it had been challenged before closure as potentially reflecting incomplete investigation rather than merely unavoidable diagnostic ambiguity.

So the omission does not concern peripheral detail.

It concerns the interpretation of one of the central surviving propositions.

### 3. The document then makes the selected disposition portable

Once finalised, the synthesis travels downstream.

The GP receives:

- Parkinsonism;
- the treatment strategy;
- Podiatry;
- “Not clear”;
- patient understanding;
- a clear plan.

Future clinicians inherit that representation.

The Protest does not travel with it.

That changes what later readers can know.

They can know:

> **what Neurology concluded.**

They cannot know from the document:

> **that the patient directly challenged the adequacy of that conclusion before it became final.**

Thus the record does more than preserve information.

It determines which parts of the encounter become **portable truth**.

### 4. Portability creates authority

Once a signed consultant discharge summary exists, it naturally acquires authority.

A GP is entitled to rely upon it.

Later specialists are entitled to treat it as a serious historical source.

The patient himself is likely to understand it as the official account.

The longer it remains uncorrected, the more authoritative it becomes.

This creates an asymmetry.

The written account is durable.

The unrecorded Protest depends on human memory.

Over time:

> **the document becomes stronger because it persists;**

while:

> **the omitted event becomes weaker because it is absent from the durable system.**

That is how documentary selection becomes longitudinally consequential.

### 5. Later evidence is then interpreted inside the document's categories

The 2017 record establishes a usable structure:

> Parkinsonism;

> cautious neurological treatment;

> structural feet;

> possible neuropathic component;

> Podiatry;

> relationship “Not clear.”

Later information arrives.

Procyclidine changes the foot state.

The left fifth MTPJ repeatedly recurs.

Foot cramping appears with tone.

The GP reconnects feet and Parkinsonian gait.

Movement Disorders identifies OFF-related pain.

Sinemet helps the feet.

But none of those facts arrives into an empty conceptual field.

They arrive into a pre-existing historical architecture.

Because the architecture is flexible enough, each new fact can be added without requiring the founding record itself to be rewritten.

The document begins to **organise the meaning of later evidence**.

### 6. This creates path dependence

A later clinician rarely starts the case again from first principles.

Why would they?

There is already a detailed university Neurology account.

It contains extensive reasoning.

It appears thoughtful.

It contains uncertainty.

It discusses alternatives.

It explains treatment policy.

It portrays a highly intelligent patient who understands that policy.

It contains a clear forward plan.

So the rational starting assumption for later care is:

> **the foundational reasoning was substantially sound; we are now managing developments within it.**

That is normal clinical behaviour.

But if the foundational representation omitted a material challenge to its own completeness, normal reliance can preserve the omission.

The stronger the record, the less likely anyone is to reopen what it already appears to have considered.

### 7. The document is especially persuasive because it contains humility

This matters.

The discharge synthesis does not read like dogmatic certainty.

It says:

> PRKN is possible.

> genetics may remain uncertain.

> Sinemet may work very well.

> delaying it may later be regretted.

> the feet are “Not clear.”

That intellectual modesty makes the document highly credible.

A later reader sees a neurologist who appears willing to acknowledge uncertainty.

And much of that humility is genuine.

But its effect is also structural:

> **if the document already looks self-critical, there is less reason for the reader to suspect that a major challenge to its adequacy has been left outside it.**

The document's apparent openness increases trust in its completeness.

### 8. The patient's intelligence becomes part of the validation structure

The record says the patient is highly intelligent.

He has exceptional insight.

He asks appropriate questions.

He understands the rationale for delaying dopaminergic treatment.

Those propositions strengthen the apparent reliability of the management process.

They imply:

> **this was not simply a plan imposed on a confused patient; it was carefully discussed with someone capable of understanding it.**

But the record omits the fact that this same intelligent, appropriately questioning patient directly challenged the foot disposition.

So the document preserves the patient's intelligence **as validation of the process** while omitting the moment when that intelligence questioned the process.

That is a powerful selective effect.

### 9. “Clear plan” converts explanation into closure

The document does not simply record uncertainty.

It says the lengthy admission helped provide:

> **“firmer footing”**

and a:

> **“clear plan.”**

Then:

> **“As per above”**

incorporates the preceding narrative into the formal recommendations.

This gives the document a particularly strong future role.

The narrative is no longer just history.

It becomes operational guidance.

Thus the selected representation becomes the management state.

### 10. The record then survives because later events can be made consistent with it

A fragile historical model would eventually break when contradictory evidence appears.

This one is unusually robust.

If Sinemet helps the feet:

> that can be pain modulation.

If pregabalin helps:

> that can support a peripheral/neuropathic component.

If the feet worsen OFF:

> that can be Parkinsonian amplification.

If Podiatry repeatedly treats the fifth MTPJ:

> structural pathology remains real.

If the relationship is still uncertain:

> “Not clear” remains honest.

Almost every later observation can be true without forcing the original architecture to fail.

That is why the record survives.

Its propositions are not easily falsifiable because they are arranged to coexist.

### 11. The author then returns to the record in 2025

This is the point at which the record becomes explicitly self-validating.

When the patient complains, Monaghan does not say:

> **the old summary is only one partial historical source.**

Instead he effectively says:

> **my reasoning is visible in the record.**

He points the reviewer back to the 2017 letters.

He treats them as the documentary account of:

- what was considered;
- why decisions were made;
- what was understood;
- and how the patient was managed.

So the sequence becomes:

> **Monaghan creates the record.**

> **The record becomes the authoritative history.**

> **Later care proceeds inside that history.**

> **The patient challenges the resulting history.**

> **Monaghan cites the original record as evidence that the history was reasonable.**

That is the closed loop.

### 12. The omitted Protest cannot challenge the record from inside the record

This is the structural problem.

The Protest is exactly the kind of fact that could tell a reviewer:

> **do not treat the discharge summary as the complete account of the adequacy of the 2017 process.**

But because the Protest is absent, the document cannot issue that warning about itself.

A reviewer who starts with the record therefore encounters:

> thoughtful clinical reasoning;

> acknowledged uncertainty;

> extensive investigation;

> patient intelligence;

> patient understanding;

> clear plan.

The missing fact that would qualify those impressions is outside the evidential object being relied upon.

That is why an omitted fact can become progressively harder to recover.

### 13. Memory makes the loop stronger

By 2025 Monaghan says, appropriately:

> **“I cannot claim to remember everything.”**

That is unsurprising after eight years.

But once personal memory fades, the record becomes even more important.

The clinician begins to remember the case **through the record**.

That creates a powerful possibility:

> **the author's later understanding of what happened can become increasingly aligned with the representation he originally wrote.**

This does not require dishonesty.

It is a normal consequence of documentary memory.

And it means omission can become self-reinforcing.

### 14. The record can therefore become more authoritative than the encounter that produced it

This is the deepest phenomenon.

In 2017:

> the encounter produces the record.

By 2025:

> the record is being used to reconstruct the encounter.

The direction of authority has reversed.

The representation now tells us what the reality was.

But that inversion is unsafe if we already know the representation omitted a material event.

The correct relationship must remain:

> **encounter → record**

not:

> **record → ultimate reality of encounter.**

The Protest proves why.

### 15. Institutional adoption magnifies the effect

Once the complaint process accepts Monaghan's explanation and relies upon the clinical record, the self-validation ceases to be purely personal.

The clinician's authored account begins functioning as the institution's evidential substrate.

So a document written by the clinician whose reasoning is being challenged becomes part of the institutional basis for deciding whether that reasoning was adequate.

That is not automatically improper.

Clinicians must explain themselves.

Their records are obviously relevant.

The problem arises if those records are treated as **self-sufficient proof of the completeness of the process** without independently testing known omissions, provenance and contrary evidence.

### 16. This is why provenance matters

A self-validating record can be tested only by stepping outside it.

Questions become:

- When was each section written?
- What existed before discharge?
- What was added after discharge?
- When did “clear plan” appear?
- When did “firmer footing” appear?
- When did patient-understanding language appear?
- Were drafts retained?
- What source entries fed the final narrative?
- What information was available to the author after the Protest?
- Why was later DaT information incorporated while the Protest was not?

These questions do not assume misconduct.

They test whether the final artefact can safely carry the evidential weight later placed upon it.

### 17. The record is strongest exactly where it should be treated most cautiously

The 2017 letter is detailed.

That is normally a strength.

It is reflective.

That is normally a strength.

It acknowledges uncertainty.

That is normally a strength.

It explains the treatment rationale.

That is normally a strength.

It was authored close to the event.

That is normally a strength.

But those strengths become dangerous if they encourage the inference:

> **therefore anything clinically important that happened must be represented somewhere inside it.**

The Protest proves that inference false.

The document can be excellent in many respects and still incomplete in one highly material respect.

### 18. The correct evidential status of the record

The discharge summary should therefore be treated as very strong evidence of:

> **what Monaghan chose to record and transmit as the settled account of the admission.**

It is strong evidence of:

- his documented reasoning;
- the management strategy;
- his stated uncertainty;
- his selected representation of the patient's understanding;
- the intended forward plan.

But it is not self-proving evidence of:

> **everything Monaghan knew;**

> **everything material that happened;**

> **the completeness of the investigation;**

> **the absence of disagreement;**

> **the adequacy of the foot disposition.**

Those propositions require independent testing.

### 19. The complaint rebuttal therefore changes the meaning of the whole archive

Before 2025, one might simply say:

> **the discharge summary was influential.**

After the rebuttal, we can say something much stronger:

> **its author affirmatively adopted it as the historical account through which his clinical reasoning should be judged.**

That makes every omission more consequential.

Because the author is no longer merely standing over the medical decisions.

He is standing over the **documentary representation of the decisions**.

### 20. The paradox

The paradox can be stated simply:

> **The more the clinician relies upon the discharge summary to prove that the process was sound, the more important it becomes to establish whether the discharge summary was a complete and fair representation of the process.**

And we already know at least one material respect in which it was not complete:

> **the Protest is absent.**

That does not prove the rest is unreliable.

It proves the document cannot validate itself.

### Profile of the Self-Validating Record

> **The July 2017 discharge synthesis began as a selective clinical representation of a larger bedside encounter. It preserved extensive diagnostic reasoning, “Not clear,” Podiatry, the cautious dopaminergic strategy, the patient's intelligence and understanding, “firmer footing,” a “clear plan” and “As per above,” but did not preserve the patient's direct discharge-day challenge that the presenting feet had not been adequately dealt with. Once signed and transmitted, that selected representation became authoritative: primary care and later clinicians inherited it, subsequent facts were interpreted within its categories, and the unrecorded Protest became progressively less visible as memory faded and documentary authority grew. In 2025 the process completed a circle when Monaghan expressly pointed the reviewer back to the 2017 letters as the place where his clinical reasoning could be seen and understood. The record had therefore become not only the product of the original decision-making process but part of the evidence offered to validate that process. Because a material contest to the adequacy of the disposition is absent from the very document later used to establish its adequacy, the record cannot safely be treated as self-proving. It is strong evidence of what was ultimately recorded and transmitted; it is not ultimate reality of everything the author knew or everything material that occurred.**

The irreducible version:

> **The encounter created the record.**
> **The record replaced the encounter.**
> **Then the record was used to prove that the encounter had been handled properly.**

And the evidential warning:

> **A document cannot prove its own completeness when a material event known to its author is already known to exist outside it.**
