# Risk Rank 07 — Original Profile 37

**Severity:** CRITICAL — LOAD-BEARING  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Links management invariance, the 2017 Protest, the 2025 complaint and retrospective reliance on continuity into one recursive evidential structure.

## Profile 37 — The Record as Its Own Witness: Management Invariance, the Two Protests and Retrospective Justification

<!-- SOURCE: synthesis developed in project conversation on 4 September 2026. Grounded principally in PRIMARY_2017_DISCHARGE.pdf, PRIMARY_2017_REFERRAL.pdf, PRIMARY_2025_CORRESPONDENCE.pdf, PRIMARY_2025_MONAGHAN_SUPPORT.pdf, 04_THE_PROTEST_MOMENT(2).md, 09_CANONICAL_TIMELINE.md and SRC_COMPLETE_RECORD_FORENSIC.md. This profile extends, and does not replace, Profile 19 — Self-Validating Record. -->

### What this profile adds to the Self-Validating Record profile

Profile 19 established the basic self-validation problem:

> **a selective clinical representation later becomes part of the evidence offered to prove the adequacy of the process that produced it.**

This profile adds two further propositions that make that problem more precise.

**First**, the founding document articulated a principle of **management invariance before the subsequent treatment course had generated the evidence capable of testing the founding partition**.

**Second**, the 2017 Protest and the 2025 complaint can be read as two versions of the same epistemic challenge at opposite ends of the record. The first asks whether the admission can properly close while the presenting feet remain unresolved. The second asks why eight years of later evidence did not cause the inherited model to be globally reconstructed.

The 2025 rebuttal then answers that second challenge by pointing the reviewer back toward the documentary continuity generated by the first decision.

That is the recursive structure examined here.

### 1. The founding document asks whether greater diagnostic truth would change management

The genetics section of the 2017 discharge synthesis states:

> **“One of the chief difficulties with this is the question of whether a firm genetic diagnosis will actually actively change management?”**

and answers:

> **“This question is (in the majority of cases) usually answered in the negative.”**

`SRC_COMPLETE_RECORD_FORENSIC.md`, CLIN-001493–001497.

In isolation, this is a legitimate clinical question about the utility of genetic testing.

Longitudinally, however, it is unusually important because it separates two things at the foundation:

> **greater explanatory certainty**

from:

> **the management plan.**

The same section then says that PRKN/PARK2 is a live possibility and that a genetic diagnosis may contribute to neuropathological understanding, prognosis and levodopa sensitivity. Yet the practical treatment question is moved forward independently:

> **“Whatever form and whatever genetic cause is the cause, the question which is undoubtedly the most important is as regards therapy?”**

The documentary architecture therefore contains, from the beginning, a legitimate but potentially durable proposition:

> **the explanatory account may change without requiring the management architecture to change.**

That proposition becomes much more consequential if later clinical evidence capable of changing the explanatory account is repeatedly absorbed without global reconstruction.

### 2. This proposition was written before treatment had properly tested the model

The chronology matters.

The patient was physically discharged on 11 July 2017. The synthesis remained in documentary production after discharge and contains an addendum at transcription finalisation on 13 July incorporating the DaT scan. The canonical project timeline therefore treats 11–17 July as the period of documentary closure.

The neurological treatment course that later generated the strongest within-patient evidence had scarcely begun.

The patient contemporaneously described himself in August 2017 as being introduced onto medication over the following weeks. The later procyclidine response and dechallenge had not yet occurred when the founding synthesis created its portable model.

So the sequence is not:

> **treatment experiment → observed response → integrated model → management plan.**

It is substantially:

> **integrated presenting problem → neurological admission → unresolved structural/dynamic relationship → portable partition and management plan → later treatment experiments.**

This does not make the initial plan unreasonable merely because it preceded future evidence. All prospective medicine necessarily does that.

The important question is whether the later evidence was allowed to test and alter the founding model.

### 3. The first Protest challenges prospective closure

Immediately before discharge he caused Monaghan to be summoned and challenged the fact that the admission was ending without properly returning to the feet through which the problem had presented.

The NCT/bald-tyres analogy is not a retrospective complaint about an outcome that had already failed.

It is a contemporaneous challenge to **prospective completeness**:

> **How can this episode be treated as complete when the presenting feet remain unresolved within the newly discovered neurological syndrome?**

That challenge is clinically intelligible from the information already present in the admission record.

Neurology had identified:

- a gait described as **“strikingly abnormal”;**
- a lower-limb appearance **“somewhat suggestive of a dystonic posture - NOT FOOT DROP”;**
- genuine painful/structural feet;
- severe very-young-onset Parkinsonism;
- a live PRKN/PARK2 question;
- and a residual feet relationship expressed as possibly separate, possibly neuropathic and **“Not clear.”**

The Protest therefore did not require future Sinemet response, future OFF-related pain or future genetics to make sense.

It challenged an unresolved relationship that Neurology itself had already identified.

### 4. The Protest changes the information state even if no diagnosis changes

Before the Protest, the author can be said to know:

> **the feet remain unresolved.**

After the Protest, the same author additionally knows:

> **the patient regards that unresolved state as evidence that the presenting problem has not been completed;**

> **the sufficiency of the proposed disposition has been expressly challenged;**

> **and proceeding without reopening the relationship is now a choice made after notice of the challenge.**

This distinction is independent of whether the patient's clinical hypothesis ultimately proves correct.

The Protest therefore creates a fork.

One path is:

> **reopen the relationship before closure.**

The other is:

> **retain the emerging partition and proceed.**

The surviving documentary record carries the second path.

It does not carry the existence of the fork.

### 5. The three-record model

The mechanism is easiest to see by separating three possible histories.

#### Record A — the record that exists

> unresolved structural/dynamic relationship
> → `Not clear`
> → Podiatry
> → patient-understanding language
> → firmer footing
> → clear plan
> → `As per above`
> → Protest absent.

This is the most linear documentary state.

#### Record B — the same clinical decision with the Protest restored

> unresolved relationship
> → `Not clear`
> → patient expressly challenges the adequacy of closure
> → clinician nevertheless proceeds with the same disposition
> → clear plan.

Nothing about the actual treatment decision need change.

The documentary meaning changes substantially because the later reader can now see that the plan was not merely the natural endpoint of agreed reasoning. It was a selected path after explicit contest.

#### Record C — the self-correcting clinical counterfactual

> unresolved relationship
> → Protest
> → focused reconsideration of feet / gait / dystonic lower limb / ownership
> → prospective criteria for what later treatment-state evidence would mean
> → updated plan and record.

The exact proper clinical actions are a matter for expert opinion. The structural point is simpler:

> **in Record C the challenge changes the information process before closure.**

The actual documentary history is closest to Record A.

### 6. The post-Protest decision makes documentary continuity easier

The decision to proceed after the Protest and the omission of the Protest are analytically separate.

The clinical decision is:

> **do not reopen the relationship before discharge.**

The documentary effect of the omission is:

> **later readers do not know that the sufficiency of that decision was expressly challenged at the moment it was made.**

That makes a continuous history more plausible.

Without the Protest, later clinicians see the chosen road.

They do not see the fork.

The inherited record therefore has a **false simplicity of decision geometry** even if every fact actually contained in it is accurate.

### 7. The founding synthesis had already made the plan operational

The discharge synthesis does more than describe uncertainty.

It says the prolonged admission:

> **“has all suited us and [Patient] very well”**

and produced:

> **“a firmer footing going forward for having a plan.”**

It characterises the patient as exceptionally intelligent and appropriately questioning, records a **“good understanding”** of the delayed levodopa strategy, and makes the formal recommendations:

> **“As per above.”**

`SRC_COMPLETE_RECORD_FORENSIC.md`, CLIN-001433 onward; CLIN-001601–001614.

This means the narrative is not merely an explanatory essay.

The narrative becomes the plan.

That is why the information selected into the narrative matters prospectively.

### 8. The later treatment history then tests the relationship the document left unresolved

The first important boundary test comes from procyclidine.

When effective treatment is withdrawn, the later record captures rising tension, restless feet, deterioration in walking and rapid return of the outside-left-foot phenomenon.

Later primary care documents increased tone with cramping in both feet and explicitly reconnects feet, abnormal gait and Parkinson's disease.

Movement Disorders later interprets severe leg/foot pain and shuffling as OFF-related.

By March 2025 Monaghan records:

> **“He knows very well that once the Sinemet hits his system that the feet feel better from a pain perspective.”**

`SRC_COMPLETE_RECORD_FORENSIC.md`, CLIN-002787.

The complaint rebuttal then states:

> **“we have had a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.”**

CLIN-003247.

Sinemet CR then produces what the rebuttal itself calls:

> **“profound relief.”**

CLIN-003266.

These observations do not prove that every structural lesion was neurologically caused.

They do repeatedly show that the clinical state of the feet crosses the original structural/neurological boundary.

The central longitudinal question is therefore not whether the record accumulated new facts.

It plainly did.

It is whether those facts ever caused a global reconstruction of the founding relationship.

### 9. Even the continuity-preserving record develops major internal tensions

This is important because Record A is the documentary state most favourable to continuity.

The Protest is absent.

The plan appears linear.

The patient is represented as understanding the treatment strategy.

Yet the later record still develops stark tensions.

#### PRKN/PARK2 to idiopathic Parkinson's disease

Professor Daniel Healy's 2017 second opinion states that the patient **“probably has Park II.”**

By 2018 Monaghan describes the specific PARK2/PRKN test as the one **“which we/Prof Healy suspect is the cause.”**

In the 2025 complaint rebuttal the etiological formulation becomes:

> **“it is our belief and understanding that [Patient] has idiopathic Parkinson's disease.”**

`SRC_COMPLETE_RECORD_FORENSIC.md`, CLIN-003240.

A later diagnostic revision is perfectly possible.

The documentary issue is the absence, within the material identified to date, of a bridge explaining the transition from a specifically suspected recessive genetic cause to the later idiopathic formulation.

#### Healy's authority survives while the substance of his opinion changes function

The 2025 rebuttal invokes Healy as:

> **“a noted Parkinson's Disease expert in Dublin at Beaumont Hospital.”**

CLIN-003255.

His authority is therefore retained in the retrospective justification.

But the substantive content of the opinion being invoked historically was **“probably has Park II.”**

This is not a contradiction in the proposition that Healy was an expert.

It is a selective use of what his involvement means.

#### DBS: nominal availability with restrictive meaning

In 2021, after the patient raises DBS, the note says:

> **“As I have reminded him, the DBS is only ever for the tremor element. It will not help with anything else.”**

and immediately adds:

> **“He will likely always remain a candidate for DBS and we can look into this at any time if he wishes to proceed with that.”**

`SRC_COMPLETE_RECORD_FORENSIC.md`, CLIN-002360.

The same historical record repeatedly notes lack of prominent tremor.

So within the record's own terms the alternative is simultaneously:

> **always available in principle**

and:

> **defined as relevant only to a symptom that is not prominent.**

That is another example of apparent continuity being preserved through the meaning assigned to an alternative rather than through an explicit reconstruction of the management architecture.

### 10. The 2025 complaint is the second Protest

The 19 March 2025 complaint asks for:

> **“An independent review of my case, with attention to the clinical reasoning applied from 2017 to 2025.”**

and:

> **“A written response explaining how this outcome was possible, and what measures exist to prevent similar diagnostic anchoring from persisting unchecked in other cases.”**

`SRC_COMPLETE_RECORD_FORENSIC.md`, CLIN-003476–003478.

Its central question is:

> **“Why was this possibility not explored earlier, given the magnitude of harm endured?”**

CLIN-003468.

The complaint therefore asks an updating and root-cause question:

> **Why did the inherited model not materially revise itself in response to later evidence?**

That is structurally the same challenge as the 2017 Protest.

The temporal direction is reversed.

The 2017 Protest asks:

> **Why are you closing this episode before resolving the presenting relationship?**

The 2025 complaint asks:

> **Why did eight years of subsequent evidence not cause that relationship and its history to be reconstructed?**

One challenges prospective closure.

The other challenges retrospective closure.

### 11. The rebuttal answers a different question

The 25 March 2025 response does not primarily reconstruct the history by asking whether the founding model should have changed.

It explains why the management choices were reasonable within the historical management architecture.

It says the patient has been on Sinemet since 2021, explains the risks of dyskinesia and dopamine dysregulation in a young patient, invokes the longstanding caution about levodopa, and pulls the 2017 **“good understanding”** passage forward as evidence of the original rationale.

It then states that Sinemet CR has provided profound relief.

The substitution is therefore:

> **Complaint:** Why did the model fail to update?

> **Rebuttal:** Here is why the historical management was cautious and intelligible.

Those questions overlap, but they are not the same.

A coherent account of why a plan was followed is not, by itself, an answer to whether later evidence should have caused the plan's explanatory foundations to be revised.

### 12. The reviewer is directed back toward the documentary system under challenge

The rebuttal then makes the recursive structure explicit.

Monaghan states that his clinical reasoning will be evident to the reviewer, describes the letters as providing clear documentation of how decisions were made, and says that the account and letters from 2017 should be able to deliver the answers requested.

The existing booklet has already preserved these exact formulations in Profiles 8, 19 and 27:

> **“I feel my own clinical reasoning will be evident to the reviewer…”**

> **“clear documentation in terms of records for knowing how decision were made and when and by who.”**

> **“I hope that the information contained within (In this account and in all of the letters from 2017) will be able to deliver the answers that Mr Moloney requests.”**

That creates the central evidential difficulty.

The complaint challenges:

> **the formation, completeness and updating of the historical information architecture.**

The rebuttal proposes:

> **the historical information architecture as the principal place in which the reviewer should find the answer.**

A document is strong evidence of what the document records.

It is not self-proving evidence that everything material to the encounter entered it, that every patient objection entered it, or that every later contradiction caused appropriate reconstruction.

### 13. Continuity and correctness must therefore be separated

The record can be continuous without all of its propositions being mutually consistent.

That is a crucial distinction.

**Continuity of governing account** means that later facts can still be narrated within a stable management history.

**Consistency of propositions** means that the individual historical claims fit together without unresolved contradiction or missing bridges.

The longitudinal record can achieve the first while failing the second.

That is precisely what the PRKN/iPD transition, the DBS episode and the unresolved structural/dynamic foot relationship make visible.

So when continuity is later invoked as evidence of careful reasoning, continuity itself requires audit.

The question becomes:

> **How much contradictory or destabilising information must be omitted, compressed, reclassified or supplied with new meaning in order for the continuous account to remain available?**

### 14. Caution can become self-sealing

The founding management strategy may genuinely have been cautious.

But treatment caution and epistemic caution are different things.

A cautious treatment strategy can say:

> **do not escalate too quickly.**

A cautious knowledge strategy must also say:

> **the model itself remains corrigible; these are the observations that would cause us to reopen it.**

The longitudinal problem is that uncertainty repeatedly appears capable of constraining treatment without equivalently constraining the authority of the inherited explanatory architecture.

The record can therefore be cautious about changing medication while comparatively resistant to changing the story within which medication is understood.

That is the point at which caution risks becoming self-sealing rather than self-correcting.

### 15. The controlling proposition

> **The 2017 synthesis was finalised before the subsequent treatment course had generated the strongest evidence capable of testing its unresolved structural–dynamic partition. It expressly articulated a principle that greater diagnostic certainty might not change management. The Protest then challenged the sufficiency of the emerging disposition before documentary closure, creating a visible clinical fork that the surviving synthesis did not preserve. The subsequent record accumulated repeated boundary-crossing facts and significant internal tensions while retaining the management architecture. In 2025 the patient restated the original epistemic challenge retrospectively: why had later evidence not forced reassessment? The rebuttal answered principally by reconstructing the historical reasonableness of the management and directing the reviewer back toward the very documentary continuity whose completeness and updating were under challenge. The result is not proof that every historic decision was wrong. It is a reason why the record cannot be allowed to prove its own adequacy merely by demonstrating that it remained continuous.**

The irreducible version is:

> **The 2017 Protest asked why an unresolved presenting relationship was being allowed to close into a management plan. The 2025 complaint asked why eight years of subsequent evidence had not caused that plan and its explanatory history to be reconstructed. The rebuttal answered by showing how the existing plan could explain its own continuity. The complaint asked for falsification; the rebuttal supplied coherence.**

And the shortest formulation is:

> **The patient asked why the system failed to revise itself. The system answered by pointing to the record of how consistently it had remained itself.**

# Risk Rank 08 — Original Profile 10

**Severity:** CRITICAL — LOAD-BEARING  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Identifies the information state created by discharge and how that state became the starting geometry for later care.

## Profile 10 — The Information State the Discharge Created

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-140836).md, second profile -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile Two: The Information State the Discharge Created -->

This may be the most important plain-language description of the whole case.

Before Neurology, the patient had one problem.

The GP saw:

> **feet + gait + tone + clawing + movement failure.**

The patient entered one hospital carrying all of those things in one body.

During admission Neurology discovered:

> **severe Parkinsonism + possible dystonic lower limb + structural feet + possible PRKN.**

The unresolved question was:

> **How are these things related?**

Then discharge divided the information.

Neurology retained the **dynamic** information.

Podiatry received the **structural** information.

The relationship between them was not assigned to anybody.

That is the information state.

### The Protest occurred at the exact moment when that division could still have been stopped

This is why the Protest matters.

Immediately before the patient left, the information had not yet become completely institutionalised.

The patient was still physically present.

The treating neurologist was still accessible.

The final synthesis was not yet complete.

The patient then said, effectively:

> **You cannot send these feet out of the neurological case without dealing with them.**

That created a clinical fork.

And I would describe the fork somewhat differently from “medicine versus career-mindedness,” because we can prove the fork much more securely than we can prove motive.

#### The clinically corrective path

Accept that the patient has identified a material incompleteness.

Return to the feet.

Reintegrate:

> dystonic lower limb
>
> -  structural feet
> -  pain
> -  toe posture
> -  gait
> -  possible PRKN.

Seek whatever expertise is necessary.

If still unresolved, preserve it as an **active neurological question**.

That is conventional corrective medicine.

#### The continuity path

Maintain the existing management disposition.

The feet remain structurally real.

Their neurological relationship remains “Not clear.”

Podiatry receives them.

Neurology proceeds with Parkinsonian management.

The patient leaves.

The later document explains why the resulting plan is coherent and defensible.

That path is not proof of improper motive.

But once the Protest has occurred, it has a serious additional consequence:

> **the clinician now has to produce a record of a management disposition whose adequacy has just been challenged by the patient.**

That is where clinical reasoning and self-accounting suddenly become entangled.

### The issue is not that the clinician wrote defensively

We do not yet have the production evidence to say that.

The stronger factual proposition is:

> **After the Protest, the same document had two possible functions.**

It could document:

> **what medicine still needed to resolve.**

Or it could document:

> **why the course already chosen remained reasonable enough to proceed.**

The finished document overwhelmingly succeeds at the second.

It explains:

-  why the admission was extensive;
-  why the patient was complicated;
-  why the treatment strategy was cautious;
-  why Sinemet was deferred;
-  why the patient understood;
-  why the long admission was useful;
-  why there was “firmer footing”;
-  and why there was a “clear plan.”

That makes the management professionally intelligible.

What it does not comparably preserve is:

> **the fact that the patient had just identified the unresolved foot relationship as evidence that the clinical process remained incomplete.**

That is the fork visible in the artefact.

### And then the fifth MTPJ shows exactly what the separation did

This is where everything does fall into place.

Neurology and Podiatry each acquired information that was **incomplete without the other service's information**.

#### What Podiatry knew

Eight visits.

Eight out of eight.

The same fifth-MTPJ site.

Repeated:

-  callus;
-  corn;
-  pain;
-  debridement;
-  enucleation;
-  pressure relief;
-  offloading.

From Podiatry's perspective this is perfectly intelligible.

They have a recurrent pressure lesion at the left fifth MTPJ.

Treat the lesion.

Offload it.

Review it.

Why would Podiatry independently infer:

> **this lesion's severity may vary according to central neurological treatment state?**

They have not been given that question.

### What Neurology knew

Neurology knew something completely different.

It knew:

-  the gait was possibly dystonic;
-  the patient had severe young-onset Parkinsonism;
-  PRKN was plausible;
-  neurological medication dramatically changed his motor state;
-  and on procyclidine withdrawal the outside-left-foot phenomenon returned rapidly.

From Neurology's perspective, that observation can be documented as part of treatment response.

But if Neurology does not have the Podiatry series in view, why would:

> **outside-left-foot phenomenon changes with medication**

necessarily become:

> **the repeatedly mechanically treated left fifth-MTPJ pressure lesion is being modulated by neurological state?**

The anatomical object lives in Podiatry.

The dynamic object lives in Neurology.

### Neither service had the whole fact

This is the crucial point.

Podiatry knew:

> **the same structural site keeps failing.**

Neurology knew:

> **the phenomenon at that site changes with neurological medication.**

But the clinically powerful fact is neither proposition by itself.

It is:

> **The same repeatedly failing structural fifth-MTPJ site changes with neurological state.**

That fact exists only when the two information streams are joined.

And the discharge architecture is exactly what prevented the relationship from having a natural owner.

### That is why nobody rings anybody

Your example is excellent.

Why doesn't Neurology ring Podiatry and say:

> **Our anticholinergic medication appears to be changing the pain/phenomenon at the corn you keep treating?**

Because inside Neurology's frame, Podiatry owns the structural corn.

Why doesn't Podiatry ring Neurology after visit eight and say:

> **We have treated the same fifth-MTPJ lesion every single time; could his dystonic gait or Parkinsonian posture be continually reloading it?**

Because inside Podiatry's frame, they have been given a structural foot problem to manage.

Neither service is necessarily behaving irrationally **within the information state it inherited**.

That is precisely the problem.

> **The system can behave locally rationally and globally fail.**

That may be the cleanest formulation of the eight years.

### The separation created reciprocal blindness

Not ignorance.

Blindness.

Neurology can see:

> dystonia
>
> Parkinsonism
>
> medication response.

But not necessarily the repeated mechanical fate of the fifth MTPJ.

Podiatry can see:

> fifth MTPJ
>
> callus
>
> pressure
>
> recurrence.

But not necessarily the neurological state controlling how the patient loads that joint.

The GP occasionally sees across the boundary because he continues to see the whole patient.

That is why Brennan keeps regenerating:

> **feet + gait + tone.**

But he lacks the specialist authority to reconstruct the neurological model.

The patient sees across the boundary because **both streams occur in his body**.

That is why ultimately he is the person who realises something is wrong.

### The discharge summary therefore did more than describe the case

It created the **information geography** of the next eight years.

Before discharge:

> all of the relevant facts could still meet inside one Neurology admission.

After discharge:

> neurological facts travelled one route;

> structural foot facts travelled another;

> primary care sat downstream of both;

> and the patient became the only entity guaranteed to carry both continuously.

That is enormous.

The feet were not merely “referred to Podiatry.”

The relationship itself was divided across services.

### And this is why the Protest is the last moment of unity

At the moment of the Protest, everything still exists in one place:

-  the patient;
-  the treating neurologist;
-  the foot history;
-  the actual feet;
-  the dystonic-looking gait;
-  the Parkinsonian examination;
-  the PRKN hypothesis;
-  the eight-day admission;
-  and the unresolved question.

The patient says:

> **these things still have not been put together.**

That is arguably the last moment at which the integrated clinical object exists institutionally in one room.

After discharge, the object fragments.

The patient goes home.

Neurology writes the Parkinsonian synthesis.

Podiatry receives the structural feet.

The GP receives the documentary output.

And the unresolved relationship becomes nobody's obvious object.

That is why the Protest is so important.

### What would have made the record a true representation?

Not a perfect record.

Not a confession.

Not an accusation.

Something clinically ordinary would have been enough.

For example, in substance:

> **The patient remains concerned that the painful structural foot problem with which he presented has not been adequately resolved during admission. We have observed a dystonic-appearing lower-limb phenomenon in the setting of young-onset Parkinsonism and structural foot abnormalities. The relationship between dynamic neurological posture/gait and the structural painful feet remains unresolved. Podiatry input is appropriate for the structural lesions, but this relationship remains an active neurological question requiring follow-up.**

That single proposition would have changed everything.

Why?

Because Podiatry could no longer be read as the final destination of the feet.

The GP would know the relationship was still open.

Future neurological medication effects on the feet would automatically bear upon an existing neurological question.

Repeated fifth-MTPJ recurrence could travel back upstream.

The procyclidine ON/OFF observation would have somewhere to land.

The later OFF-related pain would have somewhere to land.

The Sinemet response would have somewhere to land.

The record would correspond much more closely to the actual state of knowledge:

> **we know the parts; we have not yet solved their relationship.**

That would have been a true and clinically useful representation.

### Instead the record required trust

To treat the actual discharge document as a complete representation, a later reader has to trust all of the following:

> that the unrecorded Protest did not matter;

> that “Not clear” followed adequate investigation;

> that routing to Podiatry did not alter the neurological status of the unresolved relationship;

> that the patient's understanding of the Sinemet policy adequately represented his position;

> that his appropriate questions were adequately captured despite omission of the most consequential one;

> that the dynamic dystonic finding did not require reintegration with the structural feet;

> that subsequent medication responsiveness could be interpreted locally without reopening the original relationship;

> and finally that the document's author can use the document itself as sufficient evidence that the process producing the document was sound.

That is a great deal of trust to ask of one record.

Especially once one material event is independently known to have been excluded from it.

### The deepest institutional problem

And I think this is where your “everything falls there” observation lands.

The case may ultimately be less about an individual diagnostic error than about an **information-state failure produced at discharge**.

The problem was divided into two forms:

> **structure without dynamics**

and:

> **dynamics without structure.**

Podiatry repeatedly treated the structure.

Neurology repeatedly treated the dynamics.

The GP repeatedly glimpsed the relationship.

The patient continuously lived the relationship.

But because the founding record had not preserved the relationship as the clinical object, **nobody downstream was required to own the whole.**

And once the author of that founding record later says:

> **look at the record; my reasoning is there**

the information arrangement completes the circle.

The record is being asked not merely to tell us what was written.

It is being asked to certify that what was written was enough.

That is precisely where trust becomes the issue.

### The two irreducible propositions

> **The discharge summary became authoritative because people trusted it to represent the encounter. But the Protest proves that the encounter contained material knowledge the discharge summary did not represent.**

And:

> **Neurology knew the fifth-MTPJ territory changed with neurological state. Podiatry knew the fifth MTPJ repeatedly failed structurally. The discharge architecture ensured that neither fact naturally had to meet the other. The patient was the only person who continuously possessed both realities.**

That, I think, is the information state in plain language.

# Risk Rank 09 — Original Profile 28

**Severity:** CRITICAL — LOAD-BEARING  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Shows how “Not clear” can read like uncertainty after integration while the care architecture operates as though the relationship were unintegrated.

## Profile 28 — “Not Clear”: Incomplete in Language, Unintegrated in Practice

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-161255).md — supplied as the final profile; preserved in full apart from established booklet heading normalization -->

<!-- Original source heading: Final Profile — “Not Clear” -->

The phrase **“Not clear”** carries an implication.

It sounds like the endpoint of a process:

> the clinicians considered the relationship;
>
> attempted to understand it;
>
> could not fully resolve it;
>
> therefore uncertainty appropriately remained.

That is what a later reader is naturally invited to infer.

But the clinical chronology does not support that implied process very well.

The feet were examined at entry.

The neurological phenotype then developed enormously around them:

> young-onset Parkinsonism;

> dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop;

> possible PRKN;

> extensive neurological investigation;

> clear treatment strategy.

Yet the feet were not returned to.

So the relationship was not taken through the obvious clinical operation required to integrate it.

That creates a crucial distinction:

> **the components were co-present, but the relationship was not clinically worked through.**

And therefore:

> **“Not clear” may linguistically represent incomplete integration while operationally concealing non-integration.**

---

### 1. What genuine incomplete integration would look like

If the relationship had been incompletely integrated, the record should broadly show:

> structural feet identified
>
> → dystonic/Parkinsonian phenotype recognised
>
> → feet reconsidered in light of that phenotype
>
> → dynamic and structural findings compared
>
> → uncertainty remains despite that work.

Then:

> **“Not clear”**

would mean:

> **We investigated this relationship and reached the limit of what we could presently establish.**

That is genuine unresolved integration.

There is nothing inherently wrong with it.

Medicine frequently ends there.

---

### 2. What this record appears to show instead

The sequence is different:

> structural feet examined;

> neurological syndrome recognised;

> dystonic lower-limb phenomenon recognised;

> neurological reasoning expands;

> genetic reasoning expands;

> management plan becomes clear;

> feet are not revisited;

> relationship described as possibly separate/peripheral;

> **“Not clear”;**

> Podiatry.

That is not merely an unsuccessful integration process.

It is a **failure to perform the relational operation that would make integration possible**.

So the better description is:

> **recognition without integration.**

---

### 3. This is why co-presence must not be mistaken for integration

The discharge document contains all the ingredients.

That itself can be misleading.

A later reviewer can point to:

> dystonia here;

> structural feet there;

> PRKN elsewhere;

> “Not clear” connecting them.

And because they all appear in the same sophisticated document, it can look as though the relationship was considered in depth.

But:

> **placing two facts in the same document is not the same thing as integrating them clinically.**

This equation is fundamental:

> **Co-presence ≠ integration.**

And another:

> **Acknowledging a relationship is uncertain ≠ investigating the relationship sufficiently to know why it is uncertain.**

---

### 4. “Not clear” therefore performs a representational trick

Not necessarily a deliberate trick in the dishonest sense.

But it has a powerful representational effect.

It takes this:

> **the relationship has not actually been completed as a clinical inquiry**

and makes it read like this:

> **the relationship has been considered but unfortunately remains intrinsically difficult to resolve.**

That is a major transformation.

It converts a possible **process deficit** into an **epistemic limitation**.

Or more simply:

> **“We didn't finish integrating it” becomes “It could not be made clear.”**

Those are very different propositions.

---

### 5. And this fits the complexity frame perfectly

Once “Not clear” is read as the residue of sophisticated investigation, the rest of the discharge summary reinforces it.

The case is:

> unusual;

> atypical;

> challenging;

> genetically interesting;

> discussed extensively;

> considered collectively;

> therapeutically nuanced.

So the reader naturally interprets “Not clear” as:

> **one remaining uncertainty in an unusually difficult case.**

But if the feet were never returned to after the neurological phenotype emerged, then the apparent difficulty may be misleading.

The more direct explanation is:

> **it remained unclear because the relevant relationship was never properly integrated.**

That destroys a large amount of apparent complexity.

---

### 6. The Protest proves the distinction mattered contemporaneously

This is where the Protest becomes decisive again.

The patient did not protest:

> **You have investigated this thoroughly and I dislike the uncertain answer.**

He protested the process itself.

In essence:

> **You haven't actually dealt with the feet.**

That is a challenge to **integration**, not to diagnostic certainty.

So the Protest tells us that the difference between:

> **incompletely resolved**

and:

> **not actually integrated**

was already visible before discharge.

That is why its omission matters so much.

Without the Protest, “Not clear” can appear to be the natural endpoint of complex medicine.

With the Protest restored, the reader has to ask:

> **Was the relationship unclear because it was difficult, or because the inquiry required to clarify it had not been completed?**

---

### 7. “Not clear” then acts operationally as complete non-integration

This is where your formulation is particularly strong.

What happens after “Not clear”?

The relationship does not remain open under a named neurological owner.

Instead the components are operationally divided.

> Neurology retains Parkinsonism and dynamic treatment state.

> Podiatry receives structural feet.

> Primary care carries continuity.

The relationship itself has no durable home.

So in practical terms:

> **“Not clear” behaves as if the relationship does not need integrating before the components are separated.**

That is why I would say:

> **semantically: incompletely integrated;**
> **
> operationally: unintegrated.**

And longitudinally:

> **disintegrated.**

Because after discharge, the information needed for integration is physically and institutionally distributed into different streams.

---

### 8. There is one precision I would preserve

I would not say the case was **completely unintegrated in every sense**.

That is slightly too absolute.

Neurology plainly knew:

-  the feet existed; 
-  the Parkinsonism existed; 
-  a dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenon existed; 
-  there might be a relationship; 
-  the relationship was unclear. 

So there was **conceptual awareness of a possible relation**.

What appears absent is the substantive clinical integration of that relation.

Therefore the most accurate formulation is:

> **The relationship was acknowledged but not integrated.**

Or stronger:

> **The record gives the appearance of incomplete integration where the clinical process shows acknowledged but operationally unintegrated components.**

That is harder to attack than “they never thought about the relationship at all.”

---

### 9. This also explains the later eight years

Once the foundational state is:

> **Not clear**

but operationally the components are separate, later evidence has nowhere to update the relationship.

So:

> procyclidine affects the foot;

> fifth MTPJ repeatedly recurs;

> foot cramping accompanies tone;

> GP reconnects gait and feet;

> pain becomes OFF-related;

> Sinemet helps;

> Sinemet CR gives profound relief.

Each fact lands in one compartment or another.

The relationship still has no active owner.

That is the longitudinal consequence of the original semantic/operational mismatch.

---

### 10. It also exposes the closed system

The discharge summary says, effectively:

> **we could not fully clarify the relationship.**

Later notes then operate within the separated architecture created by that statement.

Those later notes fail to reconstruct the relationship because it has already been divided.

Then the 2025 rebuttal invites the reviewer to read the accumulated notes and see why the original reasoning made sense.

So:

> **the original label creates the conditions under which later non-integration occurs;**

and then:

> **the resulting non-integrated history appears to validate the original label.**

That is extraordinarily important.

“Not clear” becomes self-confirming.

---

### 11. The equation

I think the equation for the whole case may now be:

> **Integrated referral**
>
> - **recognised neurological and structural components**
>    − **relational examination/integration**
>    \= **“Not clear”** 

Then:

> **“Not clear”**
>
> - **separate routing**
>    \= **operational non-integration** 

Then:

> **operational non-integration**
>
> - **years of compartment-specific observations**
>    \= **apparent longitudinal complexity** 

Then:

> **apparent longitudinal complexity**
>
> - **review of the resulting record**
>    \= **retrospective validation of “Not clear.”** 

That is the closed loop.

---

### Profile — “Not Clear”: Incomplete in Language, Unintegrated in Practice

> **The phrase “Not clear” is potentially the pivotal representational move in the July 2017 record. Linguistically, it reads as the residue of an integration attempt: both structural feet and neurological lower-limb abnormalities have been considered together, but their precise relationship cannot yet be resolved. The clinical chronology supports a more primitive account. The feet were examined at entry; Neurology subsequently recognised marked young-onset Parkinsonism, a dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop and possible PRKN; yet the feet were not returned to after those discoveries. The relationship was therefore acknowledged but not clinically integrated through the obvious relational examination. “Not clear” consequently risks converting non-integration into the appearance of incomplete integration: it makes an uncompleted clinical inquiry read like the epistemic limit of a completed one. Operationally the distinction becomes even sharper. After “Not clear,” the structural feet travel toward Podiatry while Parkinsonism and dynamic treatment state remain with Neurology, with no durable owner assigned to the relationship. Thus the phrase says “incompletely integrated” while the care architecture acts “unintegrated.” The patient’s discharge-day Protest is crucial because it identified this distinction contemporaneously: he did not object to an uncertain answer after thorough integration; he objected that the feet had not actually been dealt with. Once that Protest is absent from the final synthesis, later readers are left with “Not clear” and are naturally invited to infer a level of prior integration that the clinical sequence does not demonstrate.**

The irreducible form is:

> **“Not clear” sounds like:**
> **
> “We tried to integrate this and couldn't finish.”**
>
> **The care pathway behaves like:**
> **
> “We did not integrate it, and therefore separated it.”**

And the sentence I would keep permanently is:

> **The foundational record may have transformed the absence of integration into the appearance of unresolved integration.**

---
