---

# Risk Rank 10 — Original Profile 12

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** The body repeatedly tests the unresolved boundary through treatment response, withdrawal, OFF states, cramping and later dopaminergic pain relief.

## Profile 12 — Natural Experiments

<!-- SOURCE: supplied in project conversation on 1 September 2026; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 2 of 12 — The Natural Experiments -->

### The Body Repeatedly Tests the 2017 “Not Clear”

One of the strongest features of this case is that the relationship left unresolved in July 2017 did not remain merely theoretical.

Over the following eight years, **changes in treatment repeatedly altered the same lower-limb and foot territory**.

These were not controlled scientific experiments. Several drugs were sometimes present simultaneously, disease state changed over time, and later observations cannot simply be treated as pharmacological proof of one mechanism.

But they function as **natural within-patient experiments**:

> treatment changes;
> the bodily state changes;
> treatment is withdrawn, altered or extended;
> the bodily state changes again.

The important question is therefore not whether any single episode proved the entire diagnosis.

It is:

> **How many times did the patient's own treatment course generate information capable of testing the relationship that Neurology had left “Not clear”?**

The answer is: repeatedly.

### Experiment 1 — Procyclidine produces extraordinary restoration

The first experiment begins almost immediately after discharge.

Procyclidine is slowly introduced.

By September 2017 the GP records:

> **“Doing well on Procyclidine.”**

The patient's own contemporaneous description is far stronger.

He describes himself as:

> **“living in a miracle, literally.”**

He records concrete changes:

- his walk came back together;
- his writing improved;
- he could stand upright;
- his legs became loose and limber;
- he could stand on a moving bus;
- lift his bicycle;
- run;
- jump;
- hop;
- skip;
- and recover ordinary dexterity.

This is not a subtle subjective response.

The neurological phenotype was dramatically modifiable.

That immediately matters to the unresolved feet because the admission had already identified a potentially dystonic lower-limb phenomenon.

But the first experiment does not yet tell us specifically what happens to the focal painful foot site.

The next one does.

### Experiment 2 — Procyclidine is withdrawn

Because of visual concerns, the patient undertakes a planned procyclidine withdrawal.

Four days into the break, he writes contemporaneously:

> **“I am feeling the tension building up, my feet are restless and my walk is falling apart....but my eyesight is back to normal.”**

This is an unusually informative observation.

One variable moves in the desired direction:

> **eyesight improves off medication.**

At the same time:

> **tension returns;**

> **feet become restless;**

> **gait deteriorates.**

The effect therefore cannot easily be dismissed as the patient simply feeling globally worse because he disliked stopping treatment. He reports both benefit and deterioration in different domains during the same withdrawal.

The body has supplied the first important challenge to the discharge partition:

> **neurological treatment state and foot/gait state move together.**

### Experiment 3 — Neurology itself records the focal outside-left-foot response

By April 2018 the observation is no longer only patient-origin evidence.

Neurology records that stopping procyclidine caused rapid return of the **outside-left-foot phenomenon**, alongside the patient becoming more “shifty” and “unrelaxed” and experiencing broader worsening.

The same review records:

- fantastic relief from tension;
- good gait;
- good bed mobility;
- and the expectation that levodopa or another dopamine agonist could make the patient:

> **“very much better.”**

This is a major threshold.

The July 2017 problem had been:

> structural painful feet + dystonic-looking lower limb → relationship “Not clear.”

Now the treating neurologist possesses an additional fact:

> **the clinical state of the focal outside-left-foot territory changes rapidly when neurological medication changes.**

That does not prove the corn is neurological.

It does not prove PRKN.

It does not establish the entire pain mechanism.

But it changes the question.

The relevant proposition is no longer merely:

> **the patient has structural foot disease.**

It is:

> **something about the clinical expression of that structural foot territory is dynamically responsive to neurological treatment state.**

### Why this experiment is particularly powerful

We now know from the anatomical profile that the outside-left focal problem is the persistent left fifth-MTPJ lesion repeatedly treated by Podiatry.

So the withdrawal observation is not merely:

> **Parkinson medication changed “the feet.”**

It bears upon an anatomically stable focal object.

That gives the experiment a physical address:

> **left fifth MTPJ.**

The structural lesion remains real.

The question becomes why its painful expression changes as neurological state changes.

That is exactly the structural-dynamic relationship that the admission had left unresolved.

### Experiment 4 — Treatment is restored and control returns

The treatment timeline records subsequent resumption of procyclidine and good symptom control.

By November 2017 primary care again describes good control, with the aim of maximising function while minimising adverse effects. The later Neurology account likewise describes the patient doing very well on the regimen.

Taken cautiously, the sequence is therefore:

> **procyclidine ON → major motor improvement;**

> **procyclidine OFF → tension builds, feet become restless, gait falls apart, focal outside-left-foot phenomenon rapidly returns;**

> **treatment resumed → useful control returns.**

That is much more informative than a single report of medication benefit.

It contains both **withdrawal and restoration**.

### Experiment 5 — September 2018: the feet deteriorate as neurological control deteriorates

The next signal is not produced by formal withdrawal.

It emerges as the effective treatment reaches its practical ceiling.

Brennan records:

- return of increased tone;
- deterioration of arm and hand function;
- **cramping in both feet**;
- previous **“great improvement”**;
- and inability comfortably to push procyclidine further because of vision and concentration problems.

His warning is:

> **“we are losing ground.”**

Again the feet do not deteriorate in isolation.

They appear in the same changing state as neurological tone.

This strengthens the earlier observation:

> **worse neurological control and worse foot phenomena are occurring together.**

It still does not tell us that every component has one cause.

But it makes the proposition that the painful feet are entirely independent of the neurological state progressively harder to treat as the only relevant frame.

### Experiment 6 — The attempts to find another therapeutic route expose the treatment ceiling

Amantadine is tried.

It does not provide useful benefit and is associated with:

> **“quite sad thoughts.”**

Neupro/rotigotine is then escalated.

At some doses the patient becomes substantially more mobile.

But increasing treatment produces:

- artificial or amphetamine-like stimulation;
- compulsive/impulsive features;
- dread as the patch wears off;
- and definite choreiform dyskinesia.

Neurology calls the situation:

> **“an extremely, extremely difficult tight rope.”**

These experiments are less directly about the fifth MTPJ.

But they establish something crucial for the later foot problem:

> **the patient's neurological state is highly modifiable, but increasing effective treatment carries substantial costs.**

That is the environment in which any neurologically modifiable foot problem will eventually have to be managed.

### Experiment 7 — Levodopa confirms the predicted ceiling

Levodopa finally enters the record in 2021.

Within a relatively short period slight chorea is already recorded.

By 2022, a higher Stalevo dose had produced:

> **obvious chorea/dyskinesia.**

This is an important natural experiment in its own right.

The 2017 concern that dopaminergic therapy might create a narrow therapeutic window was not imaginary.

It materialised.

That actually strengthens the defensibility of the original Sinemet caution.

But it creates a new practical fact:

> **if foot pain or dystonia later proves dopamine-responsive, Neurology cannot simply increase dopaminergic treatment without consequence.**

The therapeutic room for manoeuvre is becoming restricted.

### Experiment 8 — 2023: a peripheral-pain strategy is pushed to its limit

March 2023 supplies a different kind of experiment.

Severe bilateral foot and lower-leg pain leads to rapid escalation of pregabalin.

The documented total reaches:

> **600 mg/day.**

Yet the Parkinson's nurse records:

> **debilitating pain**

and:

> **“no quality of life.”**

Pregabalin later provides meaningful relief and therefore plainly has therapeutic value.

But the maximum-dose episode demonstrates something equally important:

> **a powerful neuropathic-pain treatment did not completely control the disabling state.**

This does not disprove neuropathic pain.

It does not establish dystonia.

It tells us simply:

> **the pain problem was not exhausted by the pregabalin pathway.**

The attempted treatment itself generates information about the limits of that explanatory-management route.

### Experiment 9 — Brennan independently reconstructs the old relationship

During the same crisis Brennan supplies an important longitudinal observation.

He recalls a previous episode in which pain had settled when Parkinson's control improved.

Then he writes that the severe bilateral foot pains were:

> **likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone issues**

and says:

> **“this was actually his initial presentation to me now 6 years ago.”**

This is not a drug experiment in the narrow sense.

It is a **longitudinal clinical experiment**.

The clinician who knew the patient before Neurology compares two states separated by six years and identifies the same relationship:

> **feet ↔ Parkinsonian tone/control.**

The original GP phenotype has regenerated itself.

### Experiment 10 — Movement Disorders identifies an OFF relationship

Later in 2023, severe foot/leg pain is seen alongside:

- shuffling;
- reduced mobility;
- morning worsening;
- and worsening when Sinemet has not been taken.

The Movement Disorders interpretation is:

> **the leg pain and shuffling are “off related.”**

Treatment is adjusted accordingly.

This is particularly important because an OFF relationship is inherently dynamic.

It means the clinical state varies according to dopaminergic treatment coverage.

Again, it need not mean every structural lesion was produced by Parkinsonism.

But it places the painful lower-limb state squarely into the neurological treatment cycle.

The record now contains:

> **procyclidine-responsive foot phenomenon**

and:

> **dopaminergic OFF-related foot/leg pain and gait deterioration.**

Two different periods.

Two different medication contexts.

The same broad anatomical-functional territory.

### Experiment 11 — Pregabalin works, but incompletely

By 2024 pregabalin at 150 mg four times daily is producing substantial pain benefit, particularly on the right.

That fact should be preserved.

But the same record also contains:

- residual left-foot pain;
- persistent toe curling;
- fatigue;
- dyskinesia limiting dopaminergic treatment;
- and a life described as:

> **“a lot smaller.”**

This is another useful experiment because it prevents simplistic reasoning in the opposite direction.

If pregabalin helps, that does **not** prove the entire syndrome is peripheral neuropathy.

Just as Sinemet benefit does not prove every structural lesion is neurologically caused.

Different mechanisms can contribute to the same experienced pain state.

What treatment response can tell us is:

> **which aspects of the state are modifiable by which interventions.**

The case repeatedly needed integration, not binary classification.

### Experiment 12 — Pregabalin reduction exposes the remaining pain state

By March 2025 pregabalin has fallen from the previous 600 mg/day to:

> **50 mg three times daily.**

The feet again become dominant.

The patient describes:

> **“murder in the feet”**

> **“the first 150 footsteps are torture”**

and:

> **“I won't live with it.”**

Neurology simultaneously records that once Sinemet reaches the system:

> **the feet feel better.**

This is not a clean single-variable experiment because pregabalin dose, disease state and dopaminergic treatment all coexist.

But it supplies an important contrast:

> lower pregabalin coverage exposes extreme pain;

> dopaminergic state still modifies that pain.

The painful state is therefore capable of responding along more than one pharmacological axis.

That is precisely why the correct historical question was not:

> neuropathic **or** Parkinsonian?

It was:

> **what interacting mechanisms produce this foot state?**

### Experiment 13 — Sinemet CR changes the temporal coverage

The March 2025 intervention is especially informative because the problem has a strong morning pattern.

The first steps are dreadful.

The patient knows ordinary Sinemet helps after it reaches his system.

Neurology therefore changes **when dopaminergic coverage is available**, introducing controlled-release Sinemet overnight.

Within weeks Monaghan describes the effect as:

> **“profound relief.”**

This is not merely “another painkiller worked.”

The intervention modifies the treatment state across the period in which the disabling pain was worst.

The response is temporally coherent with the pre-existing observation:

> **pain is severe before effective dopaminergic coverage and improves when that coverage arrives.**

The controlled-release preparation moves the coverage earlier.

The morning phenotype changes dramatically.

### Experiment 14 — The treating neurologist says the relationship was already known

The 2025 complaint response adds an important retrospective fact.

Monaghan says:

> **“we have had a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.”**

He explains that pregabalin was being used for neuropathic pain:

> **“(of whatever cause) in part to try to spare further Sinemet increase”**

and acknowledges:

> **“incomplete effect at maximum dose”**

before describing Sinemet CR's:

> **“profound relief currently.”**

This means the 2025 response itself joins the experiments into a treatment logic:

> **Sinemet helps pain;**

> **Sinemet escalation is constrained by dyskinesia;**

> **pregabalin is used partly to avoid additional Sinemet;**

> **pregabalin is incomplete;**

> **additional/extended Sinemet produces profound relief.**

Whether that strategy was clinically optimal is an expert question.

The existence of the relationship is documentary fact.

### What the natural experiments do not prove

This profile becomes strongest if its limits remain explicit.

They do **not**, individually or collectively, prove that:

- every foot pain was dystonia;
- every callus was created by Parkinsonism;
- peripheral neuropathy was impossible;
- structural mechanics were irrelevant;
- Sinemet should necessarily have been started in July 2017;
- procyclidine and levodopa act through identical mechanisms;
- or every later symptom has one cause.

Those would be overstatements.

What the experiments establish is narrower:

> **The foot/lower-limb state was repeatedly and materially modifiable by neurological treatment state.**

And:

> **A separate pain-directed pathway could substantially help but did not fully account for or control the whole state.**

Those facts directly bear upon an original relationship recorded as:

> **“Not clear.”**

### The pattern is more important than any single experiment

Any individual observation can be explained away locally.

#### Procyclidine

Perhaps it improved general movement and therefore indirectly improved the feet.

#### Outside-left-foot return

Perhaps increased tone altered loading.

#### Bilateral cramping

Perhaps one of several manifestations of worsening neurological state.

#### Pregabalin benefit

Perhaps there was a neuropathic component.

#### OFF-related pain

Perhaps Parkinsonism amplified pain originating structurally.

#### Sinemet benefit

Perhaps dopamine changed pain perception rather than underlying pathology.

Each explanation may be medically possible.

But notice what happens if every one is accepted.

You still arrive at:

> **the patient's structural foot pain is repeatedly sensitive to neurological state.**

The local explanations do not remove the relationship.

They describe possible mechanisms of the relationship.

That distinction is fundamental.

### This is where “of whatever cause” becomes insufficient as a longitudinal answer

By 2025 Monaghan can accept Sinemet's effect while saying pain may improve:

> **“of whatever cause.”**

That is logically possible.

But as a longitudinal explanation it does not dispose of the experiments.

Because the clinical question was never simply:

> **Can dopamine reduce subjective pain perception?**

The question generated by the history is:

> **Why does a patient with a dystonic-looking lower limb and a repeatedly failing structural fifth-MTPJ site show recurrent changes in the feet, gait, cramping, loading-related pain and mobility as neurological treatment state changes?**

“Pain modulation” may be one part of that answer.

It cannot by itself determine the structural-dynamic relationship.

### The experiments form a staircase

Seen chronologically, the evidential progression is striking.

#### 2017 — treatment responsiveness

> major restoration of movement and function.

#### October 2017 — withdrawal signal

> **“my feet are restless and my walk is falling apart.”**

#### April 2018 — focal neurological signal

> rapid return of the outside-left-foot phenomenon off procyclidine.

#### September 2018 — paired deterioration

> increased tone + bilateral foot cramping + **“we are losing ground.”**

#### 2021–2022 — treatment ceiling

> levodopa benefit constrained by early chorea/dyskinesia.

#### March 2023 — pain-pathway ceiling

> 600 mg/day pregabalin alongside debilitating pain and **“no quality of life.”**

#### 2023 — specialist dynamic classification

> pain and shuffling judged **OFF-related**.

#### 2024 — mixed response

> pregabalin helps substantially, but left-foot pain and toe curling persist.

#### March 2025 — temporal dopaminergic relationship

> severe morning feet improve when Sinemet reaches the system.

#### March 2025 — coverage experiment

> Sinemet CR introduced → **“profound relief.”**

That is not one retrospective inference.

It is an accumulating series of treatment-state observations.

### Why the earliest experiment may be the most important

Ironically, the most important experiment may not be Sinemet CR in 2025.

It may be the 2017–2018 procyclidine withdrawal.

Why?

Because it occurred before years of accumulated diagnostic controversy.

It occurred soon after the original “Not clear.”

It involved the same treating neurological service.

It happened while the patient was experiencing dramatic general neurological benefit.

And it caused the **outside-left-foot phenomenon** to reappear rapidly as neurological control deteriorated.

So nature supplied a calibration point almost immediately after discharge.

The question was available then:

> **Does the foot problem change with neurological state?**

The answer was already:

> **yes, at least in a clinically important respect.**

The later eight years did not introduce that question.

They repeatedly answered it more loudly.

### The real failure revealed by the natural experiments

The issue is therefore not a failure to obtain one perfect diagnostic test.

The patient effectively underwent multiple real-world tests over time.

The more interesting failure is **failure of integration**.

Each experiment could be recorded as a local fact:

> procyclidine works;

> stopping it worsens gait;

> foot cramping accompanies tone;

> Neupro causes dyskinesia;

> pregabalin helps pain;

> Sinemet causes dyskinesia;

> OFF causes pain;

> Sinemet helps feet;

> Sinemet CR helps more.

But the original relationship could remain untouched.

This is the information phenomenon we have already identified:

> **local update without global reconstruction.**

The record keeps learning.

The case model keeps surviving.

### Profile of the Natural Experiments

> **The eight-year treatment history repeatedly tested the relationship that July 2017 had left “Not clear.” Procyclidine first produced extraordinary neurological recovery; during planned withdrawal the patient's tension returned, his feet became restless and his walk fell apart, and Neurology subsequently recorded rapid return of the focal outside-left-foot phenomenon. As effective anticholinergic treatment reached its toxicity ceiling, increased tone and bilateral foot cramping returned together. Alternative treatments demonstrated substantial responsiveness but progressively exposed behavioural, visual, cognitive and dyskinetic limits. When levodopa eventually arrived, the anticipated dyskinesia ceiling rapidly became real. In 2023 a separate neuropathic-pain strategy was pushed to 600 mg/day pregabalin yet debilitating foot/leg pain and “no quality of life” persisted; later Movement Disorders expressly interpreted pain and shuffling as OFF-related. Pregabalin subsequently provided real but incomplete benefit, while toe curling and residual foot pain remained. By March 2025 severe morning pain was known to improve when Sinemet reached the system, and extending dopaminergic coverage overnight with Sinemet CR produced “profound relief.” None of these observations alone proves a single cause for all foot pathology. Together they establish something narrower and more difficult to ignore: the clinical state of the feet and lower limbs was repeatedly modifiable by neurological treatment state. The patient's body therefore kept experimentally interrogating the very structural-dynamic relationship that the foundational record had left unresolved.**

The irreducible version is:

> **The hospital wrote “Not clear.”**
> **The patient's body then spent eight years running the experiment.**
> **The answer was never that the structural lesion was unreal.**
> **The answer was that its clinical expression repeatedly changed with the neurological state.**

<!-- Original series navigation: Next: Profile 3 — The Life of “Not Clear.” -->

---

<a id="profile-13"></a>

---

# Risk Rank 11 — Original Profile 14

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Tracks the long life of “Not clear” and the absence of an identified point at which the founding relationship is globally reformulated.

## Profile 14 — The Life of “Not Clear”

<!-- SOURCE: supplied in project conversation on 1 September 2026; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 3 of 12 — The Life of “Not Clear” -->

### How an Unresolved Question Became a Durable Clinical Position

The phrase **“Not clear”** is one of the most important objects in the case.

At first glance it looks harmless.

Medicine contains uncertainty. A neurologist is entitled to say that a relationship is not clear. Indeed, doing so can be evidence of appropriate caution.

But in this case the question is not whether uncertainty was permissible.

It is:

> **What happened to the uncertainty after it was recorded?**

Because “Not clear” did not behave like a temporary unanswered question.

It behaved like a **stable information state**.

### 1. What was “Not clear” in July 2017?

By the end of the admission, Neurology knew several things.

The patient had severe young-onset Parkinsonism.

His gait was strikingly abnormal.

A lower-limb phenomenon resembled footdrop but was considered suggestive of dystonic posture.

His feet were structurally abnormal.

They had been painful for years.

Toe phenomena were present.

PRKN was under serious consideration.

And the GP had sent those features into Neurology as part of one unresolved presentation.

The unresolved question was therefore not simply:

> **Why does this man have a corn?**

It was:

> **What is the relationship between the painful structural feet and the abnormal neurological lower-limb phenotype?**

The discharge synthesis answered:

> **“Not clear.”**

At that instant, that could have been an entirely legitimate answer.

But only if its meaning was:

> **We do not yet know. Therefore this relationship remains open.**

That is not what happened operationally.

### 2. “Not clear” was immediately given somewhere to go

The structural feet were assigned to Podiatry.

The Parkinsonian syndrome remained with Neurology.

The treatment strategy became highly developed.

So “Not clear” acquired a peculiar practical meaning:

> **uncertain enough not to explain, but sufficiently settled not to prevent separation.**

That is the first transformation.

The uncertainty did not keep the clinical object together.

It permitted the object to divide.

### 3. The Protest changes the meaning of the words

Then the patient protests.

This is decisive.

Before the Protest, Monaghan could regard “Not clear” as ordinary residual uncertainty.

After the Protest, he possessed additional knowledge:

> **the patient was expressly telling him that the feet remained unresolved because the admission had not adequately returned to them.**

So “Not clear” now had two possible meanings.

#### Innocent uncertainty

> We investigated this relationship and could not determine it.

#### Contested incompleteness

> We have not resolved this relationship, and the patient has directly challenged that we have not adequately investigated it.

Those are very different propositions.

The final synthesis preserves:

> **“Not clear.”**

It does not preserve which kind of “not clear” it was.

That ambiguity becomes important for everything that follows.

### 4. “Not clear” loses its owner

A properly active uncertainty ordinarily belongs somewhere.

Someone should be responsible for asking:

> What evidence would resolve this?

> What happens if the symptom changes?

> What result would make us reconsider?

> Who is to bring this back for review?

But the relationship in this case acquires no obvious clinical owner.

Neurology owns Parkinsonism.

Podiatry owns the structural lesion.

Primary care owns continuity.

Nobody obviously owns:

> **the interaction between dystonic/Parkinsonian lower-limb mechanics and the painful structural feet.**

So “Not clear” becomes an uncertainty without a natural escalation mechanism.

That is the second transformation:

> **uncertainty becomes orphaned.**

### 5. New evidence should have reduced the uncertainty

Almost immediately, the patient's body begins producing information bearing upon it.

Procyclidine dramatically improves the neurological state.

On withdrawal:

> tension returns;

> the feet become restless;

> gait falls apart;

> the outside-left-foot phenomenon rapidly returns.

That is new information.

It does not establish a complete mechanism.

But it should change the probability structure around the original uncertainty.

The question is no longer simply:

> **Are the feet separate?**

It becomes:

> **Why does neurological treatment state alter the clinical expression of the foot territory?**

In other words, “Not clear” should now contain **more information than it did at discharge**.

But there is no visible global reconstruction.

### 6. The fifth MTPJ should have made “Not clear” more specific

Podiatry then repeatedly identifies the persistent focal lesion at the left fifth MTPJ.

Eight visits.

Eight treatments at the same site.

So the uncertainty could now have been refined:

> **There is a stable structural pressure lesion at the left fifth MTPJ whose clinical expression appears capable of changing with neurological state.**

That is far more informative than:

> **Not clear.**

Yet because structural information sits with Podiatry and dynamic information sits with Neurology, the combined proposition does not naturally become anybody's recorded conclusion.

So the uncertainty survives not because no information has arrived.

It survives because the information arrives **partitioned**.

### 7. By 2018 the uncertainty should no longer have been static

Brennan reports:

> bilateral foot cramping;

> increased neurological tone;

> previous great improvement;

> difficulty increasing treatment because of adverse effects;

> and “we are losing ground.”

This is another signal.

The relationship is being regenerated.

Worse neurological state and worse foot phenomena are appearing together.

Again, this does not prove a single cause.

But it makes the original uncertainty narrower.

Yet the case does not visibly move from:

> **Not clear**

to:

> **partially characterised mixed structural–neurological foot disorder.**

The uncertainty remains durable.

### 8. By 2019 primary care is more definite than the founding specialist account

Brennan explicitly relates the foot problems to:

> increased tone;

> abnormal Parkinsonian gait;

> clawing;

> and pain.

That is significant.

The GP who originally sent the integrated phenotype into Neurology is now reconstructing the relationship again.

Yet the specialist founding state still does not appear to be globally replaced.

So we now have an inversion:

> **primary care is expressing an integrated relationship more directly than the original specialist discharge synthesis.**

That should have put additional pressure on “Not clear.”

It did not dislodge it.

### 9. Then the treatment window narrows

Levodopa eventually arrives.

Dyskinesia emerges.

The feared 2017 treatment complication becomes real.

This is important for “Not clear” because the cost of not resolving the relationship is increasing.

If the feet later prove dopamine-responsive, the service can no longer simply increase treatment without consequence.

The unresolved foot question is therefore becoming **more clinically important at the same time as it becomes harder to interrogate therapeutically**.

The original uncertainty is no longer harmless.

It is accumulating consequences.

### 10. March 2023 should have been a terminal challenge to “Not clear”

The foot pain becomes catastrophic.

The patient reaches 600 mg/day pregabalin.

The GP records extreme distress.

The Parkinson's service records debilitating pain and no quality of life.

Brennan then explicitly says the severe bilateral foot pains are likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone and recognises:

> **this was actually the original presentation six years earlier.**

At this point the original relationship has returned with extraordinary force.

The clinician who sent the patient into Neurology is effectively saying:

> **We are back where we started.**

If “Not clear” were an actively managed uncertainty, this should be a natural point for global reconstruction.

Instead, the case continues through local management:

pain treatment;

Parkinson medication;

dose adjustment;

specialist communication.

The original proposition survives.

### 11. Movement Disorders then supplies an explicitly dynamic explanation

Later in 2023 the pain and shuffling are characterised as:

> **OFF-related.**

That is not merely another symptom.

It means the clinical state changes according to dopaminergic coverage.

Once again, the uncertainty should become more specific.

At minimum:

> **some clinically important component of the painful lower-limb state is dynamically linked to Parkinsonian treatment state.**

But this does not visibly force the 2017 structural/peripheral disposition to be rewritten.

The new fact is added.

The old uncertainty remains.

### 12. By 2025 the language reveals what has happened

The mature form of the uncertainty appears in:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

That sentence is extraordinary when placed eight years after:

> **“Not clear.”**

The hypothesis has not matured into:

> confirmed peripheral neuropathy.

Nor has it disappeared.

It remains conditional.

> **If.**

At exactly the same time, Monaghan says there has been:

> **a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.**

So by 2025 the record contains:

> **clear knowledge of neurological treatment effect**

alongside:

> **continuing uncertainty about a peripheral explanatory component.**

This shows that “Not clear” did not remain because nothing was learned.

A great deal was learned.

The uncertainty survived **around** the new learning.

### 13. Sinemet CR should have transformed the question again

By March 2025 the patient knows that his feet improve once Sinemet takes effect.

The morning problem is extreme.

Controlled-release Sinemet is introduced.

The result is described as:

> **“profound relief.”**

That observation does not prove:

> no structural pathology;

> no peripheral contribution;

> no pain-modulation effect.

But it adds another major piece of information to the original relationship.

At this point an actively updating formulation might say:

> **The patient has genuine structural foot disease, but the severity of the painful foot state is materially dependent upon dopaminergic state; the historical structural/peripheral and dystonic/Parkinsonian components require reintegration.**

Instead the old uncertainty remains capable of accommodating the new response.

That is the central phenomenon.

### 14. “Not clear” became elastic

Normally new evidence reduces uncertainty.

Here, “Not clear” became capable of absorbing almost anything.

A structural lesion?

Compatible.

Possible neuropathy?

Compatible.

Dystonic-looking gait?

Compatible.

Procyclidine-responsive foot phenomenon?

Compatible.

Bilateral cramping with tone?

Compatible.

Pregabalin benefit?

Compatible.

OFF-related pain?

Compatible.

Sinemet-responsive feet?

Compatible.

Profound relief with Sinemet CR?

Still compatible.

That is not ordinary uncertainty anymore.

It is an **elastic explanatory space**.

The wider the range of evidence it can absorb without changing, the less useful it becomes as an active clinical hypothesis.

### 15. The contrast with Sinemet uncertainty is revealing

The Sinemet policy was also uncertain.

But its uncertainty behaved differently.

The clinicians said:

> Sinemet may work extremely well;

> it might be regretted that it was not started sooner;

> but there are strong reasons to delay it;

> and the decision may change in future.

That is **owned uncertainty**.

It remains inside Neurology.

It has a decision-maker.

It has monitoring.

It has explicit conditions under which management can change.

And management eventually does change.

By contrast, the foot relationship receives:

> **“Not clear.”**

Then Podiatry.

No equivalent neurological ownership.

No explicit reconstruction threshold.

No clearly stated trigger for reopening.

So:

> **Sinemet uncertainty was managed.**

> **Foot uncertainty was maintained.**

That distinction may be one of the most important in the case.

### 16. The phrase became protective of the original disposition

This does not require an allegation of conscious intent.

Structurally, however, “Not clear” performs an important protective function.

Because the original account never makes a strong positive claim about neuropathy, later evidence cannot easily falsify it.

If neuropathy had been definitively diagnosed and later disproved, the record would visibly fail.

But:

> **“may have been”**

> **“perhaps”**

> **“Not clear”**

> **“If there is a degree…”**

are difficult to falsify.

They preserve possibility.

That makes the original disposition unusually durable.

New evidence can weaken one possible explanation without defeating the architecture, because the architecture never commits fully to it.

### 17. This is why uncertainty itself becomes part of the allegation

The allegation should not be:

> **Neurology was uncertain in 2017.**

That would be unfair.

It is:

> **Neurology converted an insufficiently resolved relationship into an uncertainty that did not require active ownership, and that uncertainty remained available for eight years despite repeated information capable of narrowing, reorganising or displacing it.**

The problem is not uncertainty.

It is **the failure of uncertainty to update**.

### 18. The Protest is the control experiment for “Not clear”

The Protest gives us one further insight.

The patient said, before the final document existed:

> **this is not sufficiently resolved.**

That means we know that the difference between:

> **uncertainty after adequate inquiry**

and:

> **uncertainty after inadequate return to the problem**

was already contested at the beginning.

The final document omits that distinction.

Once omitted, every later reader encounters “Not clear” without knowing that its adequacy was challenged before it became authoritative.

That greatly increases the durability of the phrase.

### 19. The record then uses its own uncertainty as evidence of open-mindedness

By 2025 the continued conditional:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

can be presented as evidence that the neurologist never dogmatically insisted upon neuropathy.

At one level that is true.

But longitudinally it produces a paradox.

> **The fact that the question was never resolved becomes evidence that the clinician remained open-minded, rather than evidence that the question may never have been adequately reconstructed.**

The absence of closure becomes self-protective.

That is a remarkable inversion.

### 20. What should have happened to “Not clear”?

There were only a few intellectually coherent destinations.

It could have become:

#### Resolved structural

> The pain is adequately explained by structural pathology, and the dystonic lower-limb phenomenon does not materially contribute.

#### Resolved neurological

> The painful foot state is primarily driven by dystonia/Parkinsonian treatment state.

#### Mixed

> Structural disease is genuine, but neurological posture, tone or OFF state materially modifies loading, pain or function.

#### Persistently unresolved but actively owned

> We still do not know; this remains an open Movement Disorders problem and later treatment responses must be interpreted against it.

Any of those would show evolution.

Instead the core state remains remarkably close to:

> **maybe structural; maybe peripheral; neurological treatment affects it; Not clear.**

That is the life of the phrase.

### Profile of “Not Clear”

> **“Not clear” began in July 2017 as an apparently legitimate statement of residual uncertainty about the relationship between painful structural feet and a dystonic-looking young-onset Parkinsonian lower-limb syndrome. Its significance changed when the patient directly challenged the treating neurologist before discharge that the feet had not actually been adequately dealt with, converting residual uncertainty into known contested incompleteness. The final synthesis nevertheless carried “Not clear” forward while routing the structural feet to Podiatry and retaining Parkinsonian management within Neurology. Over the following eight years the patient's body and clinicians repeatedly generated information capable of narrowing that uncertainty: procyclidine withdrawal altered the focal foot territory and gait; bilateral foot cramping returned with increased tone; Podiatry repeatedly treated the same fifth-MTPJ site; Brennan explicitly linked the feet to Parkinsonian tone and gait; dyskinesia narrowed the therapeutic window; catastrophic foot pain emerged; Movement Disorders characterised pain and shuffling as OFF-related; Sinemet was known to improve the feet; and Sinemet CR ultimately produced profound relief. Yet by 2025 the peripheral proposition remained only “If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”. The uncertainty survived not because the case generated no new information, but because new information could be assimilated without requiring the founding relationship to be reconstructed. “Not clear” therefore ceased to function merely as an unanswered question and became a durable information state.**

The irreducible version is:

> **“Not clear” should have been a question.**
> **Instead it became a destination.**

And the harder version:

> **The most durable diagnosis in the case may have been no diagnosis at all: an uncertainty broad enough to survive every answer the patient's body subsequently supplied.**

# Risk Rank 12 — Original Profile 04

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Primary care provides a longitudinal independent control repeatedly reconnecting feet, gait, tone, pain and Parkinsonism.

## Profile 04 — Profile of the GP, the Referral and Eight Years of Primary Care

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-114530).md -->

<!-- Original source heading: Profile of the GP, the Referral and Eight Years of Primary Care -->

### Dr Aaron Brennan / Galway East Primary Care — 2017 to 2025

#### Why primary care matters

Dr Aaron Brennan occupies an unusual position in this history.

He saw the patient **before there was a specialist diagnosis**.

He generated the referral that brought the patient into University Hospital Galway.

He then received the specialist representation produced by that admission.

And over the following eight years he continued to encounter the feet, gait, tone, medication effects, treatment limitations, pain crises and functional consequences in ordinary primary care.

This makes Brennan more than the referring GP.

He is a longitudinal control.

He allows the record to be compared at two different states:

> **What did the problem look like before specialist transformation?**

and:

> **What kept reappearing in primary care after that transformation?**

The GP record does not support a simple story in which primary care failed to recognise that the feet and neurological disorder might be related.

The opposite occurs repeatedly.

Primary care sees the relationship, loses authority over its specialist interpretation, regenerates the relationship over time, and repeatedly sends clinically significant information back upstream.

### 1. April 2017 — the case begins with feet and gait

When the patient transferred into the practice in April 2017, there was no Parkinson's diagnosis.

Brennan's first recorded presentation was:

- abnormal gait;
- bilateral foot pain;
- difficulty working on his feet;
- marked mallet toes;
- onychogryphosis;
- calluses and pressure areas.

There was genuine structural pathology.

At that point Brennan did not pretend to know its cause. Podiatry was planned, basic investigation was undertaken, and previous records were sought.

The starting primary-care question was therefore appropriately open:

> **Why does this man have abnormal painful feet and an abnormal gait?**

That uncertainty did not last long in its purely podiatric form.

By **8 June 2017**, Brennan was recording difficulty initiating movement and the patient's unusually precise description of using the deceleration of a bus to help start walking after sitting.

Brennan's own phrase is particularly important.

He was considering Neurology for:

> **“gait, feet and ‘muscle tension’ issues.”**

Primary care had already begun to integrate the presentation before a specialist ever saw it.

### 2. June 2017 — recognition of a neurological syndrome

Concern then accelerated.

An occupational-health doctor independently contacted Brennan regarding the gait.

By **23 June**, Brennan was recording:

- marked increased tone in both legs;
- brisk reflexes;
- inability to relax;
- worsening problems at work;
- difficulty gowning and ungowning;
- and alcohol being used at night in an attempt to relax the muscles.

The patient was deteriorating.

By **29 June**, an attempted return to work had exposed worsening dexterity and functional failure.

Brennan escalated urgently.

### 3. The referral — the clean pre-specialist representation

The **29 June 2017 referral** should probably be treated as one of the most important control documents in the entire case.

It was written before the university Neurology service had interpreted the presentation.

It therefore shows what entered hospital.

Brennan begins with the feet:

> the patient had attended with **bilateral foot issues**

and had attributed the gait to:

> **“callouses and corns”**

adding:

> **“his feet are not in a good state.”**

But Brennan does not accept the feet as a complete explanation.

He then brings together:

- the unusual festinant and stiff gait;
- trouble initiating movements;
- marked stiffness;
- increased tone in both upper and lower limbs;
- brisk reflexes;
- possible wrist cogwheeling;
- inability to relax;
- toe-clawing in bed;
- inability to become comfortable;
- worsening dexterity;
- difficulty with repetitive manual tasks;
- and occupational deterioration.

His conclusion is deliberately unresolved:

> **“?parkinsonian or other underlying diagnosis”**

and:

> **“certainly seems to be deteriorating.”**

He asks for urgent assessment because if the deterioration continues:

> **“he will lose his job.”**

This referral is important for what it does **not** do.

It does not send:

> Parkinsonism + unrelated foot problem.

It does not send:

> foot disease with incidental stiffness.

It does not diagnose neuropathy.

It does not separate the gait from the feet.

It sends **one patient with one unresolved syndrome**:

> **feet + gait + tone + toe-clawing + movement-initiation difficulty + deteriorating dexterity + failing function.**

That is the informational state entering Galway Neurology.

### 4. July 2017 — the GP becomes the recipient of the specialist frame

After referral, Brennan changes position.

He is no longer simply the clinician asking the question.

He becomes the downstream recipient of the answer.

The hospital returns a much richer neurological account:

- severe genuine Parkinsonism;
- very-young-onset disease;
- atypical features;
- dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenology;
- possible recessive/genetic Parkinsonism;
- structural foot abnormalities;
- deliberate restraint around levodopa;
- Podiatry for the feet;
- and an unresolved explanation of the painful foot phenomena ending in **“Not clear.”**

Primary care therefore now possesses two representations of the same history.

#### The first

Brennan's own:

> **integrated feet–gait–tone–toe–function problem requiring neurological explanation.**

#### The second

The specialist forward representation:

> **Parkinsonism extensively characterised and neurologically managed; painful structural feet remaining causally “Not clear” with Podiatry as their practical pathway.**

This distinction becomes important because Brennan was actively waiting for formal hospital correspondence.

On **6 July**, while the patient was still admitted, he contacted Podiatry and had the case prioritised **“as Parkinson's.”**

On **17 July**, he saw the patient after physical discharge while recording that formal discharge correspondence had still not arrived.

Primary care therefore had to continue caring for the patient while the definitive specialist representation was still travelling downstream.

### 5. 2017–2018 — neurological treatment changes the bodily picture

Primary care then implemented the specialist treatment strategy.

Procyclidine produced substantial improvement.

Brennan witnessed improvement sufficiently marked that dose escalation was initially unnecessary.

Then treatment withdrawal provided new information.

When procyclidine was stopped because of eyesight concerns, the record later captured deterioration involving:

- increased tension;
- restless feet;
- worsening gait;
- and rapid return of an outside-left-foot phenomenon.

Symptoms improved again after treatment resumed.

Whatever the precise clinical interpretation, this was no longer a world in which every foot phenomenon could be understood simply as a static structural lesion.

At least part of the foot/lower-limb territory changed rapidly with neurological treatment state.

Primary care remained involved in implementing and monitoring that treatment.

### 6. September 2018 — Brennan reconnects feet and neurological deterioration

By September 2018 Brennan again sent information upstream.

He recorded:

- renewed increased tone;
- deterioration affecting the arm and hand;
- **cramping in both feet**;
- previous substantial improvement;
- treatment benefit limited by visual and cognitive effects;
- and uncertainty about what should happen next.

His concern was expressed plainly:

> **“in case we are losing ground here.”**

This is important because the feet have not disappeared from Brennan's neurological picture.

They recur alongside increased tone and treatment limitation.

And Brennan does what a GP should be expected to do when specialist neurological management is required:

he re-escalates to Movement Disorders.

He does not independently redesign the specialist treatment programme.

### 7. 2019 — Brennan makes the relationship explicit

The 2019 Podiatry referral is particularly revealing.

By then Brennan writes that the patient had previously attended Podiatry because of:

> **“feet issues in relating to increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease.”**

He also writes:

> **“Parkinsons at very young age has issues with ‘clawing feet’ and pain.”**

The referral retains genuine structural information, including gait abnormalities, arch/rearfoot problems and callus.

Importantly, the low-risk form records:

> **“No neuropathy.”**

The evidential point is not that Brennan thereby proved a single neurological cause for all foot pain.

It is that by 2019 his own working representation had become explicitly relational:

> **feet + clawing + pain + increased tone + abnormal gait + Parkinson's disease.**

The GP has regenerated the relationship that the specialist discharge synthesis had left “Not clear.”

### 8. 2021 — the origin has not been forgotten

Four years after the admission, Brennan prepared a report that reconstructed the beginning of the case.

His account still remembered the originating presentation as:

- bilateral foot problems;
- stiff/festinant gait;
- initiation difficulty;
- marked tone;
- brisk reflexes;
- toe-clawing;
- inability to relax;
- worsening dexterity;
- and occupational failure.

This is important longitudinally.

The integrated origin did not vanish from primary-care memory.

The GP who made the original referral could still reconstruct it years later.

The problem was therefore not that all clinicians downstream had simply forgotten that the feet were there.

The more subtle issue was whether the **relationship** between the feet and the neurological syndrome had an authoritative specialist formulation capable of travelling with those facts.

### 9. March 2023 — primary care reopens the original problem

March 2023 is one of the most important primary-care episodes in the entire eight-year history.

It should not be reduced to Brennan sending another referral.

The wider practice became involved.

Another senior GP in the practice, **Dr Aileen Faherty**, returned to the feet and sought bilateral radiological investigation.

The resulting X-rays did not identify the anticipated significant structural/bony explanation.

The patient then communicated an acute pain crisis to the practice.

Another GP callback was arranged.

Brennan urgently escalated the matter to Neurology.

His referral describes:

- severe bilateral burning foot/lower-leg pain;
- rapid escalation of pregabalin;
- extreme psychological distress;
- a **“death wish”**;
- his own concern — **“I am worried about him”**;
- a prior occasion on which pain settled when the Parkinson's condition was better controlled;
- and a request for specialist review:

> **“before we get into trouble here.”**

Two days later, the Parkinson's nurse recorded debilitating feet/leg pain despite **600 mg/day pregabalin** and effectively no quality of life.

Then, on **31 March 2023**, Brennan makes the longitudinal connection expressly.

He describes the severe bilateral foot pains as:

> **“likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone issues”**

and writes:

> **“this was actually his initial presentation to me now 6 years ago.”**

That sentence is extremely important.

Six years after writing the original referral, Brennan himself recognises the severe current foot crisis as belonging to the territory through which the patient originally entered his care.

Primary care has therefore reconstructed the line:

> **2017 feet → Parkinsonian tone/gait → 2023 severe bilateral feet.**

### 10. The 2023 episode also exposes the limit of primary-care authority

By this stage Brennan and the practice are doing a great deal.

They are:

- examining;
- investigating;
- ordering imaging;
- prescribing pregabalin;
- escalating urgently;
- communicating with Neurology;
- correcting prescriptions;
- reconciling actual versus intended doses;
- managing work and general health consequences;
- and repeatedly receiving patient reports of benefit, failure and adverse effects.

But the therapeutic problem has become specialised.

Neurological treatments have genuine benefits and genuine ceilings:

- procyclidine helps but produces significant toxicity;
- dopamine agonist strategies create their own problems;
- levodopa improves function but dyskinesia restricts escalation;
- pregabalin reaches maximum dosing without resolving the entire clinical problem.

The GP can see this.

He cannot independently redesign it.

That distinction becomes explicit later.

### 11. 2025 — the GP states the authority boundary himself

By 2025 the patient was asking Brennan directly for help with increasingly difficult levodopa management.

Brennan's reply is revealing because he does **not** deny the problem.

He says:

> **“I am certainly minded to help”**

but continues:

> **“as a GP I cannot really be adjusting your levodopa meds without Neurology specialist guidance as that would be well beyond my ordinary scope of expertise.”**

This is the end-point of the primary-care profile.

The clinician with the longest uninterrupted view of the patient can:

- recognise;
- investigate;
- record;
- refer;
- prescribe within primary-care competence;
- reconcile;
- chase;
- support;
- and repeatedly transmit information.

But he cannot perform the specialist neurological reset.

The record therefore contains an **authority asymmetry** as well as an information asymmetry:

> **the clinician who retains the longitudinal relationship does not possess the specialist authority required to reconstruct the neurological management framework.**

### Primary care over eight years

The longitudinal picture is remarkably consistent.

#### 2017

Brennan sees:

> **gait + feet + muscle tension**

and sends an integrated urgent neurological referral.

#### 2018

He sees:

> **returning neurological tone + bilateral foot cramping**

and worries that:

> **“we are losing ground.”**

#### 2019

He expressly describes:

> **feet issues related to increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease**

with:

> **“clawing feet” and pain.**

#### 2021

He still remembers the originating syndrome as:

> **feet + stiff/festinant gait + initiation difficulty + tone + toe-clawing + inability to relax.**

#### 2023

During catastrophic bilateral foot pain, he says the pain is likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone and recognises:

> **“this was actually his initial presentation to me now 6 years ago.”**

#### 2025

He remains willing to help but states that alteration of the neurological treatment architecture lies beyond GP authority without specialist guidance.

### What the GP profile does — and does not — show

This profile does **not** establish that Brennan always interpreted every part of the case correctly.

It does not establish that every GP action was sufficient.

It does not mean every foot symptom was neurological.

It does not mean every piece of information held by Galway East was personally read by Brennan; practice-level knowledge, returned specialist information and Brennan-authored knowledge must remain distinct.

But Brennan's own authored documents establish something simpler and more important:

> **Primary care repeatedly preserved or regenerated the relationship between the feet, gait, tone and neurological disorder.**

The feet did not disappear.

The gait did not disappear.

The tone did not disappear.

The original presentation was not forgotten.

And in 2023, six years later, Brennan himself explicitly recognised the severe foot crisis as a return to the original presenting territory.

### Brennan as the natural experiment

This is what makes the GP so valuable to the reconstruction.

He exists **before and after** the specialist information arrangement.

Before Neurology:

> **one patient — one unresolved feet/gait/tone syndrome.**

After Neurology:

> **confirmed Parkinsonism + specialist treatment strategy + structural feet/Podiatry + unresolved foot relationship.**

Yet as the years pass, Brennan repeatedly produces observations that cross that boundary again:

> foot cramping with neurological deterioration;

> feet linked to tone and Parkinsonian gait;

> severe foot pain linked to Parkinsonian tone;

> the 2023 crisis recognised as the territory of the original 2017 presentation.

The current archive therefore describes Brennan aptly as the strongest naturalistic trace:

> **he generated the pre-specialist integrated representation, inherited the specialist output, and then repeatedly regenerated the relationship that had become less portable in the specialist forward account.**

### The GP profile in one paragraph

> **Dr Aaron Brennan first encountered the patient in April 2017 through painful abnormal feet and gait, recognised within weeks that the feet belonged to a wider syndrome of movement-initiation difficulty, pathological tone, toe-clawing, deteriorating dexterity and functional failure, and sent that integrated problem urgently to University Hospital Galway under the open question “?parkinsonian or other underlying diagnosis.” After Neurology established Parkinsonism but returned the painful-foot relationship as “Not clear” with Podiatry as its practical destination, Brennan remained the patient's longitudinal primary-care clinician. Over the following eight years his records repeatedly reconnected the same territory: neurological treatment altered foot phenomena; bilateral foot cramping returned with increased tone; by 2019 he explicitly linked the feet to Parkinsonian tone and abnormal gait; by 2023, during catastrophic bilateral foot pain, he stated that Parkinsonian tone likely contributed and recognised that this had “actually [been] his initial presentation to me now 6 years ago.” Primary care continued to investigate, prescribe, reconcile, refer and escalate, but by 2025 Brennan expressly identified the limiting fact: redesigning levodopa and specialist neurological treatment was beyond ordinary GP authority without Neurology guidance. The primary-care story is therefore not one in which the integrated problem disappeared. It is one in which it repeatedly reappeared in the hands of the clinician with the longest continuous view, while the authority to perform the corresponding neurological reconstruction remained upstream.**

# Risk Rank 13 — Original Profile 29

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** The same clinician repeatedly stewards later facts within the founding frame, making persistence of the original account especially consequential.

## Profile 29 — The Constrained Steward of the Founding Frame

<!-- SOURCE: synthesis from the existing treating-neurologist, closed-system, self-validating-record and “Not Clear” profiles, supplemented by the treatment-state reconstruction developed in this project on 1 September 2026. -->

The longitudinal record should not be described as though a succession of unrelated clinicians independently failed to integrate the same information.

There is an unusually continuous actor.

Thomas Monaghan was:

- the treating neurologist in 2017;
- the recipient of the integrated GP referral;
- the neurologist who documented the Parkinsonian syndrome and dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop;
- the author/adopter of the later-finalised discharge synthesis;
- the continuing neurologist who received later treatment-state information;
- and, in 2025, the author who directed the reviewer back to the 2017 letters as the place where his historical reasoning could be understood.

That continuity changes the analytical object.

The problem is not simply **diffusion across many clinicians**.

It is also **stewardship of one durable representation by the person who helped create it**.

### 1. The discharge summary made a commitment, not merely a description

The July 2017 document did not simply list observations.

It converted them into a forward state:

> Parkinsonism and dystonic-looking lower-limb behaviour → Neurology;
>
> painful structural feet → possibly separate/peripheral → Podiatry;
>
> relationship → **“Not clear”;**
>
> treatment strategy → staged and cautious;
>
> outcome → **“firmer footing”;**
>
> forward state → **“a clear plan”;**
>
> operative instruction → **“As per above.”**

That is a documentary commitment.

Once the narrative becomes the plan, later information does not arrive into a neutral file.

It arrives into a file that already knows where the components belong.

### 2. The steward then receives information capable of reopening the commitment

The procyclidine dechallenge is the clearest early example.

The same neurologist later records rapid return of the outside-left-foot phenomenon when neurological treatment is withdrawn.

That fact is capable of doing more than updating medication efficacy.

It bears directly on the founding relationship:

> **the focal foot territory changes when neurological treatment state changes.**

If the 2017 relationship were actively owned, that should be a reconstruction trigger.

Instead it is capable of entering the file as a local treatment observation without altering the founding disposition.

### 3. This is what “constrained stewardship” means

“Constrained” here does not mean the neurologist lacked intelligence, access to the file or specialist knowledge.

Nor does it prove a private intention not to integrate.

It describes the observable documentary behaviour:

> **new information is repeatedly placed inside the categories established by the founding synthesis rather than being allowed to reorganise those categories.**

The file therefore exerts a constraint upon its own steward.

The relevant question becomes:

> **What does this new fact mean inside the existing account?**

rather than:

> **Does this new fact require the existing account itself to be rebuilt?**

That is the distinction between local update and global reconstruction.

### 4. The procyclidine signal should have created anticipation

Once an anticholinergic dechallenge changes the foot state in a patient with a dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop, a prospective question becomes obvious:

> **Will future neurological medications alter the same foot territory?**

That question matters because levodopa was already anticipated as likely eventually necessary and likely to be clinically effective.

The later course shows that it did alter the feet.

So the missing anticipation is not merely hindsight.

The record had already generated the type of signal from which the hypothesis could arise.

### 5. Why the hypothesis had difficulty finding a home

The discharge synthesis had made the feet operationally manageable without solving the relationship.

The structural component had a destination.

The neurological component had a destination.

The relationship did not.

Therefore the procyclidine information could be absorbed as:

> **procyclidine response / withdrawal deterioration**

without necessarily becoming:

> **evidence that the fifth-MTPJ structural–dynamic partition must be reopened.**

Later levodopa information could similarly become:

> **OFF pain / Sinemet benefit / dyskinesia problem**

without necessarily becoming:

> **evidence that the founding “Not clear” relationship should be globally reconstructed.**

### 6. The same steward then accumulates progressively stronger contradictions

The sequence becomes increasingly difficult to treat as unrelated local facts:

> 2017 — dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop + structural feet + **“Not clear.”**
>
> 2017–18 — procyclidine dechallenge changes the focal foot territory.
>
> 2018 — bilateral foot cramping returns with increased tone.
>
> 2019 — the GP explicitly links feet, clawing and pain to Parkinsonian tone and abnormal gait.
>
> 2023 — severe pain returns; primary care identifies it as the original presentation and likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone.
>
> late 2023 — Movement Disorders describes pain and shuffling as OFF-related.
>
> 2025 — the feet are known to improve once Sinemet reaches the system.
>
> 2025 — controlled-release Sinemet produces **“profound relief.”**
>
> 2025 — peripheral neuropathy remains only conditional: **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

The important continuity is not merely that the facts accumulate.

It is that the founding state remains recognisable throughout.

### 7. 2017 and 2025 expose the constraint in two phrases

At the beginning:

> **“Not clear.”**

Eight years later:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

The neuropathy proposition has not matured into a confirmed diagnosis.

Nor has the increasingly strong neurological treatment-state evidence produced an identified point of global reconstruction.

That is what makes the stewardship look constrained by the founding representation.

### 8. The 2025 rebuttal closes the stewardship loop

When the historical adequacy of the case is challenged, Monaghan does not reconstruct the foot phenotype from first principles.

He expressly points the reviewer back to the historical letters as the documents in which his reasoning, decisions, timing and authorship can be understood.

The same representation that organised the subsequent history is therefore offered as evidence validating the historical reasoning.

This does not prove dishonest intent.

It demonstrates a closed documentary relationship between:

> **author → founding representation → later stewardship → later defence by reference to the founding representation.**

### 9. The consequence is a failure of anticipation as well as integration

The founding partition did not merely affect how old facts were remembered.

It affected what future facts were expected to mean.

If the foot relationship had remained explicitly open and neurologically owned, the procyclidine dechallenge would naturally generate a forward question:

> **What happens to this same foot territory when levodopa is eventually introduced?**

Because the relationship instead became operationally settled enough to proceed, that prospective link was not built into the management architecture.

When levodopa later modified the feet, the event could therefore appear as another local treatment fact rather than confirmation of an old structural–dynamic hypothesis.

### Profile of the Constrained Steward

> **The longitudinal non-integration in this case should not be attributed only to a diffuse succession of clinicians. Thomas Monaghan was an unusually continuous actor: he received the integrated 2017 referral, documented the Parkinsonian and dystonic-looking lower-limb phenotype, authored/adopted the discharge synthesis, continued to receive treatment-state information and in 2025 directed an external reviewer back to the 2017 letters as evidence of his historical reasoning. The discharge synthesis was therefore not merely a passive description he later inherited; it was a representation he helped create and subsequently stewarded. That representation committed the file to a practical arrangement—structural feet toward Podiatry, Parkinsonism within Neurology, the relationship “Not clear,” and the overall state presented as a clear plan through “As per above.” Later facts capable of destabilising that arrangement repeatedly entered the same stewardship: procyclidine withdrawal changed the focal outside-left-foot territory; foot cramping tracked increased tone; the GP re-integrated feet and Parkinsonian gait; Movement Disorders described pain as OFF-related; Sinemet became known to improve the feet; controlled-release Sinemet produced profound relief; yet peripheral neuropathy remained conditional in 2025. “Constrained stewardship” does not require proof that the neurologist consciously wished to prevent integration. It describes the observable behaviour of the file: new information was repeatedly interpreted inside the founding categories rather than being allowed to reconstruct them. The consequence was not only non-integration of old evidence but failure to anticipate the meaning of new evidence. Once procyclidine had shown that the focal foot territory changed with neurological treatment state, future neurological medication—including levodopa—should have been capable of testing the same relationship. The record had the signal. The founding commitment gave the signal no obvious route back into the architecture.**

The irreducible form is:

> **The same neurologist helped create the frame and then stewarded the case inside it.**
>
> **The facts changed.**
>
> **The categories largely did not.**
>
> **That is how a predictive signal could be recorded without becoming a reconstruction.**
