# Risk Rank 14 — Original Profile 15

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** No durable clinical owner is identified for the relationship between structural foot disease and dynamic neurological state.

## Profile 15 — Missing Clinical Owner

<!-- SOURCE: supplied in project conversation on 1 September 2026; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 4 of 12 — The Missing Clinical Owner -->

### Who, Exactly, Owned the Relationship?

By the end of the July 2017 admission, several parts of the patient's problem had clear owners.

Neurology owned the Parkinsonism.

Podiatry owned the structural foot lesions.

Primary care owned continuity.

Later Movement Disorders owned medication-state adjustments.

The patient carried the lived whole.

But the central relationship itself—

> **the interaction between dystonic/Parkinsonian lower-limb behaviour and the painful structural feet**

—had no obvious owner.

That is the core of this profile.

The problem was not simply that different clinicians saw different aspects of the case.

The problem was that **the relationship between those aspects was never durably assigned to anyone.**

### 1. The GP initially owned the whole problem

Before specialist referral, Brennan was dealing with the patient as one integrated case.

He saw:

- feet;
- gait;
- tone;
- toe clawing;
- movement-initiation difficulty;
- deteriorating dexterity;
- functional loss.

His referral did not split those into separate settled domains.

He sent the whole unresolved phenotype onward.

At that stage, the relationship still had an owner:

> **the referring GP asking Neurology to explain it.**

But Brennan was referring precisely because the question exceeded ordinary primary-care scope.

So ownership was being transferred upward.

### 2. Neurology received the whole phenotype

Neurology then acquired all the ingredients needed to own the relationship.

It saw the structural feet.

It saw the abnormal gait.

It identified a possible dystonic lower-limb posture.

It recognised severe young-onset Parkinsonism.

It considered PRKN.

It had relevant expertise available.

It had the patient physically present for eight days.

So, at the point of admission, Neurology was the obvious service capable of integrating:

> **structure + movement + tone + pain + gait.**

That is why the later loss of ownership matters so much.

### 3. The admission identified the parts, but did not assign ownership of the relation

The discharge architecture effectively created several domains.

#### Parkinsonism

Owned by Neurology.

#### Structural feet

Sent toward Podiatry.

#### Possible neuropathic component

Left as a possibility.

#### Treatment strategy

Owned by Neurology.

#### Rehabilitation

Distributed to allied health.

#### Work and function

Distributed to occupational and primary-care management.

But the question:

> **How do the dystonic-looking lower limb and painful structural feet interact?**

was not assigned to anyone in equivalent terms.

That relationship became:

> **“Not clear.”**

And then the system moved on.

This is what it means to say the uncertainty was orphaned.

### 4. Podiatry did not own the neurological relationship

Podiatry could appropriately treat:

- callus;
- corn;
- fifth-MTPJ pressure;
- structural deformity;
- loading;
- footwear;
- offloading.

But Podiatry was not the service diagnosing:

- young-onset Parkinsonism;
- dystonia;
- PRKN;
- medication OFF states;
- dopaminergic responsiveness.

So even if Podiatry repeatedly saw the same left fifth-MTPJ lesion, that did not automatically make Podiatry the owner of the question:

> **Is abnormal neurological posture repeatedly loading this site?**

That question lay beyond the information state Podiatry had been given.

### 5. Neurology did not own the structural recurrence

Neurology, meanwhile, understood:

- motor state;
- tone;
- dystonic-looking gait;
- procyclidine response;
- dopaminergic response;
- dyskinesia;
- OFF periods.

But the repeated eight-visit Podiatry history of the same left fifth MTPJ was not naturally part of Neurology's active longitudinal object.

So when Neurology saw:

> **the outside-left-foot phenomenon returning with medication withdrawal**

it could understand that as a treatment-state observation.

But without owning the recurring structural history, it did not necessarily have a reason, within its inherited frame, to ask:

> **Is this the same repeatedly failing pressure site?**

Again, the missing object is the relationship.

### 6. Primary care intermittently owned the whole—but without specialist authority

Brennan is the exception.

Because he saw the patient over years, he repeatedly reconstructed the integrated picture.

He linked:

> feet + gait + tone.

Later:

> cramping feet + increased tone.

Later:

> clawing feet + Parkinsonian gait.

Later:

> severe foot pain + Parkinsonian tone.

He even explicitly said this was the patient's original presentation returning.

So primary care repeatedly **saw the whole**.

But Brennan did not have authority to redesign the specialist neurological model.

By 2025 he says so directly: changing levodopa without Neurology guidance was beyond ordinary GP scope.

So primary care had longitudinal integration without specialist authority.

### 7. Movement Disorders owned the dynamic state, not the historical reconstruction

Later Movement Disorders recognises pain and shuffling as OFF-related.

That is an important local interpretation.

But it does not automatically reconstruct:

> 2017 structural feet
>
> - dystonic lower limb
> - fifth-MTPJ recurrence
> - procyclidine responsiveness
> - years of pain.

Movement Disorders can correctly treat the OFF state while leaving the founding architecture intact.

Again, local competence does not necessarily create global ownership.

### 8. The patient became the only continuous owner of the relationship

The patient experienced all of these domains simultaneously.

He knew:

- the focal fifth-MTPJ pain;
- the structural treatments;
- the changing gait;
- the tone;
- the medication effects;
- the OFF states;
- the recurrence;
- the relief;
- the original Protest.

He did not need records to know that the pain and neurological state were interacting.

He lived it.

That is why, over time, the patient increasingly becomes the integrator.

The system distributes the information.

The patient carries the whole.

### 9. The Protest was an attempt to force ownership back into Neurology

This is one of the most important ways to understand the Protest.

The patient was effectively saying:

> **You cannot discharge this relationship without somebody owning it.**

He was not asking merely for another corn to be pared.

He was objecting to the fact that, after eight days of neurological investigation, the feet had not been integrated into the neurological explanation.

The Protest therefore attempted to restore ownership to the service best positioned to resolve the relationship.

That attempt failed.

The feet still left toward Podiatry.

The relation remained “Not clear.”

### 10. Once ownership was lost, every later service could behave reasonably

This is what makes the system failure difficult to see.

Podiatry could reasonably treat the structural lesion.

Neurology could reasonably treat Parkinsonism.

GP could reasonably manage pain and coordinate care.

Movement Disorders could reasonably adjust medication.

Each service could do something sensible.

Yet nobody had to answer:

> **What is the combined model?**

So the patient could receive locally rational care while the overall case remained globally incoherent.

That is the essence of the missing-owner problem.

### 11. The fifth MTPJ shows exactly why ownership matters

The fifth MTPJ is the clearest example.

Podiatry knows:

> the same site recurs.

Neurology knows:

> neurological medication changes the same foot territory.

Who owns the proposition:

> **the recurrent structural site is dynamically modified by neurological state?**

Apparently nobody.

Without an owner, the combined fact has nowhere to live.

The record therefore contains both halves but never fully assembles them.

### 12. “Not clear” became a substitute for ownership

An active unresolved problem should normally have a responsible clinician or service.

But here the phrase:

> **“Not clear”**

effectively became the resting place for the relation.

The uncertainty itself replaced an owner.

That is dangerous because uncertainty cannot:

- call another service;
- request a reassessment;
- compare serial findings;
- decide when evidence has crossed a threshold;
- reopen the phenotype.

Only an owner can do that.

So the problem was not merely that the relation was unclear.

It was that **no one was explicitly responsible for making it clearer.**

### 13. The consequence was information drift

Over eight years, each new fact entered whichever service was closest to it.

Podiatry saw recurrence.

GP saw pain.

Neurology saw medication response.

Movement Disorders saw OFF state.

Pharmacy saw prescriptions.

The patient saw the whole.

Because there was no assigned owner of the relationship, the information drifted into local compartments.

That is how a simple question became a complicated archive.

### 14. The missing owner also explains why no one reassembled the case

There was no obvious trigger.

For Podiatry, repeated fifth-MTPJ treatment could remain a structural recurrence.

For Neurology, procyclidine response could remain a neurological treatment observation.

For GP, severe foot pain could prompt escalation.

For Movement Disorders, OFF-related pain could prompt medication change.

Each action made sense locally.

But no one had the specific responsibility:

> **reconstruct the entire historical relationship when enough cross-domain evidence accumulates.**

So reconstruction never became anybody's job.

### 15. This is why the discharge architecture mattered so much

The discharge summary did not merely describe current findings.

It implicitly allocated responsibility.

By saying:

> Parkinsonism here;

> Podiatry there;

> “Not clear” in between;

it created a map of who should do what.

The missing relation had no box.

Once the map was inherited, later clinicians could follow it faithfully.

That is the deeper effect of the discharge summary.

### 16. The 2025 complaint finally tries to create an owner again

The complaint asks the institution to investigate the reasoning from 2017 onward.

In effect, it says:

> **someone must now own the whole history.**

But the rebuttal sends the reader back to the 2017 letters.

So instead of creating a new global owner, the institution initially returns to the old allocation map.

That is why the complaint response feels circular.

The patient asks for integration.

The response points back to the document that fragmented the relationship.

### Profile of the Missing Clinical Owner

> **The central unresolved relationship in the case never acquired a durable clinical owner. Primary care initially sent an integrated feet–gait–tone phenotype to Neurology precisely because specialist integration was required. Neurology identified both the dynamic neurological component and the structural foot pathology but left their relationship “Not clear,” retaining Parkinsonism and treatment while routing the structural feet toward Podiatry. Podiatry then repeatedly treated the same structural fifth-MTPJ site but did not possess the neurological treatment-state history; Neurology accumulated dynamic information about dystonia, medication response and OFF state but did not own the repeated structural recurrence; primary care repeatedly regenerated the relationship but lacked specialist authority to reconstruct it; Movement Disorders later treated OFF-related pain locally without necessarily rebuilding the historical phenotype. The patient became the only continuous holder of the whole relationship. The problem was therefore not simply uncertainty. It was uncertainty without ownership. Each service could act rationally within its own compartment while the integrated clinical object remained nobody's explicit responsibility.**

The irreducible version is:

> **Everyone owned a part.**
> **Nobody owned the relationship.**
> **The patient had to live the whole.**

And the hardest version:

> **“Not clear” became the place where responsibility for integration disappeared.**

<!-- Original series navigation: Next: Profile 5 — The Cross-Institution Information Split. -->

<a id="profile-16"></a>

# Risk Rank 15 — Original Profile 17

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Catalogues the specific integrating clinical operations that never occurred despite repeated opportunities and later evidence.

## Profile 17 — What Never Happened

<!-- SOURCE: supplied in project conversation on 1 September 2026; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 6 of 12 — What Never Happened -->

### The Missing Clinical Operations

This profile is different from the others.

It does not ask what diagnosis was made, what drug was prescribed, what somebody later believed, or which explanation ultimately proved strongest.

It asks something much more basic:

> **What clinical operations would have been necessary to resolve the original relationship—and which of those operations never occurred?**

That matters because the case contains an enormous amount of activity.

There are admissions, consultations, scans, blood tests, genetics, medication trials, Podiatry visits, GP correspondence, Movement Disorders reviews, dose adjustments and complaint responses.

The volume of activity can make the case appear thoroughly investigated.

But activity is not the same thing as completion.

The missing operations reveal the difference.

### 1. There was no second focused examination of the feet during the eight-day admission

This is the first and most important absence.

The feet were examined on the first day.

Structural abnormality was recognised.

The history of corns, calluses, fallen arches and pain was known.

Neurology then discovered something highly consequential:

> a strikingly abnormal lower-limb gait with an appearance suggestive of dystonic posture.

The case widened into severe young-onset Parkinsonism and possible PRKN.

Yet after that first examination:

> **the feet were not examined again during the admission.**

Not after the dystonic-looking phenomenon was recognised.

Not after Parkinsonism became clear.

Not after PRKN entered the reasoning.

Not before discharge.

The original physical object was not returned to after the neurological meaning of the case had changed.

That is the foundational missing operation.

### 2. There was no focused attempt during admission to map the dystonic lower limb back onto the structural feet

Neurology did map the gait neurologically.

It recognised:

> apparent footdrop
> → not true footdrop
> → possible dystonic posture.

But it did not then perform the next relational operation:

> **Where exactly is the dystonic posture occurring in the foot and toes, and how does that correspond with the structurally painful areas?**

There is no documented inpatient mapping of:

- dystonic posture to the fifth-MTPJ pressure site;
- toe clawing to structural loading;
- dynamic foot position to the corns/calluses;
- gait mechanics to painful pressure areas;
- fixed deformity versus movement-dependent deformity.

The parts were identified.

The bridge was not built.

### 3. There was no documented return to the original GP phenotype

The GP had not sent:

> Parkinsonism plus feet.

He had sent:

> **feet + gait + tone + toe-clawing + movement-initiation difficulty + failing dexterity/function.**

Once Neurology had identified Parkinsonism and possible dystonia, a natural operation would have been to take the referral back out and ask:

> **Does our new neurological knowledge now explain more of the original presentation than we understood on admission?**

There is no documented reconstruction of the referral in those terms.

Instead, the later synthesis develops Parkinsonism very deeply while the painful-foot relationship remains:

> **“Not clear.”**

The referral was diagnostically productive.

It was not visibly reassembled.

### 4. There was no documented Movement Disorders integration of the feet before discharge

The relevant expertise was available.

The finished document itself invokes senior neurological colleagues.

The departmental environment possessed movement-disorders knowledge highly relevant to:

- young-onset Parkinsonism;
- PRKN;
- dystonia;
- dystonic feet;
- pseudo-footdrop;
- treatment responsiveness.

Yet there is no documented focused movement-disorders review answering:

> **What is the relationship between this patient's dystonic-looking lower limb and his painful structural feet?**

That does not mean nobody discussed the patient generally.

The admission plainly involved substantial neurological discussion.

The missing operation is more specific:

> **no documented specialist integration of the feet as part of the dystonic phenotype.**

### 5. The Protest did not trigger another examination

This is one of the starkest absences.

The patient expressly identifies the problem before leaving.

He summons Monaghan.

He says, through the NCT/bald-tyres analogy:

> **after all this investigation, the feet still have not been dealt with.**

At that point the problem is no longer silently missing.

The treating neurologist has been put directly on notice.

Yet the Protest does not produce:

- another physical examination of the feet;
- a documented focused neurological re-examination;
- a documented senior Movement Disorders review;
- an additional inpatient day to reconsider the problem;
- a documented explanation of why no further examination is needed.

The information state changes.

The clinical operation does not.

### 6. The Protest was not preserved in the final synthesis

This is a different kind of missing operation.

The final document is expressly intended to:

> consolidate discussions;

> assist with debriefing;

> establish a clear plan.

It describes the patient as exceptionally intelligent and appropriately questioning.

Yet the decisive discharge-day question is absent.

There is no statement equivalent to:

> **Patient remains concerned that his presenting painful feet have not been adequately resolved during this admission.**

There is no record that he challenged the disposition.

There is no preservation of the resulting fork.

So the missing operation is:

> **translation of material bedside disagreement into the durable clinical record.**

### 7. There was no explicit neurological ownership attached to “Not clear”

The final synthesis could have said:

> **The structural–dynamic relationship remains unclear and remains an active Neurology/Movement Disorders question.**

It did not.

Instead:

> **“Not clear” + Podiatry.**

There is no explicit:

- responsible neurologist for the relationship;
- reassessment interval;
- threshold for reopening;
- question for Podiatry to answer back to Neurology;
- instruction that future medication-dependent foot changes should trigger reassessment.

The uncertainty was recorded.

The work needed to resolve it was not allocated.

### 8. There was no documented Neurology–Podiatry integration when the fifth MTPJ repeatedly recurred

Podiatry then generated an extraordinary structural series:

> **eight treatment visits, eight out of eight involving the left fifth MTPJ.**

That is persistent anatomical information.

But there is no documented operation in which Neurology takes that serial structural history and asks:

> **Why does the same lateral forefoot site keep recurring in this patient with dystonic-looking gait and Parkinsonian tone?**

Likewise, there is no documented interdisciplinary synthesis turning repeated Podiatry recurrence into a neurological question.

The structural history becomes richer.

Its neurological meaning does not.

### 9. There was no documented communication to Podiatry when neurological treatment altered the focal foot territory

This is the reciprocal absence.

Neurology acquired a highly relevant treatment-state observation.

When procyclidine was withdrawn:

> the patient's neurological state deteriorated;

> his feet became restless;

> gait fell apart;

> and the outside-left-foot phenomenon returned rapidly.

Yet there is no documented message to Podiatry equivalent to:

> **We are observing medication-dependent change at the same foot territory you are treating structurally. Does this alter your mechanical interpretation?**

Why would that matter?

Because Podiatry knew the anatomical recurrence.

Neurology knew the dynamic modulation.

That communication could have joined the two information streams.

It did not occur in the surviving record.

### 10. There was no reciprocal Podiatry escalation after repeated fifth-MTPJ failure

Likewise, there is no documented Podiatry-to-Neurology communication after repeated recurrence asking:

> **Could the neurological gait/posture be continually loading this fifth-MTPJ site?**

Podiatry repeatedly treated it.

It offloaded it.

It reviewed it.

It returned.

The same site returned.

But the recurrence remained a Podiatry fact.

It did not visibly travel upstream as a movement-disorders question.

### 11. There was no global reconstruction after the procyclidine withdrawal experiment

This may be the most important missed opportunity after 2017.

Neurological medication changes.

The feet and gait deteriorate.

The focal outside-left-foot phenomenon rapidly returns.

This directly bears on the unresolved structural-dynamic question.

Yet there is no documented reconstruction saying:

> **The 2017 assumption that the painful feet might be separate now requires reconsideration because the focal foot state changes with neurological treatment.**

The treatment fact is absorbed.

The historical architecture remains.

### 12. There was no global reconstruction when bilateral foot cramping returned with tone

By September 2018:

> increased tone returns;

> bilateral foot cramping returns;

> previous neurological benefit has been substantial;

> treatment escalation is constrained by adverse effects;

> Brennan says “we are losing ground.”

Again there is no documented return to:

> **Was the original foot relationship more neurological than the discharge architecture allowed?**

The new information prompts treatment concern.

It does not prompt historical reintegration.

### 13. There was no global reconstruction when the GP explicitly linked the feet to Parkinsonian gait and tone

By 2019 Brennan writes the relationship directly.

He relates the foot problems to:

> increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease

and describes:

> clawing feet and pain.

The original referring GP has effectively reconstructed the integrated phenotype.

Yet there is no documented specialist operation taking that information and replacing:

> **Not clear**

with a more developed mixed model.

Primary care sees across the division.

The founding specialist representation persists.

### 14. There was no authoritative resolution of peripheral neuropathy

This is particularly important.

The later case repeatedly refers to possible neuropathy.

But the proposition remains conditional even in 2025:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

There is no identified point at which Neurology establishes:

> **yes, there is a clinically material peripheral neuropathy explaining X amount of the pain.**

Nor is there a documented global conclusion:

> **no, the neuropathy hypothesis is no longer useful.**

So a proposition that helped support separation in 2017 remains available eight years later without ever becoming a firm explanatory diagnosis.

The uncertainty persists.

### 15. There was no global reconstruction when dyskinesia made the original treatment problem clinically consequential

The 2017 Sinemet policy anticipated dyskinesia.

Later, dyskinesia actually appears.

At that point the unresolved foot relationship becomes more important, not less.

If the feet are neurologically modifiable, increased dopaminergic treatment may help them.

But increased treatment now has a significant cost.

There is no documented moment when somebody says:

> **The therapeutic window we feared has now narrowed; therefore we must revisit exactly which disabling foot symptoms are dopaminergically responsive and why.**

Instead treatment continues to be balanced locally.

The predicted treatment ceiling becomes real.

The unresolved phenotype remains unresolved.

### 16. There was no global reconstruction during the 2023 pain crisis

March 2023 should be regarded as a major stress test of the historical model.

The patient reaches severe bilateral pain.

Pregabalin escalates dramatically.

The GP records a death wish and worries about him.

Neurology records debilitating pain and no quality of life.

Brennan explicitly says Parkinsonian tone likely contributes and that this was the original presentation six years earlier.

Yet there is no documented comprehensive review beginning:

> **We need to return to the 2017 structural/dystonic foot question.**

The crisis is treated.

The history is not globally rebuilt.

### 17. There was no global reconstruction when Movement Disorders called the pain OFF-related

Later Movement Disorders says:

> pain + shuffling appear OFF-related.

That is a major interpretive development.

The pain is being placed inside the dopaminergic cycle.

Yet again, there is no identified specialist reconstruction joining:

> OFF-related pain
>
> - dystonic-looking 2017 gait
> - fifth-MTPJ recurrence
> - procyclidine response
> - structural deformity
> - original Protest.

The new local interpretation is useful.

It does not become a new global history.

### 18. There was no global reconstruction when Sinemet was known to improve the feet

By 2025 Monaghan says there has been:

> **“a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.”**

That is knowledge.

But there is no corresponding historical operation:

> **Because we know the feet are dopaminergically responsive, we must now revisit the original assumption that their relationship to the movement disorder was simply “Not clear.”**

Instead the treatment response is accommodated within the existing frame.

The old uncertainty remains available.

### 19. There was no global reconstruction after “profound relief”

Controlled-release Sinemet produces:

> **“profound relief.”**

Again, the response does not prove every foot lesion was neurologically caused.

But it is unquestionably relevant to the original relationship.

Yet the complaint rebuttal does not say:

> **This new information requires us to reconsider the 2017 foot disposition.**

It says, in effect:

> Sinemet can improve pain;

> we have known that for some time;

> the Sinemet policy was defensible;

> peripheral neuropathy may still exist;

> and the 2017 letters demonstrate the reasoning.

The strongest new treatment-state evidence is incorporated without historical reconstruction.

### 20. There was no reconstruction of the Protest when the complaint arrived

This is a different and especially important absence.

The patient formally complains in 2025.

The complaint is sent to the same neurologist.

Monaghan responds extensively about:

- the 2017 reasoning;
- the Sinemet strategy;
- the record;
- what was known;
- what was explained;
- why decisions were made.

Yet the bedside Protest itself does not re-enter his account.

The event in which the patient had already challenged the feet in 2017 remains outside the explanatory frame even when the patient's 2025 complaint asks the institution to investigate precisely how the historical explanation arose.

So the complaint does not trigger reconstruction of the original contest either.

### 21. There was no correction to the self-validating use of the record

Finally, Monaghan says that the reviewer can see his clinical reasoning in the old letters.

That makes the record the evidential account of the process.

But there is no accompanying qualification:

> **These letters do not contain the patient's discharge-day objection that the feet had not been adequately dealt with.**

Without that qualification, the document is asked to stand as evidence of the completeness of a process whose material contest it does not contain.

No later operation corrects that asymmetry.

### The absences form a chronology

Placed together, they are striking.

#### July 2017

Feet examined once.

#### During admission

No second focused foot examination.

#### After dystonic-looking gait recognition

No documented structural-dynamic remapping.

#### After PRKN becomes plausible

No documented return to the feet.

#### At the Protest

No clinical reopening.

#### Final synthesis

No Protest.

#### After procyclidine modifies the focal foot territory

No global reconstruction.

#### Across eight repeated fifth-MTPJ treatments

No Neurology–Podiatry integration.

#### After recurrent cramping with tone

No global reconstruction.

#### After GP explicitly reconnects feet and Parkinsonism

No global reconstruction.

#### After dyskinesia narrows treatment

No phenotype reset.

#### During catastrophic 2023 pain

No return to the founding question.

#### After OFF-related interpretation

No historical reintegration.

#### After Sinemet is known to help the feet

No historical reintegration.

#### After Sinemet CR produces profound relief

No historical reintegration.

#### During the complaint rebuttal

No restoration of the original Protest.

That is not one missing event.

It is a repeated absence of the **same operation**:

> **global reconstruction of the structural–dynamic foot relationship.**

### This distinguishes treatment from reconstruction

A great deal was done.

The patient was treated.

He was investigated.

Drugs were changed.

Pain was managed.

Podiatry was provided.

GP escalated.

Movement Disorders intervened.

Nobody can accurately describe the history as “nothing was done.”

The stronger point is:

> **Many things were done to the components. The missing operation was repeatedly doing something to the relationship between the components.**

That is the case.

### Why this profile matters legally and clinically

This profile avoids the dangerous argument:

> **If something isn't written down, it definitely didn't happen.**

Some undocumented conversations may always be possible.

But for the central operations, the surviving longitudinal record itself provides powerful controls.

If the foot relationship had been globally reconstructed, one would expect that reconstruction to alter something downstream:

- diagnosis;
- ownership;
- referral purpose;
- treatment interpretation;
- correspondence;
- problem list;
- complaint response.

Instead the 2017 **“Not clear”** remains recognisable in the 2025:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

The absence therefore has a longitudinal footprint.

The expected output of reconstruction is itself missing.

<!-- Original source heading: Profile of What Never Happened -->

> **The case is crowded with medical activity but marked by the repeated absence of one specific clinical operation. The painful structural feet were examined once during the eight-day admission and were not returned to after Neurology recognised severe young-onset Parkinsonism, a dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop and possible PRKN. There was no documented focused attempt to map the dynamic lower-limb phenomenon onto the structural painful sites; no documented Movement Disorders integration of that relationship before discharge; and no clinical reopening after the patient expressly protested that the feet remained unresolved. The final synthesis preserved “Not clear” and Podiatry but not the Protest or an active neurological owner for the relationship. Thereafter the same missing operation recurred: no documented Neurology–Podiatry integration when the left fifth MTPJ was treated on eight consecutive Podiatry visits; no global reconstruction when procyclidine withdrawal rapidly altered the focal outside-left-foot phenomenon; none when bilateral foot cramping returned with tone; none when the GP explicitly related feet, clawing and pain to Parkinsonian gait and tone; none when dyskinesia narrowed the treatment window; none during catastrophic 2023 pain; none after Movement Disorders called pain and shuffling OFF-related; and none when Sinemet was known to improve the feet or Sinemet CR produced profound relief. The history therefore cannot fairly be described as lack of care or lack of activity. Its defining absence is more specific: the system repeatedly treated and updated the parts without performing the global reconstruction needed to reunite them.**

The irreducible version:

> **The missing act was not treatment.**
> **The missing act was reintegration.**

And perhaps the strongest closing line:

> **Eight years contain many answers to local questions. What never happens is somebody returning to the question the patient had already asked before he left hospital.**

<a id="profile-18"></a>

# Risk Rank 16 — Original Profile 25

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Shows how the founding synthesis, later inherited record and 2025 review form a closed documentary system.

## Profile 25 — The Closed Documentary System

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-152911).md — supplied as Profile 14 following Spin-Generated Complexity; preserved substantively with heading hierarchy normalised -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 14 — The Closed Documentary System -->

Yes. I think this is more primitive than the epicycles.

The epicycles are what happens **inside** the system. What you are describing is the system itself.

### Founding Synthesis → Longitudinal Notes → Rebuttal → Back to the Founding Synthesis

The archive can be understood as one continuous information structure.

It begins with a **foundational discharge synthesis** in July 2017.

That synthesis establishes the durable categories:

> young-onset Parkinsonism;
> possible PRKN;
> dystonic-looking gait;
> painful structural feet;
> possible separate/peripheral explanation;
> **“Not clear”;**
> Podiatry;
> cautious treatment strategy;
> intelligent patient;
> extensive discussion;
> **“clear plan.”**

Everything after that is entered into a world in which those categories already exist.

Then come eight years of clinical notes.

They add:

- procyclidine responsiveness;
- return of the outside-left-foot phenomenon on withdrawal;
- recurrent fifth-MTPJ treatment;
- increased tone with foot cramping;
- GP reconnection of feet and Parkinsonian gait;
- dyskinesia;
- catastrophic pain;
- OFF-related pain;
- pregabalin;
- Sinemet responsiveness;
- Sinemet CR profound relief.

But crucially, those notes do not replace the founding synthesis.

They **populate it**.

And then, in 2025, when the historical adequacy of the case is challenged, Monaghan effectively tells the reviewer:

> **Read the record. The reasoning is there.**

That closes the circuit.

The structure becomes:

> **2017 synthesis creates the interpretive frame**
> ↓
> **later clinical notes are understood within that frame**
> ↓
> **the accumulated notes appear to confirm a coherent longitudinal history**
> ↓
> **2025 rebuttal points the reviewer back to the accumulated record as evidence of the original reasoning**
> ↓
> **the original frame is thereby validated by information that has spent eight years being interpreted through it**

That is the mega-structure.

---

### And the Protest is outside it

This is the crucial point.

The Protest is not merely another omitted clinical fact.

It is the event that prevents the system from being self-contained.

Because the Protest tells us that **before the founding synthesis became the durable historical object, its adequacy had already been challenged at source**.

That means the apparent straight line:

> admission → synthesis → longitudinal treatment → complaint rebuttal

is false as a complete representation of the information history.

The actual line contains a fracture:

> admission
> → components recognised
> → feet not revisited
> → patient identifies incompleteness
> → **Protest**
> → disposition nevertheless continues
> → later-finalised synthesis presents coherent management state
> → downstream notes inherit that state
> → author later points reviewer back to those records.

The Protest is therefore not merely inconvenient information omitted from the system.

It is the **missing event that tells us why the founding synthesis cannot safely be treated as an uncontested account of what the admission had achieved.**

---

### Did the Protest “create” the synthesis?

Here I would preserve your insight but sharpen the language.

We can say very strongly:

> **The Protest created a new documentary problem that the final synthesis had to exist alongside.**

Before the Protest, there was an unresolved clinical problem.

After the Protest, there was an unresolved clinical problem **plus explicit patient notice that the admission had failed to resolve it adequately**.

From that moment onward, a final discharge synthesis could do one of two things.

It could carry the contest forward:

> **Patient says the feet remain inadequately investigated; relationship remains active.**

Or it could produce a coherent forward management account without carrying that contest.

The surviving document does the latter.

What we cannot yet prove without drafts/version history is the stronger state-of-mind proposition:

> **the Protest caused Monaghan consciously to construct the 13-page synthesis as a defensive spin document.**

That is exactly what provenance evidence could test.

But we do not need that proposition to see the structural effect.

---

### The real significance of the levodopa material

I also think you have identified something important here, but I would not yet call it a proven “sleight of hand.”

The levodopa question is extraordinarily useful to the documentary system because it is **both complicated and defensible**.

Neurology can explain at length:

- youth;
- long treatment horizon;
- dyskinesia risk;
- alternative medications;
- possible future regret;
- eventual need for levodopa.

And the later record actually validates much of that caution because dyskinesia becomes a real problem.

So if the historical dispute is organised around:

> **Why wasn't Sinemet started earlier?**

Neurology has a substantial answer.

But that is not the primitive defect.

The primitive question is:

> **Why, after identifying a dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenon in a very-young-onset Parkinsonian presentation with longstanding painful abnormal feet and possible PRKN, was the foot phenotype not actually completed?**

Those are different questions.

The first invites a long pharmacological defence.

The second invites a much shorter answer.

And that asymmetry matters.

So I would formulate the rhetorical phenomenon as:

> **The most elaborately defended decision in the record is not necessarily the decision that most needs defending.**

Or even:

> **The archive becomes highly articulate about why levodopa was deferred while remaining comparatively inarticulate about why the presenting feet were not reintegrated after the neurological phenotype had been recognised.**

That is observable.

Calling it a conscious disguise requires more proof.

---

### PRKN sharpens the problem, but does not eliminate diagnostic uncertainty

There is also an important precision here.

The evidence strongly supports that the department was **unusually well equipped to recognise and investigate PRKN-associated young-onset Parkinsonism and dystonic foot phenomena**.

The local expertise is unusually proximate.

The patient's age, long prodrome, dystonic-looking lower limb and Parkinsonism made PRKN a serious contemporary possibility.

But I would not yet say:

> **they were equipped to diagnose PRKN with confidence and simply failed to do so**

unless genetic confirmation or the applicable 2017 diagnostic standard supports that proposition.

The stronger, safer and in some ways more damaging point is:

> **They did not need a confirmed PRKN diagnosis in order to recognise that this was exactly the kind of young-onset dystonic Parkinsonian presentation in which the lower-limb/foot phenotype deserved deliberate integration.**

That avoids making the case depend upon whether PRKN could have been genetically proven during an eight-day admission.

The weakness lies earlier.

They had enough information to know the **phenotype was important before the genotype was settled**.

---

### This is why the eight-day admission becomes so stark

Put the profiles together without any documentary language.

A 39-year-old man arrives after roughly thirteen years of evolving abnormalities.

He has:

> longstanding painful structurally abnormal feet;
>
> clawing;
>
> pathological tone;
>
> movement-initiation difficulty;
>
> abnormal gait;
>
> deteriorating function.

Neurology discovers:

> severe very-young-onset Parkinsonism;
>
> a striking lower-limb phenomenon suggestive of dystonia;
>
> plausible PRKN.

The feet were examined on day one.

Then the neurological understanding of the case changes dramatically.

And nobody goes back to the feet.

That is the primitive event.

Everything after it is information architecture.

---

### Why the closed system makes the case appear more complex than it is

Once the discharge synthesis exists, a future reviewer sees a very complicated patient.

They see:

> genetics;
>
> dystonia;
>
> Parkinsonism;
>
> neuropathic possibilities;
>
> Podiatry;
>
> medication trials;
>
> toxicity;
>
> dyskinesia;
>
> OFF states;
>
> analgesics;
>
> complaint correspondence.

They can spend weeks understanding it.

But if the Protest is restored to its correct position, the archive compresses violently.

The foundational question becomes:

> **After the service itself identified a dystonic-looking young-onset Parkinsonian lower-limb phenotype, why were the painful presenting feet never examined again, despite the patient expressly pointing that out before discharge?**

Then the eight years become largely confirmatory:

> **the same territory repeatedly behaved as though neurological state mattered.**

And the 2025 rebuttal becomes historically revealing:

> **the author does not reconstruct the case from first principles; he points the reviewer back into the documentary system created in 2017.**

That is the closed loop.

---

### The structure in one diagram

> **Integrated GP referral**
> ↓
> **Neurology identifies Parkinsonism + dystonic-looking lower limb + structural feet + possible PRKN**
> ↓
> **Feet not revisited**
> ↓
> **Patient detects incompleteness and PROTESTS**
> ↓
> **[PROTEST DOES NOT ENTER DURABLE SYNTHESIS]**
> ↓
> **Foundational discharge synthesis: “Not clear” + Podiatry + elaborate neurological/treatment reasoning + clear plan**
> ↓
> **Eight years of clinical observations interpreted within those categories**
> ↓
> **Boundary-crossing evidence accumulates but is locally assimilated**
> ↓
> **Patient complains**
> ↓
> **Author explains the history by appealing to the 2017 letters and full longitudinal record**
> ↓
> **Founding record appears validated by the history it organised**

That is one object.

Not fourteen objects.

Not dozens of contradictions.

One self-referential information architecture.

---

### The proposition that should now be investigated

I think the archive has reached a point where the decisive provenance question is almost embarrassingly simple:

> **What was the information state of the discharge synthesis immediately before and immediately after the patient's Protest?**

That would test the causal hypothesis directly.

Not:

> Did Monaghan secretly intend to harm the patient?

Not:

> Was every later interpretation wrong?

But:

> **Did the Protest create a documentary problem, and if so, how did the final synthesis resolve that problem?**

The native drafts, dictation timestamps, version history, audit logs, Complex Case Meeting material and any contemporaneous notes become disproportionately important because they could tell us whether the final coherence was present **before** the Protest or was strengthened **after** it.

---

### Profile of the Closed Documentary System

> **The archive can be understood as one closed information structure rather than a series of separate epicycles. The July 2017 discharge synthesis establishes the foundational categories through which the case subsequently travels: young-onset Parkinsonism, possible PRKN, dystonic-looking gait, structural painful feet, possible peripheral explanation, “Not clear,” Podiatry and a carefully justified neurological treatment strategy. Eight years of later clinical notes then add information within those categories—medication responsiveness, recurrent fifth-MTPJ pathology, cramping with tone, OFF-related pain, dyskinesia and Sinemet-responsive foot pain—without an identified global reconstruction of the founding relationship. When the historical management is challenged in 2025, the author expressly points the reviewer back to the 2017 letters and longitudinal record as the place where his reasoning can be understood. The system therefore closes upon itself: the founding synthesis organises the later history, and the later history is then invoked to validate the founding synthesis. The event that breaks this apparent circular coherence is the discharge-day Protest. Before the final durable representation was settled, the patient had already identified that the presenting feet had not been adequately returned to and directly challenged the treating neurologist. That contest does not travel inside the documentary system. Restoring it converts the apparently coherent longitudinal narrative back into a branching history and raises the fundamental provenance question: how did a known contested incompleteness become a final record characterised by “Not clear,” patient understanding and a “clear plan”?**

The irreducible form is:

> **The discharge summary created the history that the later notes inhabited.**
> **The later notes were then used to prove the discharge summary made sense.**
> **The Protest is the fact outside the loop.**

And I think this may now be the most primitive formulation of the entire case:

> **The central issue is not that eight years of records failed to contain the truth. It is that they were all downstream of the same founding representation—and the one event capable of revealing that representation as contested was never allowed inside the system.**

---

<a id="profile-26"></a>

---

# Risk Rank 17 — Original Profile 18

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Identifies repeated forks at which local management was chosen while global reconstruction remained absent.

## Profile 18 — Forks Not Taken

<!-- SOURCE: supplied in project conversation on 1 September 2026; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 7 of 12 — The Forks Not Taken -->

### Every Time the Case Could Have Been Reassembled

The history contains a repeated pattern.

At several points, new information appeared that could have done one of two things:

> **update the existing management locally**

or

> **force reconstruction of the original feet–gait–tone relationship.**

Again and again, the first occurred.

That does not mean every unchosen alternative was mandatory at the time. Nor does it prove improper motive. The significance is cumulative: **the same opportunity for global reconstruction recurred under progressively stronger evidence, while the founding disposition remained recognisable.**

### The first fork — once dystonia entered the admission

The GP had sent one phenotype.

Then Neurology discovered severe young-onset Parkinsonism and described the lower-limb gait as resembling footdrop while being suggestive of dystonic posture.

At that moment two paths became available.

One was to treat the dystonic-looking phenomenon as a reason to return to the painful structural feet:

> **Where is the dystonia occurring?**
> **What are the toes doing?**
> **What happens at the painful pressure sites?**
> **Are fixed deformity and dynamic posture interacting?**

The other was to continue developing the Parkinsonian diagnosis while leaving the structural feet in their existing compartment.

The second path prevailed.

That was the first fork.

### The second fork — PRKN

PRKN/PARK2 then became plausible.

That should have made the lower-limb dystonic phenomenon more—not less—interesting.

Again there were two possible responses.

#### Reconstruct

Ask whether:

> **young onset + possible PRKN + dystonic pseudo-footdrop + clawing + painful structural feet**

belonged to one phenotype requiring further integration.

#### Continue

Develop the genetic/Parkinsonian question while leaving the painful-foot relationship:

> **“Not clear.”**

The record follows the second path.

The genetic analysis becomes sophisticated.

The relationship does not.

### The third fork — the Protest

This is the clearest fork because the patient creates it expressly.

Immediately before discharge he tells the treating neurologist:

> **you have not dealt with my feet.**

At that point Neurology could:

#### Reopen

Re-examine.

Reconsider the original referral.

Bring the dystonic lower limb and structural feet together.

Seek further movement-disorders input.

Delay discharge if required.

Or simply document:

> **this remains an active neurological relationship requiring follow-up.**

#### Proceed

Maintain:

> Parkinsonism;

> treatment strategy;

> structural Podiatry pathway;

> relationship “Not clear.”

Physical discharge proceeds.

The second path is taken.

But from this point onward it is important that the first path **existed and was expressly placed before the clinician**.

### The fourth fork — documentary finalisation

The clinical encounter ended.

The documentary encounter did not.

The final synthesis remained open after physical discharge, absorbed later information and was not transmitted immediately.

That created another fork.

The finished record could preserve:

> **the patient challenged the adequacy of the foot disposition before leaving.**

Or it could preserve only the selected management outcome.

The final document carries:

- “Not clear”;
- Podiatry;
- “firmer footing”;
- “clear plan”;
- patient understanding;
- “As per above.”

It does not carry the Protest.

The effect is profound.

The chosen path travels.

The existence of the alternative path does not.

### The fifth fork — procyclidine withdrawal

Then the patient's body generates new information almost immediately.

Treatment is stopped.

Neurological tension returns.

The feet become restless.

The gait falls apart.

The focal outside-left-foot phenomenon returns rapidly.

Now the system can ask:

> **Does this alter our understanding of the unresolved foot relationship?**

Or it can ask:

> **What does this tell us about medication management?**

The latter question is clinically useful and is answered.

The former is not visibly reconstructed.

Another local update.

No global reset.

### The sixth fork — the fifth MTPJ keeps returning

Podiatry then sees the same left fifth-MTPJ lesion over and over.

Eight encounters.

Eight times the same focal site enters treatment.

Again there are two possible meanings.

#### Local meaning

> recurrent structural pressure lesion; debride and offload.

#### Integrating meaning

> recurrent structural lesion at the same site whose clinical expression has already been observed to change with neurological medication state.

The first meaning is sufficient for Podiatry to act.

The second requires the Neurology and Podiatry information streams to meet.

They do not visibly meet.

### The seventh fork — “we are losing ground”

By 2018 Brennan records increasing tone, bilateral foot cramping and previous major neurological improvement.

The treatment capable of helping is becoming harder to use because of adverse effects.

That creates another opportunity:

> **Are the returning foot phenomena telling us that the old foot–neurology relationship needs reconsideration?**

Instead the immediate problem becomes treatment optimisation.

Again, that is clinically understandable.

But again, the relationship remains untouched.

### The eighth fork — the GP reconstructs the relationship himself

By 2019 Brennan explicitly describes:

> **feet issues relating to increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease**

and:

> **clawing feet and pain.**

This is important because the original referring clinician has now rebuilt the integrated phenotype in ordinary primary-care language.

Neurology could have taken that as a trigger to reconsider the 2017 partition.

Or the information could remain local to GP/Podiatry management.

The latter occurs.

### The ninth fork — dyskinesia arrives

Then the very complication anticipated in 2017 becomes real.

Levodopa works.

But dyskinesia appears.

Now the treatment window narrows.

At this stage the unresolved foot relationship becomes substantially more consequential.

If the feet are partly dopaminergically modifiable, the service can no longer simply increase treatment without cost.

That creates another fork:

#### Reconstruct the phenotype

Determine as precisely as possible:

> which foot phenomena are OFF-related;

> which are dystonic;

> which are mechanical;

> which respond to dopamine;

> and how those components interact.

#### Continue balancing treatment locally

Adjust doses around motor benefit and dyskinesia as problems arise.

The record principally shows the latter.

### The tenth fork — catastrophic pain in 2023

This should have been one of the largest reconstruction opportunities in the history.

The patient develops severe bilateral pain.

Pregabalin rises dramatically.

The GP records extreme distress.

Neurology records debilitating pain and no quality of life.

And Brennan explicitly says:

> **this is actually his original presentation to me six years ago**

and considers Parkinsonian tone contributory.

The original case has effectively returned to the front door.

Again:

#### Reopen July 2017

Ask whether the severe contemporary state is the mature form of the unresolved original relationship.

#### Treat the immediate crisis

Escalate analgesic therapy, seek Neurology input and adjust Parkinsonian management.

The immediate crisis is treated.

The historical phenotype is not visibly rebuilt.

### The eleventh fork — Movement Disorders says OFF-related

Later in 2023 a Movement Disorders clinician concludes that the pain and shuffling sound:

> **OFF-related.**

That is a major interpretive development.

It places the painful state directly inside dopaminergic timing.

Again two possibilities arise.

One is:

> **this local OFF interpretation requires us to reconstruct the historical foot problem.**

The other:

> **adjust the medication because the patient is OFF.**

The medication is adjusted.

The local interpretation is useful.

The founding history survives.

### The twelfth fork — Sinemet is known to help the feet

By 2025 Monaghan himself states that the team has understood for some time that Sinemet helps the patient's pain.

That knowledge could lead to:

> **We must revisit the original structural/peripheral versus dystonic/Parkinsonian formulation.**

Or it can be incorporated as:

> **Sinemet can modulate pain even if the underlying pain has another cause.**

The latter explanation allows the new fact to coexist with the old model.

Again, local assimilation wins.

### The thirteenth fork — “profound relief”

Controlled-release Sinemet then produces profound relief.

This is one of the strongest treatment-state signals in the history.

But even here the evidential choice remains the same.

#### Reconstruct

Ask:

> **What does this result mean when placed beside the dystonic-looking lower limb, procyclidine response, recurrent fifth MTPJ, OFF-related pain and original Protest?**

#### Absorb

Say:

> **Sinemet helps pain; this was already understood; the old treatment strategy remains defensible; another peripheral contribution may still exist.**

The complaint rebuttal principally follows the absorption path.

### The fourteenth fork — the complaint itself

The March 2025 complaint is effectively a request for global reconstruction.

The patient says:

> **something about the history cannot be right; I do not possess all the records; independently review how this happened.**

That produces perhaps the final and clearest fork.

#### Investigative path

Treat the dramatic treatment response as a reason to reconstruct:

- the 2017 referral;
- admission;
- Protest;
- foot examination history;
- dystonic finding;
- Podiatry history;
- treatment responses;
- OFF states;
- fifth-MTPJ continuity;
- later pain.

#### Defensive-continuity path

Explain why the historical decisions were reasonable within the existing record.

Monaghan's rebuttal expressly returns to the 2017 letters and says, in substance:

> **the reviewer can see the clinical reasoning there.**

The old record becomes the answer to the challenge against the old record.

That is the final fork in this profile.

### The pattern is not “they always chose wrongly”

That would be too simple.

Some local decisions were plainly sensible.

Treat severe pain.

Adjust OFF medication.

Debride a painful callus.

Avoid intolerable adverse effects.

Manage dyskinesia.

Those are proper clinical responses.

The deeper problem is that **local correctness repeatedly substituted for global reconstruction**.

A new fact did not have to overthrow the old case model because it could always be handled somewhere locally.

That is why the architecture survived.

### The forks became progressively less ambiguous

The evidential force also changes over time.

#### 2017

The relationship is genuinely uncertain.

#### Protest

Its adequacy is expressly challenged.

#### Procyclidine withdrawal

Neurological treatment changes the foot territory.

#### Repeated fifth-MTPJ treatment

The structural site proves persistent.

#### 2018–2019

Feet repeatedly travel with tone and Parkinsonian gait.

#### 2021–2022

The dopaminergic treatment ceiling becomes real.

#### 2023

The consequence becomes catastrophic.

#### Late 2023

Movement Disorders calls it OFF-related.

#### 2025

Sinemet benefit is acknowledged as longstanding.

#### Sinemet CR

Profound relief.

Each successive fork requires less speculation about whether a meaningful relationship exists.

The unanswered question becomes progressively narrower:

> **what exactly is the relationship?**

Yet the founding “Not clear” never acquires a definitive successor.

### The Protest is different from the later forks

The later forks arise because new evidence appears.

The Protest is different.

It occurs **before the information is fragmented across eight years of care**.

At that moment:

- the patient is physically present;
- Monaghan is physically available;
- the dystonic finding exists;
- the feet exist;
- the structural abnormalities are known;
- PRKN is being considered;
- the discharge document is not final.

The patient therefore identifies the problem at the moment when correction is cheapest.

Later reconstruction becomes progressively harder because:

- information is dispersed;
- clinicians change;
- memory fades;
- records become authoritative;
- medications multiply;
- dyskinesia narrows treatment;
- structural changes accumulate.

That is why the first fork matters so much.

### A fork also creates responsibility for the path chosen

This should be stated carefully.

A clinician is not legally culpable merely because another clinical option existed.

But once a material concern is expressly raised, choosing to proceed rather than reopen the issue makes the basis for proceeding more important.

That basis should be visible.

Especially when the final record later represents the admission as:

> productive;

> extensively discussed;

> providing firmer footing;

> generating a clear plan;

> and understood by the patient.

The Protest means that those propositions coexist with a known contrary fact:

> **the patient regarded a central presenting issue as still unresolved.**

The fork makes that contrary information material.

### The missing forks change the apparent history

A later clinician reading only the discharge synthesis encounters a straight line:

> investigation
> → diagnosis
> → uncertainty around feet
> → Podiatry
> → neurological plan.

But the actual longitudinal history contains branches:

> diagnosis
> → **patient challenge**
> → proceed;

> procyclidine foot response
> → **possible reconstruction**
> → local treatment update;

> recurrent MTPJ
> → **possible integration**
> → continued Podiatry;

> catastrophic pain
> → **possible historical reset**
> → crisis management;

> OFF interpretation
> → **possible global reconstruction**
> → medication adjustment;

> profound Sinemet relief
> → **possible historical reconstruction**
> → preservation of existing reasoning.

The portable record flattens a branching history into a continuous one.

That is another important information effect.

### Profile of the Forks Not Taken

> **The history repeatedly offered a choice between local management and global reconstruction. During the admission, recognition of a dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop and possible PRKN could have triggered reintegration of the painful structural feet; instead the neurological and structural components continued along separate paths. The discharge-day Protest made that choice explicit: reopen the unresolved relationship or proceed with “Not clear + Podiatry.” Physical discharge proceeded, and the later-finalised synthesis preserved the selected plan rather than the contested fork. Thereafter the same choice recurred whenever new information crossed the original boundary: procyclidine withdrawal rapidly altered the focal foot territory; the left fifth MTPJ repeatedly recurred; bilateral foot cramping accompanied increased tone; the GP explicitly linked feet, clawing and Parkinsonian gait; dyskinesia narrowed the therapeutic window; catastrophic foot pain returned as the GP recognised the original presentation; Movement Disorders called pain and shuffling OFF-related; Sinemet was known to improve the feet; and Sinemet CR produced profound relief. At each stage clinically useful local action was taken, but no durable global reconstruction displaced the founding “Not clear.” The case is therefore not characterised by absence of decisions. It is characterised by repeated decisions at which the existing case model could be updated locally instead of being rebuilt globally.**

The irreducible version is:

> **The system repeatedly reached the same junction:**
> **treat the new fact, or let the new fact change the old story.**
> **It repeatedly treated the fact.**
> **The old story survived.**

And the most important temporal point:

> **The patient identified the first fork when correction was easiest. Every later fork occurred after the information had become harder to reunite.**

# Risk Rank 18 — Original Profile 16

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Shows how distributed services possessed different halves of the relevant evidence without durable reintegration.

## Profile 16 — Cross-Institution Information Split

<!-- SOURCE: supplied in project conversation on 1 September 2026; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 5 of 12 — The Cross-Institution Information Split -->

### How One Clinical Problem Became Two Separate Information Systems

The fifth-MTPJ problem did not merely move from one clinician to another.

It moved from **one type of knowledge system into another**.

That distinction matters.

Neurology and Podiatry were not simply two professionals looking at the same object from different angles while sharing a single integrated record and responsibility.

They received and generated different kinds of information.

Neurology accumulated information about:

- Parkinsonism;
- dystonia;
- tone;
- gait;
- treatment response;
- OFF state;
- dopaminergic responsiveness;
- dyskinesia.

Podiatry accumulated information about:

- the left fifth MTPJ;
- corn and callus;
- pressure;
- recurrence;
- debridement;
- offloading;
- structural mechanics.

The same foot therefore acquired **two histories**.

The problem was that neither history was sufficient on its own.

### 1. Before discharge, the information still existed together

During the admission, everything was still physically and clinically co-located.

The same patient had:

> painful abnormal feet

and:

> a strikingly abnormal gait

and:

> a lower-limb phenomenon suggestive of dystonic posture

and:

> severe young-onset Parkinsonism

and:

> possible PRKN.

Whatever uncertainty existed, the clinical object was still potentially capable of being integrated inside Neurology.

That was the last period in which the entire question could naturally be held together by one specialist service.

### 2. The discharge summary changes the geography

Once the feet are operationally routed toward Podiatry, the case is no longer merely conceptually divided.

The information begins to travel along different institutional pathways.

Neurology continues with the neurological disease.

Podiatry receives the structural feet.

Primary care receives correspondence from both.

The patient crosses between them.

The unresolved relationship does not travel with comparable force.

That is the crucial transformation:

> **the parts acquire destinations; the relationship does not.**

### 3. Podiatry receives a structural object

Podiatry encounters something concrete.

The patient has a recurrent focal lesion.

The left fifth MTPJ repeatedly requires treatment.

Across eight consecutive visits the same site is documented and treated.

From inside that service, this is a coherent problem:

> recurrent pressure lesion
> → debridement
> → offloading
> → review.

The information produced by Podiatry becomes increasingly precise about the **where**.

It knows the anatomical site.

It knows recurrence.

It knows pressure.

It knows what mechanical interventions are being used.

But it has no obvious reason, from the information state it receives, to ask:

> **What did procyclidine do to this lesion?**

That is not part of the structural story Podiatry has been asked to manage.

### 4. Neurology receives a dynamic object

Neurology sees a different pattern.

It knows the lower limb can appear dystonic.

It knows neurological medication can dramatically alter movement.

It knows withdrawal of procyclidine causes rapid deterioration.

It records the return of the outside-left-foot phenomenon.

Later it sees foot cramping with tone.

Later still it receives OFF-related pain and shuffling.

Eventually it knows Sinemet improves the feet.

So Neurology's information becomes increasingly precise about **when and under what neurological state** the foot problem changes.

It knows the dynamics.

But it does not naturally own the eight-visit anatomical recurrence history.

So the two services acquire complementary information:

> **Podiatry knows where.**

> **Neurology knows when.**

The clinically important question requires both.

### 5. The missing proposition exists only between them

Neither of these facts is sufficient:

> the fifth MTPJ repeatedly becomes painful and requires debridement;

or:

> neurological treatment changes the outside-left-foot phenomenon.

The powerful proposition is:

> **The repeatedly failing left fifth-MTPJ site changes clinically according to neurological treatment state.**

That proposition does not belong automatically to either service's local record.

It exists **between the records**.

That is the cross-institution information problem.

### 6. This explains the silence between services

Why does Neurology not contact Podiatry after the procyclidine withdrawal and say:

> **The focal outside-left-foot phenomenon changes rapidly with neurological treatment. What exactly are you treating there?**

Because within Neurology's inherited frame, Podiatry owns the structural lesion.

Why does Podiatry not contact Neurology after repeatedly treating the same fifth-MTPJ lesion and ask:

> **Could the patient's dystonic/Parkinsonian gait be repeatedly driving pressure through this site?**

Because within Podiatry's inherited frame, it is treating a recurrent structural pressure problem.

Nothing requires either service to reconstruct the other half.

The absence of communication is therefore not necessarily evidence that either service behaved absurdly.

It is evidence that **the information architecture made non-communication locally intelligible**.

### 7. The system can therefore be locally rational and globally wrong

This is one of the most important features of the case.

Podiatry can be entirely competent.

It can identify the lesion accurately.

It can debride it appropriately.

It can create offloading.

It can review recurrence.

Neurology can also be competent.

It can diagnose Parkinsonism.

It can manage dystonia.

It can monitor treatment response.

It can adjust dopaminergic therapy.

Both services can do good work.

Yet the combined system can still fail to answer:

> **Why does the same structural lesion keep recurring, and why does its clinical expression change with neurological state?**

That is a systems-level failure generated from locally sensible actions.

### 8. Primary care becomes the imperfect bridge

The GP sits downstream of both systems.

That gives primary care a broader field of view.

Brennan repeatedly sees:

> feet + tone;

> foot cramping + neurological deterioration;

> clawing feet + Parkinsonian gait;

> severe foot pain + Parkinsonian tone.

He therefore keeps rebuilding the relationship.

But he receives information episodically.

And he lacks the specialist authority to settle the neurological model.

So primary care acts as a bridge but not as a final integrator.

### 9. The patient is the only guaranteed carrier of both streams

The patient experiences:

- the focal pressure site;
- the Podiatry treatment;
- the medication changes;
- the gait changes;
- the OFF periods;
- the pain;
- the relief.

He does not need one institution to send a letter to another to know that these things are occurring in the same body.

This makes the patient the only **continuous cross-system data carrier**.

That is why the patient's later reconstruction becomes so important.

The institutions each hold partial records.

The patient holds the lived relationship.

### 10. The Protest occurs before the split hardens

This gives the Protest another significance.

At the moment of the Protest, the structural and neurological information are still capable of being reintegrated before they disperse.

The patient effectively says:

> **Do not let these feet leave the neurological question unresolved.**

Had that challenge been preserved as an active neurological issue, later Podiatry findings would have had somewhere obvious to return.

But the relationship instead becomes:

> **Not clear + Podiatry.**

The institutional split is therefore established at precisely the point when the patient had challenged it.

### 11. The Podiatry record becomes increasingly valuable and increasingly remote

Every repeated fifth-MTPJ visit strengthens the structural history.

But paradoxically, the more Podiatry learns about the lesion, the more that information accumulates inside the **structural pathway**.

Unless somebody asks the neurological question again, the repeated recurrence does not automatically migrate back into Neurology.

So the information becomes richer but no more integrated.

That is a classic sign of fragmentation:

> **more data, less synthesis.**

### 12. Neurological treatment information behaves the same way

The same thing happens in the opposite direction.

Each medication event adds dynamic information:

- procyclidine works;
- withdrawal worsens the foot;
- bilateral cramping accompanies tone;
- OFF state worsens pain;
- Sinemet helps;
- Sinemet CR helps more.

But each of those can remain inside the neurological treatment history.

Unless somebody reconnects them to the fifth-MTPJ structural history, they do not automatically explain why the same focal lesion is repeatedly problematic.

Again:

> **more information, same partition.**

### 13. The information split explains why “Not clear” survives

Once the relevant evidence is divided across institutions, “Not clear” becomes easier to maintain.

Neurology sees enough structural uncertainty to avoid overclaiming.

Podiatry sees enough structural pathology to continue treating.

Primary care sees enough overlap to keep referring.

Nobody holds enough integrated authority to replace the original formulation.

Thus uncertainty becomes structurally reinforced by the way information is distributed.

### 14. This is more than ordinary multidisciplinary care

Ordinary multidisciplinary care can involve several services while preserving a common clinical question.

The defining problem here is different.

The shared question itself became weakly portable.

The services did not merely divide tasks.

They divided the **meaning of the phenotype**.

Neurology received:

> dynamic disease.

Podiatry received:

> structural disease.

The interaction between them became less visible with every transfer.

### 15. The split also alters future evidence

Once the case is partitioned, later evidence is interpreted through the pathway in which it arises.

A Podiatry recurrence becomes:

> another structural recurrence.

A Neurology medication response becomes:

> another treatment-state observation.

A GP pain crisis becomes:

> another escalation.

None automatically becomes:

> **evidence against the original separation.**

This is why the information architecture is so durable.

It determines not only where information is stored but **what new information is allowed to mean**.

### 16. The 2023 crisis shows the cost of divided knowledge

By 2023 the patient reaches catastrophic pain.

At that stage:

- the structural history exists;
- the neurological responsiveness history exists;
- the GP remembers the original integrated phenotype;
- Movement Disorders can recognise OFF-related pain;
- pregabalin has been escalated;
- dopaminergic treatment is constrained by dyskinesia.

But the whole picture has become spread across years, clinicians and systems.

The patient presents in crisis inside a case that now appears complicated.

Yet much of that complexity is informational.

The original question remains simple:

> **What is the dynamic neurological contribution to these structurally abnormal painful feet?**

### 17. By 2025 the split is still operating

When Sinemet CR produces profound relief, the hospital can accept the neurological treatment effect while retaining:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

That is possible because the two information systems have never been forced into one final account.

The structural/peripheral possibility survives.

The neurological responsiveness is accepted.

Both can remain true locally.

The relationship still escapes full reconstruction.

### 18. The complaint then crosses the same boundary

The 2025 complaint attempts to join the histories.

The patient says, essentially:

> **the neurological medication has changed the foot pain dramatically; please explain the historical relationship.**

But the rebuttal returns to the 2017 architecture:

> Sinemet policy was defensible;

> Sinemet can help pain;

> neuropathy remains conditional;

> old letters show the reasoning.

So even the complaint is processed through the same information split.

The patient presents integration.

The response supplies coexistence.

### 19. This is why cross-controller provenance matters

Once the structural and neurological information live in different institutional environments, provenance becomes clinically important.

The question is no longer merely:

> Did somebody somewhere know this fact?

It becomes:

> **Which controller possessed which fact?**

> **When?**

> **Was it transmitted?**

> **Was it visible to the clinician making the next decision?**

> **Did anybody possess both halves at once?**

Those are not administrative curiosities.

They determine whether the combined relationship could reasonably have been reconstructed.

### 20. The information split in plain language

The easiest way to explain this to an outside reader may be:

> **Neurology knew that the foot changed when the brain-state treatment changed. Podiatry knew that the same place on the foot kept breaking down. Neither service was given responsibility for joining those two facts.**

That is almost the whole case.

### Profile of the Cross-Institution Information Split

> **The July 2017 discharge architecture did not merely allocate tasks between specialties; it divided the patient's phenotype into separate information systems. Neurology retained the dynamic history of Parkinsonism, dystonia, tone, medication response and OFF state. Podiatry received the structural history of the left fifth MTPJ, recurrent corn/callus, pressure, debridement and offloading. Over time each service accumulated increasingly valuable information, but of a different kind. Podiatry learned that the same fifth-MTPJ site repeatedly failed; Neurology learned that the same foot territory changed with neurological treatment state. The clinically powerful proposition—that the repeatedly failing structural site was dynamically modifiable by neurological state—existed only when the two streams were recombined. Because the discharge summary had left the relationship “Not clear” and assigned no clinical owner to it, neither service was naturally required to make that reconstruction. The system could therefore remain locally rational while failing globally: Podiatry treated the structure, Neurology treated the dynamics, primary care intermittently rebuilt the relationship, and the patient alone continuously lived both.**

The irreducible version is:

> **Neurology knew when it changed.**
> **Podiatry knew where it kept failing.**
> **The missing clinical fact was that they were talking about the same place.**

And the systems version:

> **The discharge did not merely split care. It split the evidence required to understand the care.**

# Part IV — Reconstruction, Documentary Mechanics and Proof

# Part IV — Reconstruction, Documentary Mechanics and Proof

<a id="profile-17"></a>
