# Risk Rank 19 — Original Profile 31

**Severity:** VERY SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Compresses eight days of founding decisions into eight years of downstream consequences and repeated uncorrected updating opportunities.

## Profile 31 — Eight Days Into Eight Years

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-115718).md -->

<!-- Original source heading: Eight Days Into Eight Years -->

### Profile of the Life Produced by the July 2017 Management State

The patient entered University Hospital Galway on **3 July 2017**.

He left physically on **11 July 2017**.

Those eight days did not create his disease.

They did not create thirteen years of preceding foot pathology, abnormal gait, pathological tension or progressive neurological dysfunction.

They did not create Parkinsonism.

They did not create dystonia.

They did not create structural foot disease.

What those eight days did was something different.

> **They created the information and management state through which the patient's disease would subsequently be understood and treated.**

That distinction is fundamental.

The patient entered hospital with one unresolved problem:

> **painful abnormal feet + gait + tone + toe-clawing + movement difficulty + failing function.**

He emerged with a highly developed neurological diagnosis and treatment architecture, but with the relationship of the feet to that neurological disorder still:

> **“Not clear.”**

Podiatry became the practical destination of the structural feet.

Neurology retained the Parkinsonian syndrome.

Dopaminergic replacement was to be approached with exceptional caution because of the patient's youth and anticipated dyskinesia risk.

The relationship between those territories did not receive an equally durable owner.

The following eight years are the life lived inside that arrangement.

### The eight days

During the admission, Neurology did many things impressively.

It recognised severe very-young-onset Parkinsonism.

It recognised that the gait looked like footdrop while actually being:

> **“somewhat suggestive of a dystonic posture — NOT FOOT DROP.”**

It considered unusually young onset, symmetry, genetics and PRKN/PARK2.

It obtained extensive imaging and laboratory investigation.

It video-recorded the neurological examination.

It discussed the case as unusual and complex.

It sought senior neurological input.

It planned long-term treatment.

It considered work, rehabilitation and follow-up.

It recorded genuine structural foot abnormalities.

But the hospital did not return after the admission examination to the painful feet themselves.

At the end of the admission the relationship between the structural feet, pain, toe phenomena, dystonic-looking lower-limb posture and gait remained unresolved.

The patient's Protest stated the obvious on the final day:

> **after all of this investigation, the thing through which he had entered the medical system had still not been dealt with.**

The completed forward synthesis nevertheless made the case capable of proceeding.

Parkinsonism had a neurological pathway.

Treatment had a neurological strategy.

The feet had Podiatry.

The unexplained relationship had:

> **“Not clear.”**

And the narrative became the plan through:

> **“As per above.”**

That was the conversion point.

### The first consequence: the patient entered a treatment strategy rather than an open relationship question

The early treatment response was extraordinary.

Procyclidine and rasagiline produced major restoration of movement and function.

The patient described walking coming back together, improved writing, better posture and freer legs.

At one point his description was:

> **“living in a miracle, literally.”**

That should have been hopeful.

But almost immediately the future shape of the problem appeared.

The drug that helped produced visual and cognitive limitations.

When procyclidine was withdrawn, the patient's body generated extraordinarily relevant information.

His eyesight improved.

At the same time:

> **“my feet are restless and my walk is falling apart.”**

By April 2018 the treating neurologist himself recorded that one of the first phenomena to return rapidly after withdrawal was the **outside-left-foot phenomenon**.

The body was already crossing the boundary created in 2017.

The feet were not behaving simply like static structural objects.

Yet this new information entered as a local treatment observation.

It did not visibly force reconstruction of the original question.

So the patient's future began to take a particular form:

> **new evidence could change treatment without necessarily changing the historical architecture through which the problem was understood.**

### 2018: improvement already comes at a price

The medication worked.

But the medication could not simply be increased until the patient was well.

Brennan recorded renewed increased tone and **cramping in both feet** while also recording the earlier “great improvement.”

At the same time procyclidine was affecting vision and concentration.

He worried that:

> **“we are losing ground here.”**

The patient's problem was therefore becoming more than disease.

He was beginning to live inside a therapeutic trade-off:

> **movement versus cognition;**

> **relief versus visual toxicity;**

> **function versus medication burden.**

The 2017 strategy had been designed to avoid the anticipated long-term problems of conventional dopaminergic treatment.

But avoiding one risk did not produce an uncomplicated life.

It produced another balancing problem.

### 2018–2019: treatment becomes a tightrope

Amantadine was tried.

It did not help and was associated in the record with:

> **“quite sad thoughts.”**

Rotigotine/Neupro was then escalated.

At times the patient became substantially more mobile.

But higher doses produced a cluster of unwanted effects:

- artificial or amphetamine-like stimulation;
- compulsive/impulsive behaviour;
- dread as the patch wore off;
- and choreiform dyskinesia.

Neurology itself described the situation as:

> **“an extremely, extremely difficult tight rope.”**

That phrase describes more than prescribing difficulty.

It describes the patient's life.

Too little treatment and the body tightened, slowed and failed.

More treatment and another form of dysfunction appeared.

Meanwhile the feet remained active.

Brennan's 2019 Podiatry referral explicitly described:

> **“clawing feet” and pain**

and related the foot problems to:

> **increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease.**

The relationship was being regenerated in ordinary care.

But it still did not acquire the force of a global specialist reconstruction.

### The patient's life becomes a pharmacological experiment he has to inhabit

By this point medication was no longer merely something the patient took.

His daily life was increasingly organised around its effects.

He had to notice:

- whether movement was better;
- whether vision was worse;
- whether concentration deteriorated;
- whether tension returned;
- whether the feet became restless;
- whether gait deteriorated;
- whether a drug produced stimulation;
- whether behaviour changed;
- whether dyskinesia appeared.

The patient became the measuring instrument through which the treatment strategy was being calibrated.

That is important.

The original admission had produced a very sophisticated explanatory document.

But over the following years the **body** repeatedly supplied information more dynamically than the foundational categories did.

### 2020: better movement at the cost of the mind

By August 2020 Brennan was seeing another form of the same contradiction.

His psychology referral records physical improvement from treatment but also:

- intellectual blunting;
- slowed thought;
- reduced drive;
- impaired concentration;
- difficulty reading;
- and difficulty reconciling physical benefit with mental cost.

The observable dilemma was:

> **better movement at the cost of cognitive function.**

This is important when the following eight years are described simply as “conservative treatment.”

Conservative treatment was not an absence of intervention.

The patient was actively living through medication choices, failed alternatives, dose ceilings and adverse effects.

His body could be improved.

The price of improvement could make another part of life worse.

### 2021: levodopa finally arrives

The first recorded Sinemet treatment appears in **September 2021**.

That is more than four years after the July 2017 admission.

It did not arrive into a simple therapeutic landscape.

Procyclidine was being removed because of raised intraocular pressure.

Comtess was already present.

Sinemet was increased.

And slight chorea appeared rapidly.

The anticipated dyskinesia problem was real.

That matters.

It vindicates part of the original caution.

But it also meant that when levodopa eventually arrived, the patient did not simply move from untreated disease to effective treatment.

He entered a new narrow therapeutic window:

> **not enough dopamine → stiffness, OFF state and loss of function;**

> **more dopamine → chorea/dyskinesia.**

The future would increasingly be lived between those two states.

### 2022: ordinary activity becomes dose management

By 2022 the patient was using small Sinemet doses according to anticipated activity.

Darts and ordinary function became part of medication planning.

Stalevo could help, but a higher dose had produced obvious dyskinesia.

The feet still took time to “warm up” in the morning.

Pain remained present.

The record was increasingly describing a patient who did not simply take a stable prescription and live his life.

He had to **manage the treatment in relation to the life he wanted to perform**.

Medication timing and activity were becoming inseparable.

### 2023: the unresolved feet become a crisis

March 2023 is the point at which the long-term cost becomes impossible to describe as abstract.

The feet became catastrophically painful.

Another GP returned physically to them and obtained bilateral imaging.

The anticipated bony explanation was not demonstrated.

The patient then wrote to the practice:

> **“When I say me feet are killing me I mean that literally.”**

and:

> **“I'm losing the will to live with them at this stage.”**

Brennan escalated urgently to Neurology.

His referral described extreme pain causing a:

> **“death wish”**

and said:

> **“I am worried about him.”**

The Parkinson's nurse recorded debilitating feet/leg pain despite pregabalin at **600 mg/day** and:

> **“no quality of life.”**

This is what “Not clear” looked like six years later in the patient's life.

It was no longer a phrase in a discharge summary.

It was severe bilateral pain being treated at the maximum documented pregabalin dose while Neurology and primary care were simultaneously trying to manage a dopaminergic regimen limited by dyskinesia.

And Brennan himself explicitly returned to the beginning.

He wrote that the severe bilateral foot pains were:

> **“likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone issues”**

and that:

> **“this was actually his initial presentation to me now 6 years ago.”**

The GP had reconstructed the circle.

The patient was suffering in 2023 in the same bodily territory through which he had entered care in 2017.

### The burden was not just pain

The 2023 record shows several burdens occurring simultaneously.

#### Pain

Severe enough to generate language of death wish, losing the will to live and no quality of life.

#### Treatment burden

Pregabalin escalated from 25 mg twice daily to a total documented dose of **600 mg/day**.

#### Dyskinesia ceiling

Levodopa/Stalevo could improve movement and pain, but greater dopaminergic treatment risked greater chorea.

#### Prescription instability

The intended prescription, medication list, pharmacy supply and dose actually taken did not always agree.

#### Patient reconciliation

The patient repeatedly had to tell primary care what he was actually taking and correct missing or outdated prescriptions.

#### Fear

When pregabalin reduction was proposed, his response was:

> **“I'm scared to death of experiencing the pain that I had previously.”**

and:

> **“it fills me with anxiety.”**

This is a different kind of dependency.

The drug no longer merely treated pain.

The prospect of losing it became frightening because there was no stable alternative capable of guaranteeing that the catastrophic state would not return.

### The patient becomes part of the healthcare infrastructure

By 2023 the patient was doing work ordinarily invisible in a clinical chronology.

He was:

- tracking medication effects;
- comparing doses;
- noticing OFF periods;
- correcting the GP medication list;
- correcting pharmacy supply;
- carrying Neurology changes back to primary care;
- explaining when Stalevo was “too strong”;
- identifying missing Stalevo prescriptions;
- reporting actual pregabalin use;
- and trying to prevent medication discrepancies from producing further instability.

This is part of what the eight years converted his life into.

He was no longer simply receiving care.

He increasingly became the **bridge between Neurology, GP, pharmacy and his own changing body**.

### 2024: “a lot smaller”

By 2024 pregabalin remained at **150 mg four times daily**.

It reportedly relieved much of the foot pain, particularly on the right.

But it did not solve the whole problem.

Left-foot pain remained.

Toe curling remained.

Fatigue remained.

Dyskinesia still limited dopaminergic treatment.

And Neurology recorded that life had become:

> **“a lot smaller.”**

That may be one of the most economical descriptions in the entire record.

The patient's life was not simply a list of symptoms.

Its available radius was shrinking.

Activities became harder.

Darts participation diminished.

Fatigue mattered.

Medication timing mattered.

The feet mattered.

Movement mattered.

The margins within which the patient could function were increasingly determined by the compromise between undertreatment, pain, medication toxicity and dyskinesia.

During the same high-treatment-burden period, severe constipation and reported nocturnal urinary incontinence entered the primary-care history. The current archive appropriately does **not** assign medication causation without expert evidence.

The significance here is simpler:

> **the clinical burden was no longer confined to Parkinsonian movement and the feet.**

The whole life was becoming medically occupied.

### The paradox of pregabalin

Pregabalin is particularly revealing because it did help.

This is not a story of a useless drug being irrationally continued.

At points it produced major pain relief.

But even at **600 mg/day** it did not eliminate the problem.

Toe curling could persist.

Residual pain could persist.

The patient was afraid to reduce it because he remembered what uncontrolled pain had been like.

Neurology then had to weigh additional levodopa against dyskinesia.

So by 2023–2024 the patient was living inside this equation:

> **pregabalin helps but is incomplete;**

> **levodopa helps but produces dyskinesia;**

> **more of one is burdensome;**

> **more of the other is dangerous to function;**

> **less treatment risks the return of intolerable pain.**

That is not merely a prescribing history.

It is a description of daily life constrained by a treatment architecture.

### March 2025: the eight years return to the first steps of the morning

By **10 March 2025**, pregabalin had been reduced to **50 mg three times daily**.

The feet again dominated the consultation.

The patient's words were:

> **“murder in the feet”**

> **“the first 150 footsteps are torture”**

and:

> **“I won't live with it.”**

The same consultation records something else equally important.

The patient knew that when Sinemet reached his system:

> **the feet felt better.**

Neurology accepted that increasing Sinemet would probably provide additional pain relief.

But it also expected more severe dyskinesia.

Eight years after the university admission, the patient's morning had therefore become a distilled version of the whole management problem:

> **wake in pain;**

> **move through torture;**

> **wait for dopaminergic medication to act;**

> **obtain relief;**

> **but remain unable simply to increase the medication because the treatment itself produces disabling excessive movement.**

This is what the abstract phrase **“treatment strategy”** had become in lived experience.

### Then controlled-release Sinemet changed the information state

On 10 March 2025 controlled-release Sinemet was introduced overnight.

Within weeks the treating neurologist described the resulting benefit as:

> **“profound relief.”**

The importance of that moment is not that it proves every historical foot lesion had one neurological cause.

It does not.

Its significance is that after eight years of managing pain, structure, neuropathic possibilities, Parkinsonian phenomena, dystonia, OFF state, pregabalin and dyskinesia separately or locally, a change in dopaminergic coverage dramatically altered the disabling pain state in the same bodily territory that had helped bring the patient into hospital in 2017.

The patient's body had supplied an answer of enormous personal significance.

And that answer arrived only after eight years of living within the preceding management architecture.

### The eight years also converted the patient into an investigator

The next transformation is epistemic.

In 2017 the patient knew his body.

The institution controlled most of the clinical information and the durable record.

By March 2025 the patient had something new:

> **a bodily contradiction he could no longer reconcile with the history he had been given.**

He began asking why.

His 19 March complaint is particularly important because he explicitly acknowledged that he did **not** yet possess every internal note, referral or correspondence.

He had discovered the contradiction through his body before he possessed the archive capable of explaining it.

The following months converted him again.

He became:

- complainant;
- records requester;
- FOI recipient;
- data subject;
- chronology builder;
- medication historian;
- source comparator;
- and eventually investigator of the admission that had occurred eight years earlier.

That was not how he entered hospital in 2017.

He entered seeking medical help.

By 2025 he was having to reconstruct the medical event itself.

### What had happened to work and ordinary life?

Work was already at risk before admission.

Brennan had urgently referred the patient partly because continued deterioration threatened his job.

Early treatment did restore substantial function and enabled return-to-work planning.

That matters: the subsequent eight years were not a simple continuous decline.

There were genuine improvements.

There were good periods.

There were treatments that worked.

There were periods in which movement was dramatically better.

But the sustainability problem never disappeared.

Over the years the record contains:

- work certification;
- treatment-related cognitive impairment;
- concentration problems;
- reduced drive;
- psychological referral;
- activity-dependent dosing;
- fatigue;
- reduced recreational participation;
- shrinking function;
- catastrophic pain;
- fear of movement;
- and eventually the description of life as **“a lot smaller.”**

That is a much fairer description than saying the patient was continuously incapacitated for eight years.

He repeatedly regained function.

But he repeatedly had to purchase that function with another cost.

### What had happened to psychological life?

Again, the sources require precision.

The record does not permit every psychological difficulty to be assigned to one cause.

But it documents the experience.

Different periods include:

- “quite sad thoughts” associated with amantadine;
- compulsive/impulsive phenomena on higher-dose Neupro;
- cognitive and concentration burden on anticholinergic treatment;
- psychological support;
- fear of recurrence of catastrophic pain;
- “death wish” language during the 2023 pain crisis;
- “no quality of life”;
- later descriptions of morning psychological distress and exhaustion;
- and by March 2025 the statement:

> **“I won't live with it.”**

The patient had not entered hospital in 2017 asking to become an expert in balancing pain, dyskinesia, cognition, impulse-control effects, medication timing and prescription transmission.

That became part of the life that followed.

### What had happened to the feet?

This may be the simplest eight-year line.

#### 2017

The feet enter Neurology already painful and structurally abnormal.

Their relationship to the neurological syndrome is:

> **“Not clear.”**

#### 2017–2018

Foot and gait phenomena change with procyclidine withdrawal and restoration.

#### 2018

Bilateral foot cramping returns alongside increased tone.

#### 2019

The GP expressly relates foot problems to increased tone and abnormal Parkinsonian gait and records clawing feet and pain.

#### 2022

The feet still require a morning warm-up and remain painful.

#### 2023

The pain becomes catastrophic.

Pregabalin reaches 600 mg/day.

The Parkinson's nurse records no quality of life.

Brennan expressly says the current severe bilateral foot pain is likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone and is the original presentation returning six years later.

#### 2023–2024

Neurology recognises OFF-related pain and shuffling.

Pregabalin helps but does not resolve all pain or toe curling.

#### 2025

The first 150 steps are torture.

Sinemet helps the feet.

More Sinemet would probably help the pain further but produces dyskinesia.

Controlled-release Sinemet produces **profound relief**.

And yet the 2025 retrospective explanation can still retain:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

That is the eight-year history of the unresolved relationship.

The feet kept producing information.

The record kept finding local explanations for each new piece.

The original relationship remained capable of staying open.

### What the eight days therefore converted

It would be excessive to say:

> **the July admission caused eight years of suffering.**

The underlying disease caused suffering.

Structural pathology caused suffering.

Treatment toxicity caused suffering.

Dyskinesia caused treatment constraints.

The natural evolution of the condition mattered.

Later clinical decisions mattered independently.

The documentary record cannot collapse all of that into one originating cause.

But it is fair to ask whether those eight days **converted the terms on which the next eight years would be lived**.

They converted:

#### One integrated presenting problem

into:

> **parallel clinical territories whose relationship lacked durable ownership.**

#### An open diagnostic referral

into:

> **a powerful specialist forward representation.**

#### A simple bodily question

into:

> **years of local interpretations: structural, neuropathic, Parkinsonian, dystonic, OFF-related and treatment-responsive.**

#### Treatment uncertainty

into:

> **a prolonged balancing problem between symptom control and adverse effects.**

#### The patient's role

from:

> **person seeking an explanation**

to:

> **person repeatedly observing, reconciling, transmitting and eventually reconstructing the explanation himself.**

#### Eight inpatient days

into:

> **an eight-year information pathway.**

### The most damning comparison

On 3 July 2017 the patient's problem could still be stated simply:

> **My feet hurt. My gait is wrong. My body is tight. My toes claw. My movement and function are deteriorating. What is happening to me?**

By March 2025 the same patient was living inside:

- Parkinsonian disease management;
- structural Podiatry;
- possible neuropathy;
- dystonia;
- OFF-related symptoms;
- dyskinesia;
- anticholinergic toxicity;
- dopamine-agonist toxicity;
- maximum-dose pregabalin;
- IR/CR levodopa balancing;
- medication self-adjustment;
- GP–Neurology authority boundaries;
- prescription mismatches;
- psychological distress;
- severe morning pain;
- and a historical record which still had not produced an authoritative reconstruction of the original feet–neurology relationship.

That is an extraordinary increase in complexity.

But the original question had not become proportionately more complicated.

It was still:

> **What is the relationship between the neurological disorder and these feet?**

### Eight days and eight years

The simplest representation is therefore:

> **Eight days were sufficient to diagnose severe Parkinsonism, construct an elaborate treatment strategy and create a durable clinical account. They were not sufficient to return to and resolve the painful feet through which the patient had entered care. The unresolved relationship was made manageable as “Not clear” with Podiatry as its structural destination. The next eight years were then lived inside that arrangement: dramatic neurological benefit constrained by visual and cognitive toxicity; medication withdrawal making the feet and gait deteriorate together; bilateral foot cramping accompanying increased tone; dopamine-agonist stimulation and compulsivity; delayed levodopa followed by early dyskinesia; activity-based dosing; catastrophic bilateral foot pain; pregabalin escalating to 600 mg/day without securing quality of life; fear of reducing it; OFF-related pain and shuffling; persistent toe curling; fatigue and a life described as “a lot smaller”; repeated prescription reconciliation by the patient himself; and finally morning pain so severe that the first 150 steps were “torture,” followed by controlled-release levodopa producing “profound relief.” The eight days did not cause all of those events. They did, however, establish the information and management architecture through which those events were repeatedly interpreted and absorbed.**

### The human consequence

The deepest consequence may not be any single drug, symptom or missed opportunity.

It is that the patient spent eight years **living the unresolved question rather than having the unresolved question carried forward for him.**

His body repeatedly generated the information.

His GP repeatedly regenerated the relationship.

Treatments repeatedly altered the relevant territory.

Neurology repeatedly recognised individual components.

Yet the burden of putting the whole story back together eventually fell upon the person who had already pointed out, on the last day of the original admission, that the story was incomplete.

That is the transformation from eight days to eight years.

> **He entered hospital as the patient with the problem.**
> **He emerged into eight years in which he increasingly became the custodian of the problem that the record had divided.**

And when the bodily contradiction finally became overwhelming in 2025, he had to return not merely to Neurology, but to **July 2017 itself**.

The eight-year journey therefore ends where the eight-day admission should have remained open:

> **the feet.**

# Risk Rank 20 — Original Profile 13

**Severity:** SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Strengthens the treatment-state relationship between neurological control and the feet without requiring single-mechanism causation.

## Profile 13 — Treatment-State Causation: Procyclidine, Formulation and the Feet

<!-- SOURCE: synthesis from the existing booklet's fifth-MTPJ, natural-experiment, treatment-burden and predictability profiles, supplemented by the 2026 St Vincent's Parkinson's-nurse correspondence reviewed 1 September 2026. -->

This profile qualifies an earlier shorthand used elsewhere in the booklet.

The phrase **“dyskinesia ceiling”** is useful only if it is understood as a ceiling produced by the **regimen then being used**, not as proof of an immutable biological bargain in which treating the feet necessarily caused dyskinesia.

The later history shows that formulation, dose size, timing and delivery materially changed both the painful foot state and the dyskinetic state.

The earlier procyclidine history shows that the record had reason to suspect this long before 2025.

### 1. The first important experiment was not levodopa

The patient's neurological state improved dramatically on procyclidine.

When procyclidine was withdrawn because of visual toxicity, the contemporaneous record captured a characteristic dechallenge:

> tension built up;
>
> the feet became restless;
>
> gait deteriorated;
>
> and Neurology subsequently recorded rapid return of the focal **outside-left-foot phenomenon**.

The Podiatry record independently fixes the persistent structural problem in that same focal territory at the **left fifth MTPJ**.

The correct inference is not that procyclidine made a structural corn disappear.

It is more important:

> **the clinical expression of the painful fifth-MTPJ territory was materially dependent upon neurological treatment state.**

That was already an integrative signal.

### 2. The dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop gave the signal a mechanism

The admission had already documented a lower-limb phenomenon resembling footdrop but judged to be:

> **“somewhat suggestive of a dystonic posture — NOT FOOT DROP.”**

That matters biomechanically and neurologically.

A dystonic foot/lower-limb state can alter:

- foot posture;
- toe position and clawing;
- timing of stance and swing;
- local pressure distribution;
- forefoot loading;
- compensatory weight transfer;
- and the painful expression of an existing structural pressure lesion.

So once a focal painful foot territory changed with an anticholinergic treatment state, the relevant question was no longer merely whether there was a corn or callus.

It was:

> **How is the dystonic/Parkinsonian movement state changing the loading and painful expression of this structurally vulnerable foot?**

That question did not require a confirmed genotype.

It arose from the phenotype and the dechallenge.

### 3. The procyclidine experiment should have changed future expectation

The July 2017 discharge state had left the relationship:

> **“Not clear.”**

The procyclidine dechallenge supplied new information capable of narrowing that state.

Once one neurological medication had shown that the focal foot territory changed with neurological treatment state, it became reasonable to anticipate that **other neurological treatments might alter the same territory**.

That does not mean anybody could know in advance exactly what levodopa would do.

It means the hypothesis should have existed:

> **if the feet are dynamically modified by one neurological treatment, future dopaminergic treatment may further expose the same relationship.**

This is why the dechallenge was not merely a medication side note.

It was an early predictive signal.

### 4. Levodopa later reproduced the same class of information

When levodopa entered the treatment history, the feet again became treatment-state informative.

The record eventually contains:

- OFF-related pain and shuffling;
- feet improving when Sinemet reaches the system;
- an expectation that additional Sinemet would probably provide additional pain relief;
- controlled-release Sinemet producing **“profound relief”**;
- and later correspondence describing the longstanding foot pain as no longer a significant problem.

So the later history does not introduce a completely new phenomenon.

It reproduces an older informational pattern:

> **neurological treatment state changes the feet.**

The pharmacology differs.

The informational lesson does not.

### 5. Formulation then supplies a second causal layer

The most important later development is that levodopa itself did not behave as one undifferentiated treatment.

The record describes immediate-release/pulsatile treatment as producing unstable transitions between:

> under-medication → toe curling, tension, painful dystonic states;

and:

> excessive peaks → dyskinesia.

Stalevo 75 produced obvious chorea/dyskinesia. Smaller immediate-release doses could still produce inconsistent or unpredictable benefit and over-effect.

Controlled-release levodopa changed that geometry.

In March 2025, half-Sinemet CR at night produced a major change and by 25 March the effect was described as:

> **“profound relief.”**

By 2026 the treatment architecture had moved much further toward sustained coverage rather than repeated immediate-release peaks.

In the June–July 2026 St Vincent's Parkinson's-nurse correspondence, the patient described a CR-based regimen with small Madopar doses used to bridge residual gaps. The July correspondence records:

- **Madopar 12.5/50 mg at approximately 9 a.m. and 9 p.m.;**
- **Sinemet CR 25/100 mg at approximately 11:30 a.m., 4:30 p.m. and bedtime;**
- **no procyclidine.**

St Vincent's subsequently confirmed and issued the updated **Madopar 12.5/50 mg twice-daily** prescription.

The same correspondence reports that the longstanding foot pain had largely resolved and that the previous cycle of foot pain, dystonia and dyskinesia had been brought much closer under control.

### 6. The later state is a natural within-patient causal test

The later state is unusually informative because many background variables remain.

The patient still has:

- the same underlying Parkinsonian disorder;
- the same history of structurally abnormal feet;
- the same need for dopaminergic treatment;
- and residual dystonic/tone phenomena.

What changes materially is the **delivery architecture of treatment**.

Earlier:

> immediate-release / pulsatile delivery
> → peaks and troughs
> → painful dystonic or under-treated states at one end
> → dyskinesia at the other.

Later:

> sustained controlled-release coverage + small strategically timed rapid doses
> → narrower fluctuation range
> → no disabling foot pain
> → no comparable disabling dyskinetic state.

That is not merely “levodopa response.”

It is evidence about **mechanism**.

### 7. What the mechanism does and does not prove

The later history does not prove that every corn, callus, hammertoe or structural lesion was caused by Parkinsonism.

It does not require that proposition.

The stronger and safer conclusion is:

> **the severity and painful clinical expression of the structurally vulnerable foot territory were materially modulated by neurological treatment state and, later, by the formulation and temporal delivery of dopaminergic treatment.**

The structure could remain while the disabling pain disappeared.

That is exactly what a structural–dynamic model predicts.

### 8. The missing investigation becomes more visible retrospectively

The record contains many observations from which the relationship can now be reconstructed.

What is much less visible is a systematic contemporaneous investigation asking:

- when exactly does the foot pain begin relative to each formulation and dose?
- what happens during OFF, transition and peak states?
- what happens to the fifth-MTPJ territory when dose size changes?
- what happens when immediate-release treatment is replaced by sustained coverage?
- does overnight coverage alter morning pain?
- can analgesic/motor benefit be retained while flattening dyskinetic peaks?
- how do toe posture, dystonia, loading and the structural lesion change across those states?

The later success makes those missing questions unusually important.

The formulation variable was not trivial.

It was capable of separating outcomes that had previously looked inseparable.

### 9. This qualifies the earlier “dyskinesia ceiling” language

The booklet should therefore read its earlier references to a **dyskinesia ceiling** with this qualification:

> **The ceiling was real within particular regimens, but it was not demonstrated to be an immutable ceiling on useful dopaminergic treatment.**

Dyskinesia was itself a treatment-state outcome requiring optimisation.

The later formulation history demonstrates that useful dopaminergic control of the feet and unacceptable dyskinesia were not necessarily the two unavoidable sides of the same bargain.

### Profile of Treatment-State Causation

> **The record generated evidence remarkably early that the painful focal foot territory was neurologically modifiable. Procyclidine produced dramatic neurological improvement; when it was withdrawn, tension returned, gait deteriorated, the feet became restless and Neurology recorded rapid return of the outside-left-foot phenomenon. Podiatry independently fixed the persistent structural lesion in that territory at the left fifth MTPJ. In a patient whose admission had already documented a dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop, that dechallenge should have transformed “Not clear” into an active structural–dynamic hypothesis: the structural lesion could be genuine while its loading and painful expression were materially influenced by neurological state. Later levodopa treatment reproduced the same class of information. OFF state, dose timing and Sinemet exposure affected the feet; controlled-release Sinemet produced “profound relief”; and by 2026 a CR-based regimen with small strategically timed Madopar doses had largely eliminated the longstanding disabling foot pain without preserving the former disabling dyskinetic state. The later course therefore does more than show that levodopa happened to help. It supplies a within-patient mechanistic test: changing dopaminergic formulation and temporal delivery changed the painful foot state and the dyskinetic state while the underlying disease and structural foot history remained. The unresolved question was not merely whether the feet were neurological or structural. It was how neurological state altered the clinical expression of a structurally vulnerable foot—and the record had reason to investigate that relationship years before the final formulation made it unmistakable.**

The irreducible form is:

> **Procyclidine showed the foot territory was treatment-state sensitive.**
>
> **Levodopa later showed it again.**
>
> **Formulation showed why the apparent pain–dyskinesia bargain was not fixed.**
>
> **The signal existed long before the relationship was reconstructed.**

# Risk Rank 21 — Original Profile 11

**Severity:** SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Uses the recurrent fifth-MTPJ territory as an anatomical information tracer across structural and neurological streams.

## Profile 11 — Left Fifth MTPJ

<!-- SOURCE: supplied in project conversation on 1 September 2026; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 1 of 12 — The Left Fifth MTPJ -->

### The Anatomical Constant

The left fifth metatarsophalangeal joint is one of the most important objects in the case because it turns an apparently diffuse eight-year history of “foot pain” into something anatomically continuous.

This was not a succession of vaguely located pains that were later retrospectively connected. The patient knew the longstanding focal lesion as **the corn on the outside of the left foot**. It was not one of several interchangeable corns. It was the persistent painful site he knew from years of living with and treating his feet.

The Podiatry record then gives that lived anatomical description its clinical address:

> **left fifth MTPJ.**

Across **eight out of eight Podiatry treatment visits**, that same site was identified and treated. It was repeatedly debrided, enucleated, padded or offloaded. There was not one visit in which a different focal site replaced it as the recurring lesion. The December 2018 encounter is particularly explicit: the patient reports pain under the left side of the left foot; the fifth-MTPJ callus is recorded; and the insole is specifically modified to offload the fifth-foot region.

So the first fact is simple:

> **There is a continuous focal structural object in this history: the painful left fifth MTPJ.**

That matters because it allows the neurological history to be attached to something physically stable.

### What Neurology knew in 2017

Neurology did not receive a patient with a fifth-MTPJ lesion floating independently from his neurological presentation.

The patient arrived with abnormal painful feet inside a wider syndrome of abnormal gait, pathological tone, toe-clawing and movement difficulty.

During admission Neurology itself observed that the gait was **“strikingly abnormal”** and that the lower-limb appearance resembled footdrop while being:

> **“somewhat suggestive of a dystonic posture — NOT FOOT DROP.”**

Neurology therefore had two different kinds of information in the same patient.

It had a **dynamic neurological abnormality** affecting the lower limb.

And it had **structural painful abnormality of the feet**.

The unresolved question was their relationship.

The fifth MTPJ is important because it shows that this was not merely a philosophical question about whether “foot pain” might sometimes accompany Parkinsonism. There was a particular structural pressure site whose behaviour could potentially be watched.

### The first natural experiment

The decisive development comes when procyclidine is withdrawn.

The patient's neurological state worsens.

His gait begins to fall apart.

He becomes more restless, shifty and unable to relax.

And the focal outside-left-foot phenomenon returns rapidly.

Because the patient identifies **the outside-left corn**, and because the continuous Podiatry record repeatedly fixes that persistent lesion at the **left fifth MTPJ**, this is not merely evidence that “something happened somewhere in the feet.”

It is evidence that the clinical expression of the longstanding fifth-MTPJ problem changed with neurological medication state.

That fact has to be kept very precise.

It does **not** prove that procyclidine cured a corn.

It does **not** prove that the fifth-MTPJ lesion was neurologically created.

It establishes something more interesting:

> **A genuine structural lesion at a stable anatomical site was clinically responsive to a change in neurological treatment state.**

That should have transformed the question.

### Structural and neurological were never competing explanations

The fifth MTPJ exposes the weakness of treating the case as though one had to choose between:

> structural foot pathology

and

> neurological pathology.

Both can be true at once.

The callus can be real.

The corn can be real.

The repeated need for debridement can be real.

The pressure sensitivity can be real.

The pes planus, hammertoes, clawing and altered foot mechanics can all be real.

And the amount of load being driven through that vulnerable site can still vary according to dystonic posture, abnormal tone, gait and neurological treatment state.

The relevant clinical question was therefore never simply:

> **Is the fifth-MTPJ pain structural or neurological?**

It was:

> **What is the neurological lower-limb state doing to this structurally vulnerable fifth MTPJ?**

That is an entirely different question.

### Why repeated debridement matters

The eight Podiatry visits are not just evidence that Podiatry provided ongoing care.

They establish persistence.

The same site kept becoming clinically important enough to require treatment.

That means the lesion was not disposed of by one successful structural intervention.

It recurred.

Again and again.

Podiatry could remove the hard tissue.

It could redistribute pressure.

It could create an offloading cut-out.

But the site continued to return.

That repeated recurrence matters profoundly once it is placed beside the neurological treatment history.

The question becomes:

> **Why did the same mechanically vulnerable place keep requiring treatment, and why did its painful expression vary with neurological state?**

Neither information stream can answer that by itself.

Together they demand the question.

### What Podiatry could see

Podiatry possessed a remarkably consistent structural history.

It repeatedly saw:

> **left fifth MTPJ lesion → pressure → pain → debridement/offloading → recurrence.**

Within Podiatry's remit, that sequence is intelligible.

The site is mechanically vulnerable.

Treat it mechanically.

Review it.

Treat it again if it returns.

But the Podiatry record by itself does not tell the clinician that an anticholinergic neurological medication had altered the patient's experience at that same focal site.

That information lived elsewhere.

### What Neurology could see

Neurology possessed the other half.

It knew:

> severe young-onset Parkinsonism;

> dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenology;

> marked neurological treatment responsiveness;

> deterioration when treatment was withdrawn;

> and rapid return of the outside-left-foot phenomenon with that deterioration.

Later it would accumulate more:

> bilateral foot cramping with increased tone;

> OFF-related pain and shuffling;

> feet improving when Sinemet reached the system;

> and profound relief after altered dopaminergic coverage.

But Neurology did not possess the fifth MTPJ primarily as an eight-visit mechanically recurring object.

That object had been dispatched into Podiatry's information stream.

### The clinically powerful fact existed only between the services

This is the essence of the fifth-MTPJ profile.

Podiatry knew:

> **the same structural site repeatedly failed.**

Neurology knew:

> **the phenomenon at that site could change with neurological state.**

The important proposition is the combination:

> **The same repeatedly failing structural fifth-MTPJ site was neurologically modifiable.**

That proposition required the two information streams to meet.

The discharge arrangement gave them no obvious place to meet.

### The fifth MTPJ therefore becomes an information tracer

This one joint allows us to watch the information architecture operate.

At the beginning, the joint is inside the whole patient.

During admission it exists alongside abnormal gait, pathological tone and possible dystonia.

At discharge the structural component moves toward Podiatry while the dynamic neurological component remains with Neurology.

Podiatry then repeatedly sees the same fifth MTPJ.

Neurology sees treatment-responsive movement phenomena.

Primary care intermittently sees both.

The patient experiences both continuously.

The relationship itself becomes distributed.

The fifth MTPJ therefore behaves almost like a tracer dye through the healthcare system: follow it, and you can see exactly where the integrated phenotype separates.

### The later Sinemet evidence did not begin the story

This is another reason the fifth MTPJ is so important.

The 2025 Sinemet response might otherwise look like a surprising late discovery.

It was not the first signal that the foot territory was neurologically modifiable.

Years earlier, procyclidine withdrawal had already altered the focal outside-left-foot phenomenon.

The later history progressively strengthened the same proposition.

The feet cramped with increased neurological tone.

Movement Disorders later considered pain and shuffling OFF-related.

The patient knew the feet improved once Sinemet took effect.

Sinemet CR ultimately produced profound relief.

The 2025 event therefore does not stand alone.

It sits at the end of a sequence whose earliest focal signal was already present around the fifth-MTPJ territory.

### Why the fifth MTPJ is dangerous to the old partition

A broad statement such as:

> **Parkinson medication can make pain feel better regardless of cause**

can accommodate a great deal.

It can preserve structural disease.

It can preserve possible neuropathy.

It can preserve the old “Not clear.”

But the fifth MTPJ makes that explanation much less satisfying as the whole account.

Because the question is not merely:

> did a patient with Parkinsonism experience less generic pain?

It is:

> **Why did the painful expression of a repeatedly identified, repeatedly treated, anatomically stable structural site change with neurological treatment state?**

That is a mechanistic question.

And it leads directly back to what the 2017 admission had failed to resolve:

> **the relationship between dynamic neurological lower-limb behaviour and structural painful foot pathology.**

### The possible loading chain

The documentary evidence generates a straightforward biomechanical hypothesis.

A dystonic/Parkinsonian lower-limb state could alter foot position or gait.

That could alter the forces passing through the lateral forefoot.

The already vulnerable fifth MTPJ could receive greater pressure.

Pain at that site could then make the patient unload it or compensate through other parts of the foot.

Those compensations could redistribute pressure across an already structurally abnormal pair of feet.

The resulting pain and altered gait could then further change loading.

The detailed biomechanics require expert analysis before being asserted as proven causation.

But no expert is needed to establish why the question exists.

The record itself supplies:

> **dynamic abnormality + stable structural lesion + repeated recurrence + offloading + neurological medication responsiveness.**

That combination demands investigation.

### What should have happened informationally

Once Neurology knew that its medication altered the outside-left-foot phenomenon, the fifth-MTPJ history should have ceased to belong exclusively to Podiatry.

And once Podiatry repeatedly treated the same fifth-MTPJ lesion, that structural recurrence should have been capable of travelling back into the neurological question.

The information should have become:

> **structural lesion, under repeated mechanical stress, whose clinical expression appears sensitive to neurological state.**

Instead the two halves remained usable independently.

Podiatry could continue treating the corn.

Neurology could continue treating Parkinsonism.

Both could behave reasonably within their respective frames.

The relationship could remain nobody's object.

### Profile of the Left Fifth MTPJ

> **The left fifth MTPJ is the anatomical constant running through the case. The patient knew it as the longstanding corn and focal pain on the outside of the left foot; Podiatry identified and treated that same left fifth-MTPJ lesion on eight out of eight consecutive treatment visits, repeatedly debriding, enucleating or offloading it. Neurology, meanwhile, had identified a dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenon in the same patient and subsequently recorded that neurological medication state altered the outside-left-foot phenomenon rapidly. The significance is not that the fifth-MTPJ lesion was therefore “really neurological” rather than structural. Its structural reality is repeatedly documented. The significance is that its clinical expression was neurologically modifiable. Podiatry possessed the structural recurrence; Neurology possessed the dynamic treatment response. The clinically powerful fact existed only when those two streams were joined: the same repeatedly failing structural site changed with neurological state. The discharge architecture separated precisely the information required to see that relationship.**

The irreducible version is:

> **Podiatry knew where the problem was.**
> **Neurology knew something neurological could change it.**
> **The patient was the only person guaranteed to know those were the same problem.**

# Risk Rank 22 — Original Profile 21

**Severity:** SERIOUS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** The patient is the only guaranteed continuous integrator across services, treatment states and bodily phenomena.

## Profile 21 — Patient as the Only Continuous Integrator

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown (3)(2).md; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 10 of 12 — The Patient as the Only Continuous Integrator -->

### The One Person Who Never Lost the Whole Case

Across the eight-year history, the patient's care becomes progressively distributed.

Neurology sees one part.

Podiatry sees another.

Primary care sees continuity.

Movement Disorders sees treatment state.

Pharmacy sees medication supply.

The complaint system sees correspondence and review.

But only one person is guaranteed to experience all of these as one continuous phenomenon:

> **the patient.**

That fact is not incidental.

It explains why the patient repeatedly becomes the person who reconnects information that the system has separated.

### 1. Before the institutions divide the problem, the patient already knows it as one body

The patient does not experience:

> Neurology;

> Podiatry;

> primary care;

> OFF state;

> fifth MTPJ;

> dyskinesia;

> structural disease

as separate systems.

He experiences:

> **my feet hurt; my gait changes; my muscles tighten; my toes claw; medication changes what happens; treatment helps one thing and worsens another.**

The institutions create categories later.

The patient's body is integrated before those categories exist.

That is why his observations have such longitudinal value.

### 2. The GP referral reflects the patient's integrated experience

Brennan's original referral is powerful partly because it captures the case before specialist separation.

The patient is describing:

- painful abnormal feet;
- inability to relax;
- toe clawing;
- movement-initiation difficulty;
- gait problems;
- deteriorating dexterity;
- functional decline.

The GP sends one unresolved phenotype.

That representation corresponds closely to the patient's lived experience.

At this stage, no one has yet told him that the feet belong in one pathway and the Parkinsonism in another.

### 3. During the admission, the patient watches the problem being divided

The patient spends eight days inside Neurology.

He sees the depth of investigation.

He understands that something major has been discovered.

He knows the clinicians are taking the neurological condition seriously.

But he also knows something simpler:

> **the feet have not been returned to.**

This is why the Protest matters so much from the patient's perspective.

He is not bringing in outside medical theory.

He is monitoring whether the original problem remains intact.

The clinicians are solving an increasingly sophisticated neurological case.

The patient is still carrying the original integrated object.

That is why he notices the mismatch.

### 4. The Protest is the patient's first act of reintegration

The NCT/bald-tyres analogy is an integrative act.

It says:

> **You have assessed the system, but the part I came in with is still unresolved.**

The patient is attempting to force the whole case back into one frame before discharge.

That effort fails institutionally.

But it is the first example of the role he will repeatedly perform over the next eight years.

### 5. After discharge, the patient is the only guaranteed carrier between Neurology and Podiatry

Once the feet move toward Podiatry and Parkinsonism remains with Neurology, the patient becomes the physical connection between the two systems.

Podiatry knows:

> the fifth MTPJ keeps failing.

Neurology knows:

> neurological treatment changes the foot/lower-limb state.

The patient knows both.

He knows:

> **this is the same foot; this is the same focal pain; and it changes when my neurological treatment changes.**

No transfer letter is required for him to know that.

He lives it.

### 6. The patient's body becomes a longitudinal sensor

Over time, the patient repeatedly supplies highly discriminating observations.

He notices when:

- movement improves;
- tension reduces;
- the gait comes back together;
- the feet become restless;
- the outside-left pain returns;
- treatment wears off;
- pain changes with Sinemet;
- pregabalin helps;
- dyskinesia limits benefit.

These observations are not random diary entries.

They are effectively a continuous within-patient monitoring system.

The patient is detecting relationships across domains that the formal record stores separately.

### 7. He becomes the bridge between medication states

The medication history increases this burden.

The patient has to know:

> what he is actually taking;

> what effect it has;

> what happens when it is stopped;

> whether the pharmacy has supplied the intended dose;

> whether the GP medication list is accurate;

> whether Neurology's instructions match the prescription.

That means the patient becomes not merely the recipient of treatment but part of the mechanism by which treatment information is reconciled.

The more complicated the medication system becomes, the more indispensable his own memory and observation become.

### 8. He becomes the bridge between institutions

Over time, the patient carries information among:

- Neurology;
- GP;
- Podiatry;
- pharmacy;
- complaint services.

The system increasingly relies on him to explain:

> what happened elsewhere;

> what medication changed;

> what pain returned;

> which site is painful;

> what another clinician said.

That is a sign of fragmentation.

The patient is performing integration work that ideally should also exist in the record.

### 9. His lived knowledge remains more continuous than the documentary knowledge

This is especially important for the fifth MTPJ.

The patient does not need to reconstruct that the outside-left corn and the repeatedly treated fifth-MTPJ site are the same enduring problem.

He already knows it.

He has been living with it.

Likewise, he does not need a formal multidisciplinary conference to know:

> **the foot feels different when the neurological state changes.**

That continuity is inherent in lived experience.

The record must reconstruct what the body never forgot.

### 10. The system repeatedly asks the patient to translate himself

Because the problem is divided, the patient has to speak differently in different settings.

To Podiatry:

> pain, pressure, corn, lateral foot.

To Neurology:

> stiffness, gait, medication effect, OFF state.

To primary care:

> severe pain, function, medication burden.

To complaint services:

> history, harm, reasoning, missing explanation.

The same clinical object is repeatedly translated into the vocabulary of the service receiving it.

The danger is that each translation loses part of the relationship.

### 11. The patient therefore becomes the only persistent cross-domain memory

Clinicians change.

Documents travel.

Services hold partial records.

Personal memory fades.

But the patient continues to remember:

> the pre-2017 feet;

> the admission;

> the lack of return to the feet;

> the Protest;

> the early medication response;

> the recurrent focal pain;

> the Podiatry treatments;

> the later OFF states;

> the severe crisis;

> the Sinemet response.

This makes the patient the longest continuous archive of the relationship.

That is not the same thing as saying every patient recollection is infallible.

It means the patient possesses a category of longitudinal continuity that no single institutional actor necessarily possesses.

### 12. The patient's observations repeatedly precede institutional integration

This pattern recurs.

The patient notices the feet changing with medication before the system globally reconstructs it.

The patient notices when pain becomes intolerable.

The patient knows when Sinemet reaches his system and changes the feet.

The patient recognises the contradiction in 2025 before he has access to all of the documents needed to explain it.

Again and again:

> **the lived relationship appears before the documentary relationship.**

That is why the patient eventually becomes investigator.

### 13. The patient also carries the one event the record does not

The Protest exists most securely because the patient carries it forward.

The discharge summary does not.

Later clinical summaries do not.

The institutional record therefore lacks the very event in which the patient attempted to stop the information split.

So the patient is not merely carrying symptom continuity.

He is carrying **documentary continuity against the record itself**.

That is extraordinarily important.

### 14. This changes the meaning of later complaint-making

By 2025 the patient is not simply dissatisfied with care.

He is trying to reconcile:

> what his body has shown;

with:

> what the historical record says.

That is why the complaint begins before full documentary disclosure.

The body has already produced a contradiction.

The patient then seeks the records to explain it.

The complaint is therefore an act of reconstruction:

> **bring the separated information back together.**

### 15. The patient becomes an investigator because the system did not preserve the whole

This is one of the deepest transformations in the case.

The patient enters hospital as someone seeking explanation.

He later becomes:

- chronology builder;
- medication historian;
- records requester;
- comparator of documents;
- anatomist of his own fifth-MTPJ history;
- investigator of the Protest;
- analyst of the discharge summary.

That role was not self-appointed out of preference.

It emerged because the information required to explain his own course had become dispersed.

### 16. The patient's continuity is also why the original Protest remains so important

The Protest proves that the patient was already trying to preserve integration before the fragmentation hardened.

He was not reconstructing years later out of hindsight.

He identified the incompleteness **at the point of discharge**.

That gives later patient integration a powerful continuity:

> he said the feet were unresolved then;

> he continued experiencing those same feet across the next eight years;

> and he eventually discovered treatment-state evidence that made the old incompleteness impossible for him to ignore.

The later reconstruction is therefore not disconnected from the original encounter.

It is its continuation.

### 17. The system progressively relies on the patient while discounting the significance of what only he can integrate

This is the paradox.

The patient is repeatedly relied upon for:

- symptom reporting;
- medication effects;
- actual dosing;
- pain severity;
- functional consequences;
- treatment timing.

His information is clinically useful.

But when the same patient says:

> **the historical relationship itself is wrong or incomplete**

the system can retreat to its written record.

So his observations are trusted locally.

His global reconstruction is treated more cautiously.

That is another information asymmetry.

### 18. Why this matters for reliability

The fact that the patient is the only continuous integrator does not mean:

> believe the patient automatically.

It means:

> **do not treat institutional fragmentation as superior evidence simply because it is documentary.**

The patient holds continuity.

The institutions hold specialised partial records.

A reliable reconstruction requires both.

The mistake would be to let the partial institutional records overrule the integrated lived chronology simply because they are official.

### 19. The patient is effectively the missing longitudinal multidisciplinary meeting

This may be the simplest metaphor.

Over eight years, the system never seems to create a single durable forum in which:

> Neurology's dynamic knowledge;

> Podiatry's structural knowledge;

> primary care's continuity;

> Movement Disorders' OFF-state knowledge;

> and the patient's lived experience

are all placed together.

The patient is the only place where that multidisciplinary synthesis continuously exists.

He is carrying the meeting around in his body.

### 20. The eventual reconstruction is therefore structurally inevitable

Once Sinemet CR produces profound relief, the patient's accumulated knowledge becomes impossible to ignore.

He knows:

- the same foot has always mattered;
- Podiatry has repeatedly treated it;
- neurological medication has changed it before;
- the pain has become catastrophic;
- and altered dopaminergic coverage now profoundly relieves it.

The patient therefore does what the information system never fully did:

> **he joins the timeline.**

That is the origin of the modern case reconstruction.

### Profile of the Patient as the Only Continuous Integrator

> **As the patient's care became distributed across Neurology, Podiatry, primary care, pharmacy, Movement Disorders and later complaint processes, the patient remained the only entity guaranteed to experience every part of the phenotype continuously. He knew the longstanding focal fifth-MTPJ pain, the structural treatments, the gait and tone changes, the medication ON/OFF effects, the recurrent cramping, the OFF-related deterioration, the dyskinesia ceiling, the prescription discrepancies, the catastrophic pain and the later Sinemet response as events occurring in one body rather than in separate service categories. The discharge-day Protest was his first explicit attempt to preserve that unity before the information fragmented: he recognised that Neurology had extensively investigated the whole patient while failing to return to the presenting feet. After discharge he became the practical bridge between institutions and medication states, repeatedly supplying information that crossed the boundaries created by the record. By 2025, when his bodily response contradicted the historical explanatory frame, he had to become not merely patient but records requester and investigator in order to reconstruct the relationship the system had never durably owned. His evidential significance therefore does not arise from the proposition that patient recollection is infallible. It arises from continuity: the institutions possessed specialised fragments; the patient continuously possessed the whole.**

The irreducible version:

> **The institutions divided the case.**
> **The patient's body never did.**

And the hardest form:

> **For eight years, the only truly integrated clinical record was the patient himself.**
