# Risk Rank 28 — Original Profile 36

**Severity:** SYNTHESIS / EVIDENTIAL CONTROL / SECONDARY  
**Primary defendant exposure:** States the allegation in disciplined form and identifies the precise documentary and clinical propositions that carry it.

## Profile 36 — Profile of the Allegation

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-130919).md -->

<!-- Original source heading: Profile of the Allegation -->

### The Case in the Clinical System’s Own Words

The allegation can be stated almost entirely from phrases generated by the clinicians and institution themselves.

It begins before the hospital.

Brennan was considering Neurology for:

> **“gait, feet and ‘muscle tension’ issues.”**

His urgent referral described bilateral foot problems, an unusual stiff/festinant gait, marked increased tone, inability to relax, toe-clawing, worsening dexterity and occupational failure. His differential remained:

> **“?parkinsonian or other underlying diagnosis”**

and he warned that the patient was deteriorating and could lose his job.

That is what entered Neurology.

### What Neurology found

The hospital's own examination says:

> **“The gait is strikingly abnormal.”**

The patient walked:

> **“almost with a footdrop appearance somewhat suggestive of a dystonic posture - NOT FOOT DROP.”**

The record also documents:

> **“Pes planus”**

and:

> **“Hammertoes.”**

The impression was:

> **“A Young Onset Parkinsonsm”**

with the possibility of autosomal-recessive disease. The later genetic discussion says:

> **“it is quite plausible that Liam may have one of these disorders”**

and specifically asks whether:

> **“he does have a PRKN gene disorder (PARK 2)?”**

So the institutional record itself contains, in July 2017:

> **young-onset Parkinsonism + dystonic-looking lower-limb gait + abnormal structural feet + possible PRKN.**

No reconstruction is necessary to create that combination.

The hospital wrote it.

### What happened to the feet

The same document then says:

> **“The leg symptoms including corns and callouses and burning may have been completely separate or perhaps a secondary peripheral neuropathy…”**

and concludes:

> **“Not clear.”**

It says the patient had in his own mind:

> **“markedly divorced”**

the leg tension from the corns, calluses and pains in the feet.

The practical instruction is:

> **“Please refer Liam to outpatient Chiropody/Podiatry services.”**

The compressed discharge section later says:

> **“Podiatry referral has been sent to review issues with feet reported by the patient.”**

Thus the institution's own sequence is:

> **dystonic-looking gait → structural feet → painful feet → possible separate process / possible neuropathy → “Not clear” → Podiatry.**

### What happened to the neurological treatment question

The treatment side of the case received much greater definition.

The discharge document says:

> **“The greatest of care has been advised with Prof Counihan and Neurology Colleagues”**

around future dopamine agonist or levodopa treatment.

The reason was the patient's exceptionally young age and the risk that dyskinesia might develop rapidly.

The strategy is explicit:

> **“We were likely try and avoid these for as long as possible.”**

Later in the same definitive document the neurologist says:

> **“Ordinarily we would use levodopa or dopamine agonists”**

but:

> **“we tend to be extremely cautious in their usage”**

and:

> **“we will try to leave off on this for as long as possible.”**

Yet the document also accepts the potential benefit:

> **“It is possible that he will do very well with levodopa”**

and even:

> **“Indeed in time to come - we may regret that we did not start this earlier.”**

That is the management problem in Neurology's own words:

> **levodopa may work very well;**
>
> it may later be regretted that it was not started earlier;**
>
> nevertheless, try to avoid it for as long as possible.**

### How the admission was converted into a settled plan

The later synthesis explains its own purpose.

It says it is unusually long partly because it is for the patient and is meant:

> **“to consolidate a lot of discussions which we have had.”**

It is intended to help:

> **“with debriefing”**

and:

> **“to set out for him a clear plan.”**

The prolonged admission is then characterised as successful:

> **“this has all suited us and Liam very well”**

because it allowed the team to:

> **“explore a lot of questions.”**

The longer admission is said to have:

> **“helped us have a firmer footing going forward for having a plan.”**

The patient is described as having:

> **“an exceptionally high cognitive baseline”**

and as someone who will:

> **“question very appropriately with exceptional insight.”**

Later:

> **“Liam has a good understanding of why we wished to delay the commencement of levodopa or dopamine agonist type treatments.”**

And the formal recommendations are simply:

> **“As per above.”**

That is the founding documentary state in its own language:

> **“Not clear” + Podiatry + levodopa deferred + “firmer footing” + “clear plan” + patient understanding + “As per above.”**

### What happened after the “clear plan”

Primary care then documented:

> **“Doing well on Procyclidine.”**

The improvement was substantial enough for restoration of function and return-to-work planning.

But when procyclidine was withdrawn, later Neurology recorded that the first rapidly returning physical feature included the outside-left-foot phenomenon; gait/tension deteriorated and the patient became more “shifty” and “unrelaxed.”

By September 2018 Brennan was documenting:

> **cramping in both feet**

alongside return of increased tone and previous:

> **“great improvement.”**

His concern was:

> **“we are losing ground here.”**

By 2019 Brennan wrote:

> **“feet issues in relating to increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease.”**

He added:

> **“Parkinsons at very young age has issues with ‘clawing feet’ and pain.”**

The same form records:

> **“No neuropathy.”**

Again, these are not our words.

The GP is himself reconnecting:

> **feet + tone + abnormal gait + Parkinson's disease.**

### By 2023 the institutional language becomes severe

During the March 2023 crisis Brennan described severe bilateral burning feet/lower-leg pain and wrote that it was:

> **“causing death wish.”**

He said:

> **“I am worried about him”**

and requested review:

> **“before we get into trouble here.”**

The Parkinson's nurse then recorded:

> **Lyrica 600 mg/day**

with:

> **“debilitating pain”**

and:

> **“no quality of life.”**

Then Brennan explicitly returned to 2017.

He described the severe bilateral foot pains as:

> **“likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone issues”**

because:

> **“this was actually his initial presentation to me now 6 years ago.”**

That sentence is devastatingly simple.

The GP who made the original referral is saying, six years later:

> **this severe feet problem is linked to Parkinsonian tone and this is where the case began.**

### Movement Disorders then used its own language

Later in 2023 the Movement Disorders record received severe foot pain together with reduced mobility and shuffling. The subsequent specialist interpretation was that the pain and shuffling appeared **OFF-related**, and Parkinson medication was altered. The record therefore no longer contained only a possible neuropathic explanation; it now contained a specialist dopaminergic interpretation of the same general territory.

Yet the original question was not replaced by an authoritative reconstructed account of:

> **what was structural, what was dystonic, what was OFF-related, what caused pain, and how those components interacted.**

### By 2025 Neurology knew Sinemet helped the feet

The March 2025 record says:

> **“He knows very well that once the Sinemet hits his system that the feet feel better from a pain perspective.”**

The same explanation says:

> **“Sinemet should not do anything for pain”**

while accepting that it will:

> **“relieve the experience of pain.”**

Monaghan's complaint response states that:

> **“we have had a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.”**

His explanation for reluctance to increase Sinemet was:

> **“the concerns of evolution of the dyskinesia”**

and the other risks of treating a younger patient.

So by 2025 the treating neurologist's own position contains both propositions:

> **Sinemet helps the feet/pain.**

and:

> **increasing Sinemet is constrained by dyskinesia.**

That is precisely the treatment trap anticipated in 2017.

### The original uncertainty is still present in 2025

Yet Monaghan still writes:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

That word matters:

> **“If.”**

Peripheral neuropathy is still available as a possible explanation.

It is not being stated as an established diagnosis.

So eight years after:

> **“Not clear”**

the treating neurologist can simultaneously acknowledge:

> **Sinemet helps the feet**

while retaining:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

That is the allegation in miniature.

### Then the institution itself describes Sinemet CR as analgesic

Quality & Patient Safety wrote to the patient in April 2025:

> **“You recently started Sinemet CR, which has been an effective analgesic for your foot pain.”**

That is important because the analgesic effect is no longer merely the patient's interpretation.

The hospital's complaint correspondence states it as part of the factual background.

Monaghan also apologised:

> **“I am truly very, very sorry that this has been Mr Moloney's experience.”**

and:

> **“I have realised the pain and discomfort that he has gone through.”**

Yet later institutional review concluded:

> **“there was no deficit in the care provided.”**

### The allegation, assembled from their words

Strip away nearly all of our language and the documentary allegation reads like this:

> The GP referred **“gait, feet and ‘muscle tension’ issues”**, later asking whether this was **“?parkinsonian or other underlying diagnosis.”**
>
> Neurology found a gait that was **“strikingly abnormal,”** with a footdrop appearance **“somewhat suggestive of a dystonic posture - NOT FOOT DROP,”** alongside **“Pes planus”** and **“Hammertoes.”**
>
> Neurology considered that the corns, calluses and burning **“may have been completely separate or perhaps a secondary peripheral neuropathy”** and concluded **“Not clear.”**
>
> The feet were sent to Podiatry.
>
> At the same time, Neurology decided **“We were likely try and avoid [levodopa/dopamine agonists] for as long as possible,”** while accepting that he might **“do very well with levodopa”** and that **“we may regret that we did not start this earlier.”**
>
> The admission was then represented as having **“suited us and Liam very well,”** giving **“a firmer footing going forward,”** producing **“a clear plan,”** with an exceptionally intelligent patient who **“question[ed] very appropriately with exceptional insight”** and had **“a good understanding”** of the treatment rationale.
>
> The formal plan became **“As per above.”**
>
> Thereafter neurological medication withdrawal rapidly altered a foot phenomenon and gait; the GP later recorded **“cramping in both feet”** while **“we are losing ground”;** then explicitly described the foot problems as related to **“increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease”** and **“clawing feet”** and pain.
>
> By 2023 severe bilateral foot pain was **“causing death wish”;** Brennan wrote **“I am worried about him”** and **“before we get into trouble here”;** Neurology received a patient on Lyrica 600 mg/day with **“debilitating pain”** and **“no quality of life.”**
>
> Brennan then wrote that the severe bilateral foot pains were **“likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone issues”** and that this was **“actually his initial presentation to me now 6 years ago.”**
>
> Movement Disorders later treated pain and shuffling as OFF-related.
>
> By 2025 Neurology said **“once the Sinemet hits his system that the feet feel better”** and that it had had **“a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.”**
>
> QPS itself described Sinemet CR as **“an effective analgesic for your foot pain.”**
>
> Yet the possible alternative explanation remained: **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**
>
> And the institutional conclusion was ultimately:
>
> **“there was no deficit in the care provided.”**

That is the allegation without calling it **anchoring**, **partition**, **path dependence**, **defensive reconstruction**, **information architecture**, or anything else.

The documents supply almost all of it themselves.

### The irreducible allegation in their own vocabulary

> **A patient referred with “gait, feet and muscle tension issues” entered Neurology. His gait was “strikingly abnormal” and “somewhat suggestive of a dystonic posture - NOT FOOT DROP”; he had pes planus and hammertoes; PRKN/PARK2 was considered. The painful feet were nevertheless left “Not clear” and sent to Podiatry while levodopa was to be avoided “for as long as possible.” The admission was said to have produced “firmer footing,” a “clear plan” and “As per above.” Over the following years the feet cramped with increased tone, were described by the GP as related to Parkinsonian tone and abnormal gait, became “debilitating” with “no quality of life,” were judged OFF-related, and became known to improve when Sinemet “hits his system.” Sinemet CR was eventually described by the hospital itself as “an effective analgesic for your foot pain.” Yet the original alternative remained only conditional — “If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…” — and the institutional conclusion was “no deficit in the care provided.”**

And perhaps the most powerful final line is simply:

> **The allegation is the distance between “Not clear” in 2017 and “we have had a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain” in 2025 — without an identified point at which the original unresolved feet were globally reconstructed.**

---


# Part VII — Meta-Documentary Reconstruction

<a id="profile-37"></a>

---

# Risk Rank 29 — Original Profile 34

**Severity:** MATERIALITY / HARM / FAIRNESS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Shows increasing foreseeability and predictability as later signals repeatedly reproduce the unresolved relationship.

## Profile 34 — Profile of Predictability

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-120121).md -->

<!-- Original source heading: Profile of Predictability -->

### How Much of the Later Course Was Already Visible?

The later severity of this case should not be described as though it arrived without warning.

Nor should hindsight be used unfairly.

No clinician in July 2017 could have predicted the exact dates, exact drugs, exact doses or exact severity of the crisis that would emerge years later.

Nobody could have known that in March 2023 the patient would be taking 600 mg/day of pregabalin while describing pain severe enough to produce a “death wish.”

Nobody could have predicted the exact sentence:

> **“the first 150 footsteps are torture.”**

But that is not the relevant test of predictability.

The relevant question is:

> **Was the kind of problem that eventually developed foreseeable from the information progressively available to the treating service?**

On the documentary record, the answer becomes increasingly difficult to avoid.

The later course was not predictable all at once.

It became predictable in stages.

And some of the most important elements were visible from the beginning.

### 1. July 2017 already contained the basic ingredients

The admission presented an unusually specific cluster:

- very-young-onset Parkinsonism;
- marked symmetry;
- severe akinetic-rigid disease;
- possible PRKN/PARK2;
- a lower-limb phenomenon resembling footdrop but expressly described as suggestive of dystonic posture;
- structural abnormality of the feet;
- longstanding painful feet;
- toe phenomena;
- and a deliberate intention to defer conventional dopaminergic treatment because of the expected long-term risk of dyskinesia.

Those facts did not predict eight years of severe pain.

But they did predict that the lower limbs and feet deserved particular longitudinal attention.

And they predicted a difficult therapeutic problem.

The July plan itself anticipated that conventional dopaminergic treatment might work very well while also producing serious long-term complications in such a young patient.

The service was therefore already planning for a future tension between:

> **benefit**

and:

> **treatment toxicity.**

That is not hindsight.

It is the management rationale recorded in 2017.

### 2. The departmental knowledge made the phenotype less surprising, not more

The Galway environment makes this particularly important.

The project record establishes that Timothy Counihan had co-authored the 2013 *JAMA Neurology* paper on parkin disease.

That literature described:

- juvenile/young-onset Parkinsonism;
- dystonia;
- **foot dystonia**;
- strong and sustained levodopa responsiveness;
- and a tendency toward early dyskinesia.

Within weeks of this patient's admission, Counihan, Patrick Browne and Galway colleagues published the clinical-reasoning case:

> **“A demure teenager and her dystonic foot.”**

That case involved:

> apparent footdrop → recognition of dystonia → parkin mutations → procyclidine treatment → discussion of strong levodopa responsiveness and dyskinesia risk.

The point is not that the two patients were identical.

They were not.

The point is much narrower:

> **The conceptual combination of young-onset Parkinsonism + parkin disease + dystonic foot phenomenology + strong treatment response + dyskinesia difficulty was unusually familiar within the clinical environment receiving this patient.**

So the possibility that the feet might become an important expression of the neurological disorder should not be treated as an exotic insight available only retrospectively.

### 3. The original management strategy itself predicted the later treatment trap

One of the most revealing features of the case is that Neurology correctly anticipated a major future problem.

The clinicians were worried that exposing such a young patient to levodopa or dopamine agonists would eventually produce:

- dyskinesia;
- impulse-control problems;
- punding;
- and other long-term complications.

They therefore intended to avoid those therapies:

> **“for as long as possible.”**

That concern later proved well founded.

Neupro produced behavioural/stimulation problems and choreiform movement.

When Sinemet was eventually introduced, chorea appeared at relatively low doses.

Stalevo 75 later produced obvious dyskinesia.

By 2023 the GP described a:

> **“very narrow window”**

between controlling stiffness/bradykinesia and producing dyskinesia.

Thus one major feature of the later course was not merely predictable.

It was **predicted**.

The clinicians knew from the beginning that successful dopaminergic treatment might become difficult to sustain.

What appears not to have received equivalent forward planning was the implication of that strategy for the unresolved painful feet.

### 4. Within months, treatment responsiveness made the neurological relevance harder to miss

The uncertainty changed dramatically once treatment began.

Procyclidine produced major improvement.

The GP recorded:

> **“Doing well on Procyclidine.”**

Function returned.

The patient later described the state as:

> **“living in a miracle, literally.”**

Then came the planned withdrawal.

Eyesight improved.

But:

> **“my feet are restless and my walk is falling apart.”**

By April 2018 Neurology itself recorded that the **outside-left-foot phenomenon was the first thing to return rapidly** when procyclidine was stopped, together with broader loss of relaxation and worsening motor state.

This is the first major predictability threshold.

By that point, the service did not merely have a theoretical possibility that some lower-limb phenomena were neurological.

It had a treatment withdrawal/rechallenge observation in the relevant anatomical territory.

From that moment forward, future deterioration of the feet during worsening neurological control should at least have been an intelligible possibility.

### 5. September 2018 supplies the next warning

Brennan then sees the same relationship again.

He records:

- return of increased neurological tone;
- deterioration in arm/hand function;
- **cramping in both feet**;
- previous “great improvement”;
- inability comfortably to increase procyclidine because of vision and concentration effects.

His conclusion is:

> **“we are losing ground here.”**

And he asks Movement Disorders for help.

That is an extraordinarily informative moment.

The future problem is already visible in miniature:

> **effective neurological treatment exists;**

> **the feet change with neurological state;**

> **the effective treatment has a ceiling;**

> **symptoms begin returning because treatment cannot simply be increased.**

That is essentially the architecture of the later crisis.

The later drugs change.

The underlying problem does not.

### 6. By 2019 primary care was saying explicitly what the future record would repeatedly rediscover

Brennan's 2019 Podiatry referral described the foot problems as relating to:

> **increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease**

and wrote that young-onset Parkinsonism involved:

> **“clawing feet” and pain.**

The form also recorded:

> **“No neuropathy.”**

This does not prove that all pain was neurological.

But it means that, by 2019, a clinician with continuous knowledge from before specialist diagnosis was already documenting the integrated relationship explicitly.

The later reappearance of:

- foot cramping;
- dystonic possibilities;
- OFF-related pain;
- shuffling;
- toe curling;
- and levodopa-responsive pain

was therefore not the emergence of an entirely new idea.

It was the repeated return of a relationship already being observed.

### 7. The treatment course itself became increasingly predictive

Between 2018 and 2021 the medication history produced another series of warnings.

#### Procyclidine

Strong benefit.

But vision, cognition and eventually intraocular-pressure concerns restricted it.

#### Amantadine

No useful benefit and “quite sad thoughts.”

#### Neupro

Motor benefit.

But artificial stimulation, compulsive/impulsive phenomena and choreiform dyskinesia.

Neurology described the situation as:

> **“an extremely, extremely difficult tight rope.”**

#### Comtess

By 2019 the department was using an expressly:

> **“not commonly done”**

Comtess regimen while the patient was not on Sinemet or Madopar, apparently attempting to preserve useful benefit within the continuing pre-levodopa strategy.

This history progressively tells the same story:

> **the patient is responsive, but the usual routes to greater treatment are constrained.**

That is exactly the kind of patient in whom an unresolved symptom capable of worsening with treatment state becomes particularly important.

Because if it becomes severe, there may not be an easy pharmacological escape.

### 8. By 2021 the predicted dyskinesia problem actually arrived

Sinemet was finally introduced in September 2021.

Within weeks:

- it had been increased;
- Kemadrin was being removed because of ocular problems;
- Comtess was already present;
- and slight chorea had appeared.

By 2022 Stalevo 75 had produced:

> **obvious chorea/dyskinesia.**

This is another major threshold.

The theoretical 2017 concern had now become observed clinical fact.

The patient had:

> **a treatment-responsive neurological disorder**

but also:

> **an increasingly narrow ability to increase the treatment.**

From here, a future OFF-related pain or dystonia problem was potentially much harder to manage than it would have been in a patient with a broad therapeutic window.

### 9. By March 2023 the severe crisis was not predictable only in hindsight

The exact severity was extraordinary.

But the mechanism of risk had been building for years.

Immediately before the crisis:

- another GP physically returned to the feet;
- the proposed calcaneal/bony explanation was not demonstrated by X-ray;
- the patient reported extreme worsening;
- pregabalin was rapidly escalated;
- Brennan recalled previous pain settling when Parkinson's control improved;
- and Brennan explicitly connected the current severe bilateral foot pains to Parkinsonian tone and the original 2017 presentation.

He then warned:

> **“I am worried about him”**

and requested review:

> **“before we get into trouble here.”**

Two days later Neurology had a patient on **600 mg/day Lyrica** with debilitating pain and:

> **“no quality of life.”**

At that point nobody had to predict a future crisis.

The crisis had arrived.

But Brennan's language is important.

> **“before we get into trouble here”**

shows that primary care itself understood the trajectory as dangerous while it was developing.

### 10. The 2023 Movement Disorders interpretation makes the later Sinemet response increasingly unsurprising

Later that year the patient again presented using the inherited language of severe “neuropathic” pain.

The Movement Disorders clinician looked at the actual state:

- shuffling;
- poor mobility;
- morning worsening;
- later worsening when Sinemet had not been taken.

The conclusion was:

> **“It sounds like his Parkinson's is being undertreated and the leg pain and shuffling is off related.”**

Additional Parkinson medication was proposed and agreed.

This is important to predictability.

By late 2023 a Movement Disorders clinician had already identified the pain/mobility state as dopaminergic/OFF-related.

So when the 2025 record eventually says that Sinemet improves the feet, that is not a bolt from the blue.

It is increasingly consistent with an established chain of observations.

### 11. By 2025 “profound relief” was dramatic, but not wholly surprising

The March 2025 note records:

- “murder in the feet”;
- first 150 footsteps “torture”;
- prior maximum-dose Lyrica failing to eliminate the pain;
- feet improving once Sinemet reached the system;
- expectation that more Sinemet would provide more pain relief;
- and inability simply to increase it because dyskinesia would become worse.

Controlled-release Sinemet was then introduced.

Within weeks Monaghan described:

> **“profound relief.”**

The magnitude of that response may have been striking.

But by 2025 the *direction* of the response was not unforeseen.

Neurology itself recorded that it had understood for some time that Sinemet helped the pain. The 2025 complaint response explicitly says:

> **“we have had a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.”**

It also re-adopts the long-standing effort to spare Sinemet escalation because of dyskinesia.

Thus the final contradiction is not:

> nobody could possibly have expected levodopa to help the feet.

It is closer to:

> **the service expected levodopa to help, knew increasing levodopa would probably help more, and simultaneously knew that dyskinesia prevented easy escalation.**

That is the mature form of the problem first visible years earlier.

### 12. The later crisis follows the shape of the original warning almost exactly

Put 2017 and 2025 beside each other.

#### 2017

The service anticipates:

> **young patient + highly treatment-responsive Parkinsonian syndrome + substantial dyskinesia risk → use great caution with dopaminergic therapy.**

At the same time:

> **painful feet + dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenon + structural pathology → relationship “Not clear.”**

#### 2025

The service has:

> **foot pain improved by Sinemet + expectation of further pain benefit from more Sinemet + inability comfortably to increase Sinemet because of severe dyskinesia.**

That is an extraordinary symmetry.

The very complication used to justify prolonged caution in 2017 becomes the complication preventing straightforward treatment of the severe foot problem by 2025.

The broad shape of the later therapeutic trap was therefore present in the original plan itself.

What was missing was not foresight about dyskinesia.

It was equivalent foresight about what would happen if the unresolved feet later proved to be treatment-state dependent.

### 13. The question is therefore not “could they predict catastrophic pain?”

That would set an unrealistic standard.

A fairer question is:

> **Given a very young patient with suspected PRKN, dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenology, painful abnormal feet, demonstrated dramatic neurological responsiveness, early medication-dependent foot changes, recurrent bilateral foot cramping with increasing tone and progressively narrowing treatment options, was it foreseeable that failure to maintain the feet as an active integrated neurological question could become clinically consequential?**

By 2018, that question is strong.

By 2019, stronger.

By 2021, when the dyskinesia ceiling actually emerged, stronger again.

By March 2023, it no longer concerns prediction.

The foreseeable risk has materialised.

### 14. Predictability increased with every failed opportunity to reconstruct

The case therefore has a **predictability staircase**.

#### July 2017 — plausible risk

The relevant phenotype and treatment problem are already present.

> **Young onset + possible PRKN + dystonic-looking foot/lower-limb phenomenon + structural painful feet + planned dopaminergic restraint.**

#### Late 2017 / April 2018 — direct signal

Neurological treatment transforms function.

Withdrawal makes:

> **feet restless + gait fall apart**

and the outside-left-foot phenomenon returns rapidly.

#### September 2018 — explicit deterioration

> **bilateral foot cramping + increased tone + treatment ceiling + “we are losing ground.”**

#### 2019 — longitudinal integration

GP explicitly links:

> **feet + clawing + pain + increased tone + abnormal Parkinsonian gait.**

#### 2021–2022 — therapeutic ceiling confirmed

Levodopa works but dyskinesia appears at low levels.

#### March 2023 — risk becomes crisis

> **severe bilateral pain + 600 mg pregabalin + “death wish” + “no quality of life.”**

Brennan links it explicitly to Parkinsonian tone and the original presentation.

#### Late 2023 — specialist local reconstruction

Movement Disorders calls the pain/shuffling:

> **OFF-related.**

#### March 2025 — mature contradiction

Sinemet already known to improve the feet.

More would likely help more.

Dyskinesia prevents straightforward escalation.

Sinemet CR then produces:

> **“profound relief.”**

At every stage the later course becomes less surprising.

### 15. What was predictable and what was not

The distinction should remain disciplined.

#### Not reasonably predictable from July 2017 alone

- the precise future severity of pain;
- the March 2023 crisis;
- the exact pregabalin dose;
- the exact pattern of adverse effects;
- the timing of levodopa introduction;
- the exact eventual Sinemet CR response;
- every downstream psychological or functional consequence.

#### Clearly anticipated in 2017

- a difficult long-term treatment course in a very young patient;
- substantial expected dopaminergic responsiveness;
- major concern about early dyskinesia;
- the possibility of PRKN disease;
- dystonic lower-limb phenomenology.

#### Increasingly foreseeable by 2018–2019

- that at least part of the foot/lower-limb phenotype changed with neurological treatment state;
- that feet and increased tone could deteriorate together;
- that treatment capable of improving the neurological state was already limited by adverse effects;
- that the feet remained part of the Parkinsonian/tone/gait story in primary care.

#### Established rather than merely foreseeable by 2021–2023

- the dopaminergic therapeutic window was genuinely narrow;
- dyskinesia materially constrained escalation;
- severe pain could coexist with undertreatment/OFF state;
- Parkinsonian control could affect pain;
- extreme foot pain could become a major clinical crisis.

### The most important predictability failure

The strongest question may ultimately not concern prediction of symptoms at all.

It concerns **prediction of information need**.

In July 2017 the clinicians deliberately chose a strategy that they already knew would make future treatment unusually difficult:

> **delay and minimise dopaminergic exposure because the patient is young and likely to develop dyskinesia.**

If that is the strategy, then any unresolved symptom complex potentially linked to neurological treatment state becomes especially important to characterise and preserve longitudinally.

Because once the patient develops dyskinesia, the service may no longer possess the luxury of simply increasing dopaminergic treatment to determine whether the unresolved symptoms improve.

That future constraint was foreseeable from the beginning.

Indeed, it was the reason for the treatment strategy.

So the central predictability question becomes:

> **If Neurology could foresee the future dyskinesia problem clearly enough to build the entire treatment strategy around avoiding it, why was comparable foresight not applied to preserving and resolving the painful foot/lower-limb question before that therapeutic window became narrow?**

That is much stronger than saying:

> they should have predicted eight years of pain.

They did not need to predict the pain.

They needed to recognise that **an unresolved lower-limb problem in a highly responsive young patient was going to become much harder to interrogate once the very complication they already feared appeared.**

And that complication did appear.

### Profile in one paragraph

> **The later course was not predictable in every detail, but its underlying architecture became foreseeable remarkably early. In July 2017 Galway Neurology already had a very-young-onset, relatively symmetrical Parkinsonian syndrome with PRKN under consideration, a dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop, painful structural feet and a treatment strategy expressly designed around the expectation that dopaminergic therapy would work but create major dyskinesia problems in such a young patient. The department's own academic environment was unusually familiar with parkin disease, foot dystonia, strong treatment responsiveness and early dyskinesia. Within months the patient demonstrated dramatic neurological improvement; withdrawal of procyclidine then made his feet restless and his walk fall apart, and Neurology itself recorded rapid return of an outside-left-foot phenomenon. By September 2018 Brennan was reporting bilateral foot cramping with increased tone, treatment toxicity and “we are losing ground”; by 2019 he explicitly linked clawing feet and pain to Parkinsonian tone and gait. When levodopa finally arrived, the predicted dyskinesia ceiling appeared rapidly. By March 2023 the patient had reached severe bilateral foot pain despite 600 mg/day pregabalin, with “death wish,” “I am worried about him” and “no quality of life”; Brennan explicitly tied that crisis to Parkinsonian tone and the original presentation. Later in 2023 Movement Disorders classified pain and shuffling as OFF-related. By March 2025 Neurology already knew Sinemet improved the feet, expected more Sinemet to provide more pain relief, but could not freely escalate because of dyskinesia; controlled-release Sinemet then produced “profound relief.” The exact crisis could not have been predicted in 2017. The trajectory—neurologically modifiable feet inside an increasingly narrow therapeutic window—became progressively difficult not to foresee.**

### The shortest version

> **They could not have predicted exactly how bad it would become. But they predicted the very treatment limitation that eventually made it so difficult to treat, while the feet were already supplying the warning signs that would later become the crisis.**

And I think the hardest closing sentence is:

> **The tragedy is not that the future was perfectly knowable in 2017. It is that each year made it more knowable, while the original information architecture made knowing more compatible with not reopening the original question.**

---

# Part VI — Case Synthesis and Allegation

# Part VI — Case Synthesis and Allegation

<a id="profile-35"></a>

---

# Risk Rank 30 — Original Profile 32

**Severity:** MATERIALITY / HARM / FAIRNESS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Documents improvement, loss of ground and eventual severity, strengthening consequence and longitudinal materiality.

## Profile 32 — Profile of Improvement, Loss of Ground and Eventual Severity

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-115850).md -->

<!-- Original source heading: Profile of Improvement, Loss of Ground and Eventual Severity -->

### How the Case Got Better Before It Became Much Worse

The longitudinal record should not be read as though the patient left hospital in July 2017 and simply deteriorated for eight years.

That did not happen.

For a period after the admission, **the case got dramatically better**.

That fact is essential.

It shows that the neurological syndrome was highly treatment-responsive, that substantial function could be restored, and that later deterioration occurred against the memory and evidence of what successful treatment had once achieved.

The later severity is therefore not simply the natural continuation of the pre-2017 state.

The trajectory is:

> **severe presentation → dramatic recovery → emerging treatment limits → repeated attempts to preserve benefit → progressively narrower therapeutic window → severe recurrent foot pain → major treatment burden → catastrophic crisis.**

### 1. July 2017 began from substantial disability

Before admission, the patient was already deteriorating significantly.

Brennan's referral described:

- abnormal stiff/festinant gait;
- difficulty initiating movement;
- marked increased tone;
- inability to relax;
- toe-clawing;
- deteriorating dexterity;
- difficulty performing simple repetitive manual tasks;
- and impending occupational failure.

Brennan was sufficiently concerned that without urgent assessment the patient could lose his job.

Neurology then established severe young-onset Parkinsonism.

So the starting point was serious.

But it was not irreversible.

### 2. Treatment initially worked spectacularly

The early response to rasagiline and particularly procyclidine was not marginal.

By September 2017 Brennan recorded:

> **“Doing well on Procyclidine.”**

The patient did not even want to continue escalating the dose while he was doing well.

By November symptom control was described as good and return to work was being planned.

The patient's own description of this period is much stronger.

He described himself as:

> **“living in a miracle, literally.”**

The surviving reconstruction records him running, jumping, hopping and skipping.

By April 2018 Neurology recorded:

- **fantastic relief from tension**;
- good gait;
- improved bed mobility;
- low anxiety;
- and substantial overall benefit from the regimen.

This is a critical point in the case.

> **The patient demonstrated that a large part of his disability was neurologically modifiable.**

He did not merely report feeling a little better.

Movement and function were substantially restored.

### 3. The improvement produced its own experiment

The first sign of the later difficulty appeared because successful treatment could not simply be continued without cost.

Procyclidine caused visual problems.

It was therefore withdrawn temporarily.

When it was stopped, eyesight improved.

But the neurological state deteriorated.

The patient reported:

> **“my feet are restless and my walk is falling apart.”**

By April 2018 Neurology itself recorded that one of the first physical phenomena to return rapidly was the abnormality at the outside of the left foot, accompanied by increased shiftiness and loss of relaxation.

This is important because deterioration was already appearing through **the same territory that had been left unresolved in 2017**.

The early story was therefore not:

> treatment works, then disease simply progresses.

It was:

> **treatment works extremely well, but the effective treatment itself has limits.**

That distinction governs much of what follows.

### 4. By September 2018, Brennan could see benefit and recurrence together

Brennan's September 2018 communication is almost the perfect transitional document.

He remembered the earlier:

> **“great improvement”**

but now saw:

- increased tone returning;
- arm and hand difficulty;
- bilateral foot cramping;
- visual problems;
- concentration problems;
- and uncertainty about how much further procyclidine could safely be pushed.

His conclusion was:

> **“we are losing ground here.”**

That phrase marks the transition.

The patient had proved that improvement was possible.

Now the question became whether improvement could be sustained.

### 5. The problem became a therapeutic tightrope

The following treatment trials show repeated attempts to preserve the gains without creating unacceptable new problems.

#### Amantadine

It did not provide useful benefit and was associated with:

> **“quite sad thoughts.”**

#### Neupro / rotigotine

It could improve mobility considerably.

But at higher doses it produced:

- artificial or amphetamine-like stimulation;
- dread as the effect faded;
- impulsive/compulsive behaviour;
- and choreiform dyskinesia.

Neurology's own description was:

> **“an extremely, extremely difficult tight rope.”**

That is the real shape of the middle years.

The problem was no longer simply finding a drug that worked.

Several treatments worked.

The problem was finding a level of treatment at which the benefit did not produce another form of disability.

### 6. Even during these difficult years, the patient was not uniformly deteriorating

This should be preserved carefully.

There were periods in which physical status was relatively good.

By 2020 the record could describe meaningful physical improvement.

In 2022 walking and activity could again be generally good on a low-dose dopaminergic regimen.

This is why a simple “eight years of continuous decline” narrative would be inaccurate.

The more disturbing trajectory is:

> **repeated recovery followed by loss of sustainable treatment space.**

Each time treatment found benefit, another ceiling appeared.

Anticholinergic treatment:

> major benefit → visual/cognitive ceiling.

Dopamine agonist:

> mobility benefit → behavioural/dyskinesia ceiling.

Levodopa:

> motor and pain benefit → dyskinesia ceiling.

Pregabalin:

> pain benefit → incomplete control even at very high dose.

The therapeutic window became progressively narrower.

### 7. Levodopa arrived into an already difficult treatment landscape

The first recorded Sinemet treatment appears in September 2021.

It did not produce a simple solution.

By October it had already been increased while slight chorea was appearing.

In 2022 Stalevo 75 produced obvious chorea/dyskinesia, while even lower-dose regimens produced some excessive movement.

Thus the original concern about dyskinesia was real.

But its practical consequence was serious:

> **the drug capable of improving function could not simply be increased until the patient was comfortable.**

The case was becoming trapped between two undesirable states:

> **too little dopaminergic effect → stiffness, OFF state, poor gait and pain**

and:

> **more dopaminergic effect → dyskinesia/chorea and over-treatment.**

That narrow window becomes essential to understanding how severe the later pain crisis became.

### 8. March 2023 was not ordinary deterioration — it was a crisis

By March 2023 the feet had become severely painful again.

The practice reopened the physical question.

Bilateral X-rays did not demonstrate the particular bony explanation being considered.

Pregabalin was started and rapidly escalated.

The dose ultimately reached a documented:

> **600 mg/day.**

But the patient's distress was escalating faster than treatment could contain it.

His message to the GP practice said:

> **“When I say me feet are killing me I mean that literally.”**

and:

> **“I'm losing the will to live with them at this stage.”**

Brennan's urgent referral described the pain as:

> **“causing death wish”**

and stated:

> **“I am worried about him.”**

The Parkinson's nurse then recorded that despite high-dose Lyrica the patient had:

> **debilitating pain**

and:

> **“no quality of life.”**

Those phrases are important because they fix the severity contemporaneously.

This was not a retrospective description invented after the dispute began.

By March 2023 the clinical record itself was documenting a patient in extreme distress.

### 9. And the crisis occurred despite treatment, not because nothing was being done

This is another important distinction.

By this stage there was significant medical intervention.

Pregabalin had escalated from **25 mg twice daily** to **600 mg/day**.

Dopaminergic treatment was already present.

Neurology was adjusting Stalevo and Sinemet.

Primary care was prescribing, reconciling and escalating.

The problem was therefore not simple therapeutic neglect.

It was:

> **a patient receiving increasingly substantial treatment while remaining severely symptomatic and while the treatments capable of helping him were themselves limited by adverse effects.**

Neurology recognised that more evening levodopa could improve walking and pain.

But more levodopa was expected to produce more chorea.

The patient had reached a state in which:

> **the symptom burden demanded more treatment than the therapeutic window comfortably allowed.**

### 10. Even maximum-dose pregabalin did not close the problem

Pregabalin did help.

That needs to be preserved.

At later points it significantly reduced pain, especially in the right foot.

But the record also establishes its limit.

The treatment-burden timeline records:

- 600 mg/day exposure;
- debilitating pain during the 2023 escalation;
- incomplete effect;
- persistent toe curling and functional restriction;
- and eventual agreement that even the maximum dose had not eliminated the pain.

Reduction then became frightening.

When a decrease was proposed, the patient feared the return of the catastrophic pain state.

So the treatment itself became part of the lived burden:

> **high dose did not completely solve the problem, but reducing it risked exposing something the patient had become terrified of experiencing again.**

### 11. By 2024 the crisis had become chronic restriction

The 2024 record is quieter than March 2023, but that should not be mistaken for recovery.

Lyrica remained at **150 mg four times daily**.

The right-foot pain was much better.

But:

- left-foot pain remained;
- toe curling remained;
- fatigue remained;
- dyskinesia continued to restrict treatment;
- and Neurology described the patient's life as:

> **“a lot smaller.”**

That phrase is almost more revealing than a pain score.

The patient's world had contracted.

The issue was no longer merely whether he could survive a pain crisis.

It was how much ordinary life remained available inside the treatment compromise.

### 12. Reduction of Lyrica then exposed how severe the underlying problem still was

By March 2025 pregabalin had fallen from the previous 600 mg/day to **50 mg three times daily**.

The feet immediately dominate the clinical encounter.

The patient's descriptions are stark:

> **“murder in the feet”**

> **“the first 150 footsteps are torture”**

and:

> **“I won't live with it.”**

This is the mature severity of the case.

The patient had once described himself as:

> **“living in a miracle.”**

Now the first 150 steps of the morning were:

> **“torture.”**

Those two quotations belong opposite each other.

They define the trajectory better than any abstract severity scale.

### 13. Neurology could also see the treatment trap by 2025

The March 2025 clinic record contains several facts in one place:

- foot pain had become the principal issue;
- previous maximum Lyrica had not eliminated it;
- Sinemet made the feet feel better;
- more Sinemet would probably provide more pain relief;
- but more Sinemet would produce substantially worse dyskinesia.

Primary care therefore received a specialist document containing the full late-stage equation:

> **extreme foot pain + incomplete pregabalin control + dopaminergic pain response + dyskinesia ceiling.**

That is very different from the patient's position in 2017.

The problem had become pharmacologically and functionally severe.

### 14. Then Sinemet CR produced a striking reversal

Half-Sinemet CR was introduced overnight on 10 March 2025.

By 25 March Monaghan described the effect as:

> **“profound relief.”**

That response is important partly because it mirrors the beginning.

In 2017:

> **“living in a miracle.”**

In 2025:

> **“profound relief.”**

The case contains two striking therapeutic recoveries, separated by years of increasingly constrained treatment.

That means the historical story is not:

> an untreatable disease steadily became more severe.

It is much closer to:

> **a highly treatment-responsive disorder repeatedly encountered treatment ceilings, and the patient's life became increasingly constrained by the difficulty of sustaining adequate benefit without unacceptable adverse effects.**

### 15. The later evidence makes the contrast even sharper

The treatment timeline continues beyond the eight-year period.

By February 2026 the patient reported:

> **the pain was “totally gone”**

while underlying dystonic tone remained.

Sinemet CR was keeping the pain away and the gait was strengthening, even though another component of the neurological syndrome remained active.

That later separation is valuable because it shows once again that severe pain and severe neurological tone were not simply identical phenomena.

The state could be altered.

The pain could disappear while the underlying tone persisted.

### What the trajectory really looks like

The case therefore has four distinct phases.

### Phase 1 — Rescue

**2017–early 2018**

Severe impairment is followed by dramatic treatment response.

- gait improves;
- movement returns;
- function returns;
- return to work becomes possible;
- the patient describes himself as **“living in a miracle.”**

### Phase 2 — Loss of sustainable treatment space

**2018–2022**

Benefit remains possible, but treatment ceilings accumulate.

- visual and cognitive toxicity;
- bilateral foot cramping;
- “losing ground”;
- amantadine failure;
- behavioural and dyskinetic problems with Neupro;
- first levodopa;
- early chorea;
- Stalevo over-effect.

Neurology describes the problem as an:

> **“extremely, extremely difficult tight rope.”**

### Phase 3 — Severe pain and life contraction

**2023–2024**

The feet become catastrophic.

- “losing the will to live”;
- “death wish”;
- “I am worried about him”;
- 600 mg/day pregabalin;
- debilitating pain;
- “no quality of life”;
- OFF-related pain/shuffling recognised;
- persistent toe curling;
- fatigue;
- life **“a lot smaller.”**

### Phase 4 — Crisis and dramatic relief

**March 2025**

Pain again becomes dominant after pregabalin reduction.

- “murder in the feet”;
- “first 150 footsteps are torture”;
- “I won't live with it”;
- Sinemet known to improve the feet;
- more Sinemet constrained by dyskinesia;
- controlled-release Sinemet introduced;
- **“profound relief.”**

### The severity should be allowed to speak for itself

There is no need to exaggerate this chronology.

The contemporary language is already severe enough.

The record progresses from:

> **“Doing well.”**

to:

> **“living in a miracle.”**

then:

> **“great improvement.”**

then:

> **“we are losing ground.”**

then:

> **“an extremely, extremely difficult tight rope.”**

then:

> **“losing the will to live.”**

then:

> **“death wish.”**

then:

> **“no quality of life.”**

then:

> **“a lot smaller.”**

then:

> **“murder in the feet.”**

then:

> **“the first 150 footsteps are torture.”**

then:

> **“I won't live with it.”**

and finally:

> **“profound relief.”**

That is the clinical arc.

### Why the early improvement is so important to the case

Without the early recovery, later severity could be described simply as progression of a difficult neurological disease.

But the early response demonstrates something important:

> **the patient's function was capable of being restored to an extraordinary degree.**

The later record then repeatedly shows the limiting problem was not absence of treatment responsiveness.

It was the difficulty of **sustaining enough effective treatment within increasingly restrictive adverse-effect ceilings**.

The patient's life did not simply deteriorate because nothing worked.

In some ways, the history is worse than that.

> **Things worked. Sometimes they worked extraordinarily well. But the therapeutic architecture repeatedly failed to preserve the improvement without extracting another price.**

That is why the later severity is so striking.

### Profile in one paragraph

> **The post-2017 course was not one of uninterrupted decline. Initial neurological treatment produced extraordinary recovery: Brennan recorded that the patient was doing well, function returned sufficiently for return-to-work planning, Neurology later documented fantastic relief of tension and good gait, and the patient described himself as “living in a miracle, literally.” The problem then became sustainability. Procyclidine benefit was limited by visual and cognitive effects; withdrawal rapidly worsened the feet and gait; by September 2018 bilateral foot cramping and increased tone led Brennan to warn that “we are losing ground”; amantadine failed with “quite sad thoughts”; Neupro improved mobility but produced artificial stimulation, compulsive behaviour and choreiform dyskinesia; and Neurology described treatment as an “extremely, extremely difficult tight rope.” Levodopa eventually improved function but itself produced dyskinesia at relatively low doses. By March 2023 the feet had become catastrophically painful: the patient said he was “losing the will to live,” Brennan described a “death wish” and wrote “I am worried about him,” and the Parkinson's nurse recorded debilitating pain and “no quality of life” despite pregabalin escalating to 600 mg/day. Partial analgesic improvement followed, but toe curling, fatigue and treatment constraints persisted, and by 2024 life was described as “a lot smaller.” After pregabalin reduction, March 2025 exposed the underlying severity again: “murder in the feet,” “the first 150 footsteps are torture,” and “I won't live with it.” Yet the feet were known to improve when Sinemet reached the system, and controlled-release Sinemet then produced what Monaghan described as “profound relief.” The defining trajectory is therefore not simple deterioration but dramatic responsiveness followed by progressive loss of sustainable treatment space, culminating in severe pain, profound restriction of life and a final demonstration that major relief remained physiologically achievable.**

### The shortest version

> **He did not simply get worse. He first got extraordinarily better. Then the treatments that made him better became progressively harder to use, until a man who had once described himself as “living in a miracle” was describing his first 150 steps as “torture.”**

# Risk Rank 31 — Original Profile 33

**Severity:** MATERIALITY / HARM / FAIRNESS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Documents pain, distress and safeguarding consequences once the unresolved pathway produced severe functional burden.

## Profile 33 — Pain, Psychological Distress and Safeguarding

<!-- SOURCE: synthesis from the existing GP, treatment-burden and severity profiles, supplemented by the treatment-formulation reconstruction and 2026 St Vincent's correspondence reviewed 1 September 2026. -->

## How a modifiable foot problem became a prolonged safety problem

This profile asks two related but distinct questions:

> **Why did the foot pain become severe enough to generate prolonged psychological danger?**

and:

> **What safeguarding response surrounded that deterioration?**

The questions should not be collapsed.

The record demonstrates significant concern and active intervention, especially in primary care.

It also demonstrates that the dangerous psychological state persisted around a painful problem whose neurological and treatment-state dependence was becoming increasingly visible.

### 1. March 2023 marks the transition from pain problem to safety problem

By March 2023 the feet were no longer simply troublesome.

The patient wrote:

> **“When I say me feet are killing me I mean that literally.”**

and:

> **“I'm losing the will to live with them at this stage.”**

Brennan's urgent referral described the pain as:

> **“causing death wish”**

and stated:

> **“I am worried about him.”**

He sought specialist help:

> **“before we get into trouble here.”**

The Parkinson's service then recorded debilitating pain despite **600 mg/day pregabalin** and effectively:

> **“no quality of life.”**

Those are safeguarding facts, not merely severity adjectives.

### 2. The response was not “nothing was done”

The record shows active primary-care and specialist intervention.

There was:

- clinical reassessment;
- bilateral foot imaging;
- pregabalin initiation and rapid escalation;
- urgent GP referral;
- specialist contact;
- medication reconciliation and adjustment;
- continuing neurological treatment;
- later attempts to reduce treatment burden;
- and later psychological/supportive involvement in the broader pathway.

A fair safeguarding profile must preserve that.

The allegation should not be:

> **nobody noticed or nobody acted.**

The harder question is:

> **Did the response acquire sustained ownership proportionate to a recurrent, pain-driven safety problem?**

### 3. The danger did not end when the acute March 2023 episode passed

High-dose pregabalin substantially reduced some of the pain, but it did not eliminate the underlying problem.

The record preserves:

- persistent left-foot pain;
- persistent toe curling;
- fatigue and treatment burden;
- fear of reducing pregabalin because of the remembered severity of the pain;
- and by 2024 a life described as:

> **“a lot smaller.”**

The patient was therefore not simply rescued from one isolated crisis.

The clinical problem became chronic restriction around fear of recurrence and incomplete control.

### 4. March 2025 shows recurrence of the safety signal

After pregabalin had been reduced from the previous 600 mg/day to **50 mg three times daily**, the feet again dominated the clinical encounter.

The language is stark:

> **“murder in the feet”**

> **“the first 150 footsteps are torture”**

> **“I won't live with it.”**

The significance is not that every such statement proves a fixed suicidal intention.

It is that the record contains recurrent death-related language over a prolonged period in direct association with severe physical pain.

That requires safeguarding interpretation as well as pain interpretation.

### 5. The psychological distress cannot fairly be detached from the physical treatment problem

The record contains other psychological and behavioural medication effects across the years.

But the 2023–25 crisis language repeatedly appears in the context of severe foot pain, fear of its return, shrinking function and treatment instability.

The relevant structure is therefore:

> **pain burden + fear of recurrence + restricted treatment options + repeated treatment-state fluctuation → psychological danger.**

Treating the distress as an unrelated psychological problem would reproduce the same fragmentation criticised elsewhere in the case.

The pain and the safety state need to be understood together.

### 6. Why did the pain become so severe?

The earlier booklet used the shorthand:

> **dopaminergic benefit versus dyskinesia ceiling.**

The later formulation history requires that to be refined.

The severe pain developed inside a treatment architecture in which:

- the focal foot territory had already shown neurological treatment-state dependence on procyclidine dechallenge;
- immediate-release and other pulsatile dopaminergic regimens could produce unstable peaks and troughs;
- under-medicated states were associated with tension, toe curling and painful foot phenomena;
- excessive peaks could produce dyskinesia;
- pregabalin was escalated to 600 mg/day yet remained incomplete and burdensome;
- and the relationship between formulation, timing, dystonia, pain and the focal structural foot was not visibly reconstructed as one longitudinal mechanism.

So the pain did not simply become severe because the underlying disease inexorably worsened.

A more coherent reconstruction is:

> **a structurally vulnerable foot phenotype remained dynamically sensitive to neurological state while the treatment strategy repeatedly oscillated between inadequate coverage and adverse treatment effects, and pain-directed medication was layered on without fully resolving the treatment-state mechanism.**

### 7. The later formulation history changes the retrospective safeguarding question

In March 2025 the record already says:

- the feet improve once Sinemet reaches the system;
- additional Sinemet would probably provide additional pain relief;
- but the current pattern of treatment also produces troublesome dyskinesia.

Controlled-release Sinemet is then introduced and produces:

> **“profound relief.”**

By 2026 the treatment architecture has changed further toward sustained CR coverage with small Madopar doses used strategically for residual gaps.

The longstanding disabling foot pain is described as no longer a significant issue, while the former problematic dyskinetic state is also no longer dominating the clinical picture.

That later outcome matters to safeguarding because it demonstrates that the 2023–25 trade-off was not necessarily immutable.

The dangerous symptom was modifiable.

The treatment adverse effect was also modifiable.

The formulation and timing mattered.

### 8. The safeguarding question therefore becomes more exact

The relevant retrospective question is not:

> **Why did nobody simply give more levodopa?**

Nor is it:

> **Why was the patient not referred to Psychiatry instead?**

It is:

> **During a prolonged period in which severe foot pain repeatedly generated death-wish language, fear, life contraction and “I won't live with it,” what clinical system owned the combined problem of pain + neurological treatment state + formulation + psychological risk?**

That question includes:

- pain control;
- suicide/self-harm risk assessment where indicated;
- safety planning;
- psychological support;
- Movement Disorders ownership;
- medication-formulation review;
- and explicit follow-up of the relationship between symptom control and psychological safety.

The currently assembled booklet demonstrates active concern and escalation.

It does not, by itself, demonstrate a clearly identified **longitudinal owner of the combined safeguarding problem**.

That distinction should remain visible.

### 9. The early procyclidine signal increases the significance of the later crisis

The crisis was not preceded by complete absence of information about neurological modifiability.

Years earlier, procyclidine withdrawal had already changed the foot state and focal outside-left-foot phenomenon.

So by the time pain became psychologically dangerous, the record already contained a historical reason to ask whether treatment-state instability was part of the pain mechanism.

The later OFF interpretation, Sinemet responsiveness and CR response did not arise from nowhere.

They progressively confirmed an older signal.

### 10. The human consequence of non-integration

This is where the information architecture becomes clinically consequential.

If the foot relationship remains:

> **“Not clear”**

and the structural foot, neurological state, pain treatment and psychological distress are each handled in different compartments, then the system can be very active while the combined causal problem remains insufficiently owned.

That is exactly the kind of failure the booklet has been describing:

> **local rationality; global non-integration.**

By 2023–25 the price of that non-integration was no longer abstract.

It had become a safety problem.

### Profile of Pain, Psychological Distress and Safeguarding

> **By March 2023 the unresolved foot problem had crossed from chronic morbidity into safeguarding territory. The patient said he was “losing the will to live”; Brennan described pain “causing death wish,” wrote “I am worried about him” and sought help “before we get into trouble here”; the Parkinson's service then recorded debilitating pain and “no quality of life” despite pregabalin escalating to 600 mg/day. The response was not absence of care: primary care investigated, prescribed, escalated urgently and involved Neurology, while specialist treatment continued. But the dangerous state did not reduce to one isolated crisis. Fear of recurrence made pregabalin reduction difficult, life became “a lot smaller,” and by March 2025 the record again contained “the first 150 footsteps are torture” and “I won't live with it.” The later treatment history changes how this period should be understood. The foot territory had shown neurological treatment-state dependence years earlier on procyclidine dechallenge; later OFF-state observations and Sinemet responsiveness strengthened that relationship; and controlled-release levodopa then produced profound relief. By 2026 a different dopaminergic delivery architecture had largely abolished the disabling foot pain without preserving the former disabling dyskinetic state. The safeguarding question is therefore inseparable from the treatment question. The issue is not simply whether somebody reacted to expressions of distress. It is whether the combined problem—severe pain, neurological treatment state, formulation, adverse effects and psychological risk—ever acquired sustained clinical ownership before a modifiable symptom had spent roughly two years repeatedly threatening the patient's ability to endure it.**

The irreducible form is:

> **The pain became a safety problem.**
>
> **The record shows people reacting to the danger.**
>
> **What is less clear is who owned the combined causal problem longitudinally.**
>
> **The later formulation history shows that the dangerous symptom was modifiable.**

# Risk Rank 32 — Original Profile 22

**Severity:** MATERIALITY / HARM / FAIRNESS  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Defines the minimum adequate alternative information process against which the actual pathway can be compared without overclaiming treatment outcome.

## Profile 22 — Minimum Adequate Alternative

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-143348).md; source text preserved in full below. -->

<!-- Original series label: Profile 11 of 12 — The Minimum Adequate Alternative -->

### What Would Have Been Enough?

One of the most important safeguards against hindsight in this case is to ask a modest question:

> **What would Neurology actually have needed to do in July 2017 to prevent the central information failure?**

The answer is strikingly small.

It did not require:

- proving PRKN;
- solving every cause of pain;
- starting Sinemet immediately;
- disproving neuropathy;
- eliminating Podiatry;
- or producing a perfect mechanistic explanation.

It required preserving one unresolved relationship honestly and actively.

### 1. The standard was not diagnostic perfection

By discharge, Neurology had already done enough to know that:

- the feet were structurally abnormal;
- the gait had a dystonic-looking lower-limb component;
- the patient had severe young-onset Parkinsonism;
- PRKN was plausible;
- and the relationship between those findings was unresolved.

That is enough to justify uncertainty.

The problem is not that the clinicians failed to reach certainty.

The problem is what they did with the uncertainty.

A minimally adequate discharge position could simply have said:

> **The patient has genuine structural foot disease and a dystonic/Parkinsonian lower-limb phenotype. The relationship between the two remains unresolved. Podiatry should treat the structural lesions, but the structural-dynamic relationship remains an active neurological question.**

That one formulation would have preserved the case.

### 2. Why that would have changed everything

Because then Podiatry would not become the final destination of the feet.

It would become one part of an open investigation.

The GP would know:

> **Neurology has not finished with this relationship.**

Later clinicians would know:

> **future foot changes may bear on an existing neurological question.**

Medication effects on the feet would have somewhere to land.

Repeated fifth-MTPJ recurrence would have somewhere to return.

The Protest would be consistent with the record rather than outside it.

The later OFF-related pain would not appear as a new idea.

Sinemet responsiveness would not require reconstruction from first principles.

### 3. A second foot examination would have been a simple corrective act

The patient was still in hospital.

The feet were physically present.

The dystonic-looking lower-limb finding was already known.

A second focused examination could have asked:

- what the toes were doing dynamically;
- whether the lateral forefoot was being overloaded;
- whether posture changed with gait;
- whether pressure sites aligned with the dystonic position;
- which abnormalities were fixed and which changed with movement.

Even if the result were:

> **still uncertain,**

the uncertainty would then be more clearly earned.

The minimum adequate alternative did not require certainty.

It required **returning to the object once the neurological meaning had changed.**

### 4. The Protest provided an obvious trigger

After the patient explicitly challenged the unresolved feet, the threshold for reopening should have become even lower.

At that point a minimally adequate response could have been:

> **The patient remains concerned that the feet have not been adequately addressed. We have re-examined them / we will seek further movement-disorders input / this remains an active question.**

That would have preserved both:

> the clinicians' judgment;

and:

> the patient's challenge.

No accusation was required.

No admission of error was required.

Just a fair record of the unresolved state.

### 5. The minimum adequate record needed to preserve ownership

The single most important thing was to assign the relationship somewhere.

For example:

> **Neurology/Movement Disorders to review if foot pain, toe posture or gait changes with neurological treatment state.**

That one instruction would have created a future trigger.

Then, when procyclidine withdrawal changed the outside-left-foot phenomenon, the record itself would say:

> **this matters.**

Without that instruction, the observation could remain local.

### 6. The minimum adequate alternative would have protected the Sinemet policy too

This is important.

Neurology could still have delayed Sinemet.

It could still have said:

> young age;

> dyskinesia risk;

> avoid conventional dopaminergic therapy for as long as reasonably possible.

Nothing about preserving the foot relationship requires changing that policy.

In fact, the policy becomes more defensible if the record says:

> **we are deferring levodopa, but we are actively monitoring whether the foot phenotype is neurologically modifiable.**

That would separate:

> treatment timing

from:

> phenotype completeness.

The current case becomes problematic precisely because those two issues became entangled.

### 7. The minimum adequate alternative did not require Neurology to “own Podiatry”

Podiatry still had an obvious role.

The callus was real.

The fifth-MTPJ lesion was real.

Offloading was reasonable.

The minimum adequate alternative was not:

> **Neurology should treat the corn.**

It was:

> **Neurology should retain ownership of the question of what neurological posture or gait is doing to the corn.**

That is a much smaller obligation.

### 8. It did not require predicting the future

The team did not have to foresee:

- 600 mg/day pregabalin;
- catastrophic 2023 pain;
- severe dyskinesia;
- the exact 2025 Sinemet CR response.

They only had to preserve enough uncertainty so that later evidence could modify the model.

That is the key.

A good record does not need to predict every future fact.

It needs to leave the case **updateable**.

### 9. The actual record made updateability harder

By saying:

> structural feet → Podiatry;

> relationship → “Not clear”;

> neurological plan → clear;

the document allowed later facts to be added without forcing structural-dynamic reassessment.

The minimum adequate alternative would have done the opposite:

> **uncertainty remains active; later changes must inform it.**

That difference is enormous.

### 10. A single sentence could have preserved the fork

Something as simple as:

> **Before discharge the patient expressed concern that the painful feet had not been adequately resolved during the admission; given the dystonic-appearing lower-limb phenomenon and structural foot pathology, their relationship remains an active neurological question despite Podiatry referral.**

That would have done several things at once.

It would preserve:

- the Protest;
- the unresolved relation;
- Podiatry's legitimate role;
- Neurology's continuing responsibility;
- and the reason later treatment-state information mattered.

The absence of such a sentence shows how small the missing safeguard actually was.

### 11. Why this profile is important for fairness

Without this profile, the case can sound as though it demands a perfect retrospective diagnosis.

It does not.

The criticism is much narrower.

The system did not need to know exactly what the fifth MTPJ meant in July 2017.

It needed to know that **it did not yet know**—and preserve that uncertainty in a form capable of being resolved later.

That is a far more modest standard.

### 12. The later record shows why this small safeguard mattered

If the relationship had remained active, then:

#### Procyclidine withdrawal

would bear directly on it.

#### Eight fifth-MTPJ treatments

would bear directly on it.

#### Bilateral cramping with tone

would bear directly on it.

#### GP reconnection

would bear directly on it.

#### OFF-related pain

would bear directly on it.

#### Sinemet responsiveness

would bear directly on it.

#### Sinemet CR profound relief

would bear directly on it.

The system would not need to rediscover the question in 2025.

The question would already be waiting for the answers.

### 13. The minimum adequate alternative also prevents self-validation

Had the Protest and unresolved relation been preserved, Monaghan could still point a reviewer to the 2017 record in 2025.

But the record would then tell the reviewer:

> **we knew this relationship was unresolved and contested.**

The document would not be able to present the admission as more settled than it was.

That would make later review much safer.

### 14. The smallest fair clinical model

The entire case could have been carried forward in four lines:

> **1. Severe young-onset Parkinsonism with dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenon.**
> **2. Genuine painful structural foot pathology, including recurrent pressure lesions.**
> **3. Relationship between the dynamic neurological phenotype and structural foot pathology unresolved.**
> **4. Podiatry to manage structure; Neurology/Movement Disorders to retain and reassess the relationship, especially if foot symptoms change with neurological treatment state.**

That is all.

No perfect diagnosis.

No hindsight.

No heroic medicine.

Just preserved clinical ownership.

### Profile of the Minimum Adequate Alternative

> **The July 2017 case did not require diagnostic perfection to avoid the later information failure. Neurology already knew enough to state a modest mixed model: genuine structural foot disease coexisted with a dystonic/Parkinsonian lower-limb phenotype, and the relationship between them remained unresolved. Podiatry could appropriately manage the structural lesions while Neurology retained ownership of the structural-dynamic question. A second focused examination after the dystonic finding, an explicit response to the patient's discharge-day challenge, or even a single sentence preserving the relationship as an active neurological problem would have been sufficient to keep the case updateable. The Sinemet restraint policy could remain unchanged. PRKN could remain unproven. Neuropathy could remain possible. The only essential safeguard was that “Not clear” remain a question with an owner rather than a destination. Had that occurred, later procyclidine responsiveness, repeated fifth-MTPJ recurrence, foot cramping with tone, OFF-related pain and Sinemet responsiveness would all have had an existing clinical question to update.**

The irreducible version is:

> **They did not need to solve it.**
> **They only needed to keep it open properly.**

And the strongest fairness point:

> **The case does not ask what perfect medicine would have done. It asks why ordinary uncertainty was not preserved in an ordinary way.**
