# Risk Rank 33 — Original Profile 05

**Severity:** SYNTHESIS / EVIDENTIAL CONTROL / SECONDARY  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Shows what primary care could not know because the specialist record did not carry the Protest, the fork or the full structural–dynamic problem forward.

## Profile 05 — What the GP Still Does Not Know

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-144328).md — first component; preserved verbatim -->

<!-- Original source heading: What the GP still does not know -->

Assuming Brennan's knowledge remains approximately where the surviving GP record leaves it, he knows an enormous amount.

He knows that he originally referred **one integrated feet–gait–tone problem**. He knows the hospital diagnosed very-young-onset Parkinsonism. He received the specialist position that the feet might be separate/peripheral and that the relationship was **“Not clear.”** He saw the dramatic response to procyclidine. By 2019 he was himself explicitly writing that the foot problems related to increased tone and abnormal Parkinsonian gait. In 2023 he recognised the catastrophic painful-feet presentation as, in his own words, essentially the original presentation returning. And by March 2025 he had a specialist letter telling him, in the same document, that the foot pain was extreme, pregabalin had failed to abolish it, Sinemet made the feet better, more Sinemet would probably help further, and dyskinesia prevented easy escalation.

So Brennan is **not missing the relationship clinically**.

What he is missing is the hidden history of that relationship.

### He probably does not know that the feet were only examined once

This may be the most important revelation for him.

He sent a patient into a university Neurology service with:

> feet + gait + tone + clawing + movement initiation + deteriorating function.

The service then discovered severe Parkinsonism, possible PRKN and a dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop.

A GP receiving the eventual discharge account could perfectly reasonably assume that, over an eight-day neurological admission, the feet had been reconsidered in light of those discoveries.

But they were not.

> **Monaghan examined the feet on day one. Nobody returned to examine them during the remainder of the admission.**

I doubt Brennan understands “Not clear” in that factual context.

That changes the phrase radically.

He may have received it as:

> **Neurology investigated the relationship but could not determine it.**

He was not told:

> **Neurology identified both sides of the relationship, did not return to the feet, and then recorded the relationship as “Not clear.”**

That is an entirely different information state.

### He does not know about the Protest

Unless the patient subsequently told him in a way we have not identified in the GP material, Brennan was not given the critical fact that **before leaving hospital the patient himself noticed exactly this problem and directly challenged Monaghan about it**.

That means Brennan was never told that “Not clear” had already been contested at source.

Imagine what that would have done to his reading of the discharge summary.

Instead of:

> difficult case → prolonged admission → unresolved feet → Podiatry,

he would have known:

> prolonged admission → no return to feet → patient notices → patient directly challenges treating neurologist → nevertheless the final account carries “Not clear + Podiatry.”

The Protest is not merely something Brennan does not know about the patient.

It is something he does not know about **the provenance of the specialist conclusion he was asked to inherit**.

### He does not know that his own original question was never actually answered

This is wonderfully subtle.

Brennan may think Neurology changed the question.

But in reality, **his question survived unanswered**.

He asked, in substance:

> What unifies these feet, this gait, this tone, this clawing, this initiation difficulty and functional deterioration?

Neurology brilliantly answered part of that:

> young-onset Parkinsonism.

It also recognised:

> dystonic-looking lower-limb behaviour.

But it never completed the second step:

> **how does that neurological phenotype relate to the painful structural feet Brennan sent in with it?**

Brennan may therefore not realise that when he wrote in 2019:

> feet issues relating to increased tone and abnormal Parkinsonian gait,

and again in 2023:

> this was actually his initial presentation to me six years ago,

he was not introducing a new GP theory.

He was **independently reconstructing his own original referral after the specialist system had partitioned it**.

That is something I think he deserves to know.

### He may possess both halves of the fifth-MTPJ story without knowing they are one evidential object

This is perhaps the most remarkable thing Brennan could be shown.

On the structural side:

> the same left fifth MTPJ was treated by Podiatry again and again—eight out of eight treatment encounters.

On the neurological side:

> after procyclidine withdrawal, the first rapidly changing focal phenomenon Monaghan records is the return at the **outside-left corn**, alongside deterioration in gait and relaxation.

Those are not two vague foot facts.

They concern the same persistent focal territory.

Brennan may have had access at different times to pieces of both streams. But nothing suggests he was ever invited to put them together as:

> **The same repeatedly failing structural site was clinically changing with neurological treatment state.**

That proposition is much more informative than either record separately.

### He probably does not know that Podiatry was missing the neurological half too

This would further change his understanding.

Podiatry repeatedly sees and treats the fifth MTPJ.

But the surviving Podiatry material does not show that it was told:

> **this outside-left-foot phenomenon changes rapidly when neurological treatment changes.**

So Brennan may have assumed that “Podiatry” represented another part of an integrated multidisciplinary plan.

In practice, Podiatry seems to have received the **structure without the dynamics**.

And Neurology retained the **dynamics without reintegrating the recurrent structure**.

The relationship Brennan kept seeing in primary care existed between those information systems.

### He probably does not know that the document remained open after the Protest

This is another important piece.

Brennan knew he was waiting for formal correspondence after discharge.

What he may not appreciate is the significance of that chronology.

The patient left on 11 July.

The document continued developing.

The DaT result arrived later and was incorporated.

The surviving synthesis received later consultant confirmation.

Therefore the final document was capable of incorporating information arising after physical discharge.

Yet the Protest did not enter the durable account.

That does **not** prove why it was omitted.

But it means Brennan did not receive a simple contemporaneous ward note. He received a **later-settled synthesis** in which some things were made portable and the Protest was not.

That is highly relevant to how much trust he reasonably placed in it.

### He does not know that the patient's intelligence was preserved while the patient's consequential question was not

Brennan received a document telling him that the patient had:

> exceptional cognitive ability;

> exceptional insight;

> an ability to question appropriately;

> and a good understanding of the treatment rationale.

Those statements would naturally reassure a GP that the complex plan had been carefully explained and processed.

What Brennan was not told was:

> **that this same intelligent, appropriate questioner had used those exact capacities to challenge the unresolved feet before leaving hospital.**

So Brennan inherited the patient's intelligence as evidence supporting the apparent coherence of the process.

He did not inherit the patient's actual challenge to that coherence.

I suspect that distinction would land very strongly with him.

### He probably does not know that “Not clear” never acquired a clinical owner

Brennan repeatedly behaved as though the relationship existed.

But he may not have noticed that nobody upstream had formally accepted responsibility for it.

Neurology owned Parkinsonism.

Podiatry owned structural lesions.

Brennan owned continuity.

Later Movement Disorders owned medication state.

But no document appears to tell Brennan:

> **Neurology/Movement Disorders retains responsibility for determining how the dystonic/Parkinsonian lower-limb phenotype interacts with the structural feet.**

He may have assumed somebody did.

That assumption would be entirely reasonable.

Apparently nobody did.

### He does not know that his own later observations were potentially corrective evidence against the founding arrangement

This is crucial.

When Brennan wrote in 2018:

> increased tone + bilateral foot cramping,

and in 2019:

> feet related to increased tone and abnormal Parkinsonian gait,

and in 2023:

> Parkinsonian tone likely contributes and this was the original presentation,

he may have thought he was simply reporting developments to the specialists.

He may not know that, seen against the actual 2017 examination history, **his observations were repeatedly supplying the very integration the founding record lacked**.

His record looks different when read that way.

Brennan becomes an independent longitudinal control.

Before Neurology:

> integrated.

After Neurology:

> inherits “Not clear.”

Later:

> repeatedly returns to integrated.

That is very powerful.

### He may not know what happened to his 2023 reconstruction upstream

By March 2023 he had supplied an extraordinary corrective packet:

> severe bilateral pain;

> high-dose pregabalin failure;

> previous pain improving when Parkinson's control improved;

> narrow therapeutic window;

> explicit connection to Parkinsonian tone;

> explicit recognition that this was the original presentation.

He sent that upstream.

What he does not appear to have received back is an equivalent specialist document saying:

> **Yes. We have now globally reconstructed the original feet–gait–tone relationship.**

Instead, local management continued.

So he may not realise that his very strong 2023 reconstruction did not actually produce a corresponding reconstruction of the historical specialist model.

### He probably does not know the significance of the 2025 rebuttal

This may be almost entirely outside his knowledge.

By 2025 Monaghan says:

> there has been a **“clear understanding for quite some time”** that Sinemet helps the pain.

At the same time he retains:

> **“If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here…”**

And, importantly, he tells the complaint reviewer that his clinical reasoning can be understood from the 2017 letters.

That means the discharge material Brennan originally received as clinical information is later being used by its author as **evidence validating the history it created**.

Brennan may have no idea that the document he reasonably relied upon downstream has acquired that later evidential role.

### What I think Brennan most fundamentally does not know

It reduces to this:

> **He knows what happened to the patient.**
>
> He does not know what happened to the information.**

He knows the feet.

He knows the Parkinsonism.

He knows the treatment responses.

He knows the pain crisis.

He knows the dyskinesia problem.

He knows that the relationship exists.

What he probably does not know is:

> **that the integrated question he sent to Neurology was never properly extinguished; that the patient identified the incompleteness before discharge; that the final document did not tell him this; and that he then spent eight years repeatedly rediscovering the relationship inside a specialist information frame that had made it look unresolved or separate.**

That is almost poignant, because **Brennan may have been much closer to the truth than he realised.**

### What could Brennan be told now?

I would not give him the whole project.

I would tell him something much simpler.

Something like:

> **In 2017 you referred one integrated problem: painful abnormal feet, abnormal gait, pathological tone, toe clawing, initiation difficulty and deteriorating function. During the eight-day Neurology admission, Parkinsonism and a dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenon were identified, but the feet were examined only on the first day and were never returned to after those neurological findings emerged. At discharge the patient expressly challenged Dr Monaghan that the feet had not been adequately dealt with. That challenge was not included in the final discharge account you later received, which instead described the relationship as “Not clear” and routed the structural feet to Podiatry.**
>
> **Afterwards, information developed in two separate streams. Podiatry repeatedly treated the same left fifth-MTPJ site on eight consecutive treatment visits. Neurology separately recorded that the outside-left-foot phenomenon changed rapidly when procyclidine was withdrawn, while gait and tension worsened. You subsequently reconnected the feet to increased tone and Parkinsonian gait yourself in 2019, and in 2023 explicitly recognised the severe pain as the original presentation returning and likely contributed to by Parkinsonian tone. Later Movement Disorders described pain and shuffling as OFF-related, and by 2025 Neurology acknowledged that Sinemet had been known for some time to improve the pain; Sinemet CR then produced profound relief.**
>
> **The issue is therefore not that the structural foot pathology was unreal, nor that the original Sinemet strategy was necessarily wrong. The unresolved issue is that the structural and neurological components were separated before their relationship was adequately established, and the patient’s contemporaneous challenge to that incompleteness was not transmitted to you. Your later records appear repeatedly to have reconstructed the relationship independently.**

That is probably enough.

And I think there is one sentence in particular Brennan should be given:

> **When you received “Not clear” in 2017, you were not told that the feet had only been examined once, that nobody returned to them during the eight-day admission, or that the patient had directly challenged that very incompleteness before leaving.**

That sentence changes what **“Not clear”** means to the GP.

It may change his understanding of the entire subsequent history.

# Risk Rank 34 — Original Profile 03

**Severity:** FOUNDATIONAL CONTEXT  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Provides the admission chronology and factual hospital setting from which the higher-order documentary defects are derived.

## Profile 03 — Profile of the Hospital Stay

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-114331).md -->

<!-- Original source heading: Profile of the Hospital Stay -->

### University Hospital Galway — 3 to 11 July 2017

#### The admission

The patient entered University Hospital Galway on **3 July 2017** by direct referral to the Medical Assessment Unit and remained an inpatient on St Anthony's Ward until **11 July 2017**.

The problem arriving with him was serious enough to require inpatient neurological investigation. The admission document reproduces a referral for a 39-year-old man with difficulty initiating movement, increased tone and stiffness in all four limbs and possible cogwheeling, requesting neurological assessment and further investigation.

The history then expands dramatically.

The hospital reconstructed a possible disease course extending approximately **thirteen years**, beginning around 2004 with abnormal bodily tension and unusual posturing. The patient described having to tense or position his body in order to experience relief.

The feet entered that history early.

He recalled subsequently developing:

- corns and calluses;
- apparent fallen arches in both feet;
- a requirement for repeated foot treatment from approximately 2009–2010;
- continuing foot problems;
- and significant pain which improved after he stopped prolonged bar work in 2012.

The hospital recorded his distinction between the subjective experience of foot pain and the continuing internal tension of the legs. The foot pathology itself remained present.

The history then tracks years of progressive neurological dysfunction: deteriorating dexterity, difficulty gowning and working, impaired coordination, abnormal leg movements, increasing difficulty initiating movement, hand locking, fatigue and progressive occupational impairment.

By July 2017 the patient was no longer presenting simply with an unusual gait. He was functionally failing.

### What Neurology examined

The neurological examination was extensive and detailed.

It recorded:

- markedly and symmetrically increased tone;
- probably greater involvement of the right upper limb;
- a small resting tremor eventually visible in the right lower limb;
- symmetrically brisk reflexes;
- reduced blink rate;
- mild hypomimia;
- striking gait abnormality;
- complete absence of arm swing;
- Parkinsonian upper-limb posturing while walking;
- lead-pipe rigidity bilaterally;
- axial rigidity;
- minimal right-sided cogwheeling;
- gross bradykinesia;
- preserved vertical gaze and normal saccades;
- slow coordination;
- micrographic/deteriorated handwriting;
- and abnormal hand posture.

The examination was sufficiently important that it was **video recorded with the patient's permission**.

The lower-limb phenomenology was particularly notable.

The gait was described as:

> **“strikingly abnormal”**

and the patient walked:

> **“almost with a footdrop appearance somewhat suggestive of a dystonic posture — NOT FOOT DROP.”**

The left lower limb was also described as kicking out **“like a foot drop.”**

This was therefore not an admission in which the lower limbs were neurologically unremarkable.

A potentially dystonic lower-limb phenomenon was explicitly recognised.

### What was recorded about the feet

Within that extensive examination, the direct physical findings recorded for the feet themselves are remarkably brief:

- **pes planus**;
- **hammertoes**;
- minimal/mild pitting around the sock bands.

The admission history already contained:

- longstanding corns;
- calluses;
- fallen arches;
- repeated foot treatment;
- persistent abnormal feet;
- and previous pain significant enough to change with occupational weight-bearing.

Yet after those brief structural observations, the surviving admission summary does not document another focused examination of the feet during the inpatient stay.

It does not record serial reassessment of:

- the corns or calluses;
- the painful anatomical sites;
- toe posture;
- plantar loading;
- footwear or pressure distribution;
- whether the dystonic-looking lower-limb posture altered the structural feet;
- whether the structural feet influenced the gait;
- or how fixed structural abnormalities were to be separated from dynamic neurological phenomena.

The neurological examination expands.

The investigations expand.

The differential expands.

The feet do not.

That is one of the defining characteristics of the admission as documented.

### The diagnostic investigation

The hospital then undertook a broad investigation of an unusually young patient with severe Parkinsonism.

The working impression was **young-onset Parkinsonism**, with possible symptoms extending back approximately thirteen years.

The differential was deliberately wide. The document considers:

- young-onset Parkinson's disease;
- an autosomal-recessive form;
- other synucleinopathies;
- Parkinson-plus disorders;
- glucocerebrosidase-associated disease;
- possible metabolic or metal-related conditions;
- acquired/toxic possibilities;
- and later specifically recessively inherited Parkinsonian genetics.

The patient was discussed at a **Neurology Complex Case Meeting**, where the working position was likely idiopathic Parkinson's disease, albeit with atypical features.

This was therefore not a superficial diagnostic encounter.

It was treated as an unusual neurological case requiring substantial investigation and specialist discussion.

### Imaging and laboratory investigation

During the admission the hospital performed or arranged an extensive investigation programme.

A **CT brain on 3 July** was obtained urgently because of severe Parkinsonism without an obvious cause. It showed no acute intracranial pathology.

A **chest radiograph on 4 July** was essentially unchanged apart from slight hyperinflation.

An extensive laboratory programme included or sought:

- inflammatory studies;
- copper/ceruloplasmin investigation;
- autoimmune testing;
- paraneoplastic and neuronal antibodies;
- anti-GAD antibodies;
- infectious screening;
- immunoglobulins/electrophoresis;
- vitamin and haematinic studies;
- hepatitis and syphilis screening;
- and other biochemical investigations.

An **abdominal ultrasound on 10 July** specifically investigated the possibility of splenic enlargement in the context of concern about Gaucher-related Parkinsonism. It found no splenomegaly, although an incidental gallbladder polyp was identified.

An **MRI brain and cervical spine on 10 July** was obtained because of severe atypical Parkinsonism without an obvious cause and the possibility of demyelination. The brain and cervical spinal cord were essentially normal, apart from minor findings that prompted further internal consideration.

The clinicians themselves reconsidered the imaging and entertained questions concerning basal-ganglia calcification, metal deposition and brainstem appearance.

Again, the distinguishing feature is the breadth of inquiry.

When an uncertainty arose around the neurological syndrome, the hospital investigated it.

### What happened to the foot question

After all of that investigation, the admission synthesis returns to the legs and feet.

It says that:

> **“The leg symptoms including corns and callouses and burning may have been completely separate or perhaps a secondary peripheral neuropathy…”**

and finishes:

> **“Not clear.”**

The document also records the patient's own phenomenological distinction between his internal leg tension and the pain associated with corns and calluses.

But the hospital stay as documented does not show an investigation equivalent to the investigations generated by the neurological differential.

There is no recorded neurological re-examination of the feet after the initial examination.

There is no documented focused attempt to partition:

> **fixed structural foot disease**
> **versus**
> **dynamic neurological foot/toe posture**
> **versus**
> **interaction between the two.**

Instead, uncertainty survives.

And the practical response is Podiatry.

### Management during the stay

The admission generated an elaborate treatment strategy.

Senior neurological caution around dopaminergic treatment is expressly recorded. The document states that **“the greatest of care”** had been advised with **Prof Counihan and Neurology colleagues** concerning future use of the more conventional dopaminergic therapies.

The concern was the patient's exceptionally young apparent disease onset and the long-term risks of:

- dyskinesia;
- impulse-control disorders;
- punding;
- and other complications.

The stated policy was therefore:

> **“We were likely try and avoid these for as long as possible.”**

Levodopa or dopamine agonists were not forgotten.

They were deliberately deferred.

Meanwhile a staged symptomatic plan was developed involving:

- clonazepam;
- later rasagiline;
- subsequent very slow procyclidine titration;
- possible future amantadine;
- physiotherapy;
- occupational therapy;
- exercise;
- extensive counselling;
- work restriction/redeployment considerations;
- Long Term Illness Scheme paperwork;
- and possible involvement with the Parkinson's Association.

This is an extraordinarily developed forward neurological management programme.

### Allied-health and foot pathways

The admission plan records:

- inpatient physiotherapy;
- inpatient occupational therapy;
- proposed Parkinson's Disease Advanced Nurse Practitioner input;
- community physiotherapy;
- community occupational therapy;
- and outpatient Podiatry/Chiropody.

For Podiatry the document asks the GP to refer and notes that an SHO had tried to arrange this within the hospital.

That is important because Podiatry unquestionably had a rational structural role: the patient had genuine deformity and longstanding structural disease.

But nothing in the pre-clinic-note admission summary shows Podiatry being asked to answer the neurological integration question.

Nor does it show Neurology returning to that question itself.

The foot pathway and neurological pathway therefore leave the admission in parallel.

### The physical discharge state — 11 July 2017

By the time of physical discharge, an enormous amount had happened.

The hospital had:

- reconstructed a thirteen-year neurological history;
- identified severe young-onset Parkinsonism;
- recognised dystonic-looking lower-limb phenomenology;
- videoed the neurological examination;
- performed extensive laboratory investigations;
- obtained CT brain imaging;
- obtained MRI brain and cervical spine imaging;
- investigated Gaucher-related possibilities;
- discussed the patient at a complex-case meeting;
- involved or contemplated multiple allied-health disciplines;
- sought senior neurological input about treatment;
- developed a detailed staged medication strategy;
- considered recessively inherited Parkinsonian disease;
- arranged genetic/second-opinion thinking;
- addressed work, disability and long-term follow-up;
- and directed the structural feet toward Podiatry.

What it had **not done in the surviving admission record** was return after the first examination and resolve the condition of the painful feet through which much of the original presentation had been experienced.

The admission therefore ends with a remarkable asymmetry:

> \*\*The patient was investigated intensely.
> His Parkinsonism was investigated intensely.
> His atypicality was investigated intensely.
> His genetics were considered intensely.
> His brain and spinal cord were imaged.
> Rare systemic and metabolic explanations were pursued.
> His future treatment was planned in considerable detail.
>
> The painful abnormal feet themselves were not revisited after the first-day examination.\*\*

That is the profile of the hospital stay.

### A necessary temporal distinction

The admission-summary object contains one important item that does **not** belong to the physical eight-day stay.

The **DaT scan occurred on 13 July 2017**, two days after physical discharge.

It showed marked bilateral reduction of uptake consistent with a Parkinsonian disorder and was subsequently incorporated into the evolving documentary account.

That fact should be kept separate.

For purposes of profiling what happened **inside hospital between 3 and 11 July**, the admission ended before the DaT scan.

Its later incorporation demonstrates that the documentary object continued developing after the physical admission had ended.

### The admission in one paragraph

> **From 3 to 11 July 2017, University Hospital Galway undertook an unusually extensive inpatient investigation of a 39-year-old man with a long, progressive feet–gait–tone syndrome and severe functional deterioration. Neurology identified marked symmetrical Parkinsonism, explicitly recognised a dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop, documented pes planus and hammertoes, videoed the neurological examination, discussed the patient as a complex case, pursued broad structural, metabolic, autoimmune, genetic and imaging explanations, consulted senior neurological expertise and constructed a highly detailed long-term treatment strategy. Against that extraordinary intensity of neurological inquiry, the surviving admission record shows only the initial examination of the structurally abnormal feet and no subsequent focused return to them during the eight-day stay. Their relationship to the neurological lower-limb phenotype therefore left the admission not resolved but “Not clear,” with Podiatry as the practical destination.**

# Risk Rank 35 — Original Profile 01

**Severity:** FOUNDATIONAL CONTEXT  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Defines the patient and integrated phenotype at entry to Neurology; necessary foundation rather than independent defendant exposure.

## Profile 01 — Patient Profile at Entry to Neurology — July 2017

<!-- SOURCE STATUS: supplied earlier in this project conversation. No separate indexed pasted-markdown attachment was recovered when this booklet was assembled. The content below preserves the supplied profile substance and known verbatim closing paragraph. -->

<!-- Original source heading: Patient Profile at Entry to Neurology — July 2017 -->

The patient who entered Neurology in July 2017 was a **39-year-old working man** with a gradual, multi-year illness rather than a recent diagnostic crisis.

Neurology reconstructed the possible beginning of the illness to approximately **2004**, with abnormal bodily tension and posturing. The history later acquired a distinct foot component: corns, calluses and apparent fallen arches, with repeated foot treatment from approximately **2009–2010** onward.

The feet were not a recent incidental complaint. They had existed as a longstanding bodily and functional problem before the neurological diagnosis was known.

The historical account also records that foot pain improved after the patient left prolonged bar work in **2012**. That change was important because the patient could distinguish the weight-bearing pain associated with the feet from the continuing internal tension of the legs. It permitted a **phenomenological distinction** between experiences. It did not, by itself, establish that the processes were causally independent.

By 2017 the illness was increasingly affecting ordinary function and employment. The patient worked in a medical-device environment in which gowning and ungowning, handling materials, repetitive manual activity and dexterity mattered. His deterioration was becoming sufficiently visible that his GP feared he could lose his job.

The patient was also an unusually detailed observer of his own movement. He described, among other things:

- using the deceleration of a bus to help initiate movement after sitting;
- the need consciously to initiate movement;
- an inability to relax;
- toe clawing in bed;
- declining dexterity;
- progressive interference with work and ordinary function.

Those observations mattered because the GP did not refer a predetermined Parkinson's case. He referred an **integrated and unresolved feet–gait–tone–toe–function syndrome** for neurological explanation.

The later discharge synthesis itself portrays the patient as highly intelligent and insightful, able to retain complicated information and capable of asking appropriate questions. The unusual length of the later-finalised document is expressly connected in part with the discussions and questions generated during the admission and with its intended use for the patient, debriefing and a clear plan.

The patient therefore entered the admission carrying at least three kinds of knowledge that matter to the later reconstruction:

1. **He knew his feet.** They had been painful, abnormal and treated for years.
2. **He could distinguish bodily changes.** He was capable of separating experiences such as weight-bearing foot pain from internal leg tension without thereby supplying a medical causal diagnosis.
3. **He could follow and question neurological reasoning.** The later hospital record itself treats him as unusually intelligent, retentive and appropriately questioning.

He did not enter hospital knowing what the specialist diagnosis would be.

He did enter hospital knowing the bodily problem through which he had sought help.

> **An intelligent, observant, independently functioning 39-year-old man entered a university Neurology department after years of evolving bodily dysfunction, with longstanding painful abnormal feet now embedded in an integrated syndrome of abnormal gait, pathological tone, toe-clawing, impaired movement initiation and deteriorating function. He did not yet know what the neurological diagnosis would be. He did know what his feet were like, why he had sought help, and whether anybody had returned to them. That is the patient who must be kept in view throughout the eight-day admission.**

# Risk Rank 36 — Original Profile 02

**Severity:** FOUNDATIONAL CONTEXT  
**Primary defendant exposure:** Maps the specialist environment and available expertise; contextual support for what the department could recognise and integrate.

## Profile 02 — Clinical Environment Profile — Galway Neurology, July 2017

<!-- SOURCE: Pasted markdown(20260901-113901).md -->

<!-- Original source heading: Clinical Environment Profile — Galway Neurology, July 2017 -->

The patient did not arrive in a neurological service in which very-young-onset Parkinsonism, dystonia, PRKN/PARK2 disease, dystonic feet or the treatment difficulties of recessive Parkinsonism were obscure subjects.

He arrived in a **university teaching Neurology department with unusually proximate expertise in exactly that territory**.

That proposition needs to be stated carefully. It does not mean that every member of the department examined him, read every page of his chart or participated in every decision. It means that the unresolved phenotype arose in an environment where the relevant clinical concepts and specialist expertise were demonstrably available.

### The treating and advisory environment

| Person / source | Position in the 2017 environment | Why it matters |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Dr Thomas Monaghan**                                        | Treating Galway neurologist and author of the principal longitudinal synthesis            | His own record identifies severe very-young-onset Parkinsonism, possible PRKN, the dystonic-looking pseudo-footdrop, structural feet and the treatment strategy. The project record also preserves his later statement that he was not a movement-disorders neurologist.                               |
| **Prof Timothy J. Counihan**                                  | Consultant Neurologist, Galway University Hospitals; senior Galway neurological expertise | Counihan was publicly identified in 2015 as a Consultant Neurologist at Galway University Hospitals and President of the Irish Neurological Association. He was a co-author of the major 2013 JAMA *Parkin Disease* paper and, in 2017, of Galway's “dystonic foot” PRKN case.                         |
| **Patrick Browne**                                            | Galway Movement Disorders specialist nurse / RANP                                         | Contemporary professional material identifies Browne as a Movement Disorders ANP within Galway Neurology. In the August 2017 *Neurology* paper he is specifically listed as **RANP**. He also subsequently appears directly in this patient's Parkinson's-service treatment pathway.                   |
| **Prof Daniel G. Healy**                                      | External Beaumont consultant neurologist and specialist second opinion                    | Healy was not part of the Galway inpatient team. He was an external expert to whom Monaghan subsequently turned for genetic/clinical advice. Importantly, Healy and Counihan had both co-authored the 2013 Parkin paper. Healy later examined the patient and recorded that he “probably has Park II.” |
| **Michael J. Hennessy / Patrick W. Cullinane**                | Galway Neurology colleagues                                                               | They co-authored the 2017 Galway dystonic-foot/PRKN clinical-reasoning case with Browne and Counihan. Hennessy is independently documented as a Consultant Neurologist in the Galway department in 2017.                                                                                               |

The patient's own discharge architecture expressly brings Counihan into the management environment: it states that the **“greatest of care”** had been advised with “Prof Counihan and Neurology Colleagues.” The surviving project material therefore does not require us to infer that relevant senior expertise existed somewhere in the hospital; the clinical narrative itself invokes it.

There is also the later-recovered clinician-to-clinician correspondence to Daniel Healy in which Monaghan wrote:

> **“Myself and Tim are keen to hold back on Levodopa insofar as maybe possible unless you'd advise otherwise?”**

The contemporaneous page itself does not name “Tim”; the project's later source reconstruction identifies Counihan from separate clinician material. That distinction should remain visible. But the page independently demonstrates that the patient's unusual young-onset presentation was being handled through a **consultative specialist network**, including referral to Healy for genetic advice and possible second opinion.

### Timothy Counihan and the pre-existing PRKN knowledge

The most important background publication is the 2013 *JAMA Neurology* article **“Parkin Disease: A Clinicopathologic Entity?”** Timothy Counihan and Daniel Healy were both authors. Counihan's affiliation was the School of Medicine at NUI Galway; Healy's was Beaumont Hospital.

Its clinical content is unusually relevant.

The paper describes PARK2/parkin mutations as the most common cause of autosomal-recessive juvenile- and young-onset parkinsonism. In its five parkin cases the mean age at onset was 34, and dystonia was prominent. The paper specifically describes the proposed phenotype as including **foot dystonia**. One of its cases began with **toes turning up and leg pain**, with foot dystonia on examination.

It also describes the classic therapeutic difficulty: sustained responsiveness to relatively low-dose levodopa alongside a tendency toward early dyskinesia and dystonia.

That matters because, four years later, the Galway admission contained a remarkably adjacent cluster:

**very young onset; probable long disease duration; marked symmetry; akinetic-rigid Parkinsonism; possible PRKN/PARK2; dystonic-looking lower-limb gait; abnormal feet; and concern about future levodopa-induced dyskinesia.**

The JAMA paper cannot prove what Counihan or anyone else thought about this individual patient's feet on any particular day.

It establishes something narrower but important:

> **PRKN disease presenting in young adults with dystonia—including foot dystonia—and combining strong dopaminergic responsiveness with an increased dyskinesia problem was established published knowledge to which senior clinicians in this patient's own advisory network had personally contributed.**

### Patrick Browne and the Galway movement-disorders infrastructure

Patrick **Browne** is the correct spelling.

By the years preceding the admission, Browne was publicly writing from the Neurology Department at Galway University Hospital as a **Movement Disorders ANP Candidate**, frequently alongside Counihan on Parkinson's and movement-disorder subjects. His published topics included Parkinsonism, Parkinson's treatment, dystonia and movement disorders.

This matters for a different reason from Counihan's role.

Counihan demonstrates senior consultant-level specialist knowledge.

Browne demonstrates that Galway had a **dedicated movement-disorders clinical infrastructure below and alongside the consultant level**. This was not simply a general-neurology department that occasionally encountered Parkinson's disease.

And Browne was not merely publishing in the abstract. He subsequently became directly involved in this patient's management, including later medication strategies documented within the record.

### “A demure teenager and her dystonic foot”

Then comes the extraordinary temporal proximity.

On **14 August 2017**, barely a month after this patient's July admission, *Neurology* published:

**“Clinical Reasoning: A demure teenager and her dystonic foot”**

by **Patrick W. Cullinane, Patrick Browne, Michael J. Hennessy and Timothy J. Counihan**, all affiliated with the Department of Neurology, University Hospital Galway / NUI Galway.

This was not merely a paper about Parkinsonism.

The case began with an abnormal gait and a foot posture producing the appearance of **foot drop**. The authors' phenomenological analysis was that the apparent foot drop was actually **dystonia**.

Subsequent examination disclosed symmetrical bradykinetic/parkinsonian features. Genetic testing then demonstrated **compound heterozygous pathogenic parkin mutations**, confirming autosomal-recessive juvenile parkinsonism. Treatment was commenced with **procyclidine**.

And the treatment discussion is particularly striking. The Galway authors say that levodopa response in parkin disease is typically very good, but because of the long anticipated duration of treatment and high dyskinesia risk, it may be prudent to postpone levodopa using an anticholinergic alternative.

That is why the paper belongs in the departmental profile rather than being deployed as some sort of retrospective “gotcha.”

It shows that at essentially the same time as this patient's admission, the Galway Neurology department was capable of publishing clinical reasoning built around this chain:

> **young patient → abnormal gait → apparent footdrop → recognise dystonic foot posture → emerging Parkinsonism → compound-heterozygous parkin disease → procyclidine → caution about early levodopa because of dyskinesia.**

The resemblance to individual components of this patient's admission is obvious. But we should be precise about what follows from it.

It **does not prove** that the teenager's case dictated what should have happened to this patient.

It **does prove that the concepts needed to recognise and investigate such a relationship were not remote, exotic or unavailable within Galway Neurology in July 2017.**

### Daniel Healy — the external control

Healy strengthens this profile because he provides something Galway itself sought: an outside specialist opinion.

The contemporary project record shows Monaghan asking Healy for advice regarding appropriate genetic testing and whether the patient should be seen in Dublin. Healy subsequently examined him in September 2017 and wrote:

> **“I think [he] probably has Park II”**

and recommended testing PARK2 first.

He also recorded a **dramatic improvement in motor symptoms with low-dose procyclidine and rasagiline** and noted that PARK II patients may remain well for long periods on relatively low doses of dopamine replacement but can become dyskinetic if over-used.

This is particularly important because Healy was also a co-author of the 2013 JAMA Parkin study. His current Beaumont profile continues to list genetic disorders of the nervous system within his scope of practice.

Thus the external opinion did not move away from the PRKN hypothesis.

It strengthened it.

### The department-level profile

Putting these sources together, Galway Neurology in 2017 can fairly be described as:

> **a university teaching Neurology department containing consultant and advanced-nursing movement-disorders expertise, actively publishing on Parkinson's disease, dystonia and PRKN-related disease, with senior clinicians who had contributed to published Parkin research and with a contemporaneous Galway case demonstrating dystonic pseudo-footdrop leading to compound-heterozygous parkin diagnosis.**

That is not an allegation.

It is the environment.

And it radically changes the context in which we should read the eight-day admission.

The difficult question is no longer:

> **Could anybody reasonably have known that a dystonic-looking foot/lower-limb phenomenon in a very young patient with suspected PRKN might deserve movement-disorders integration?**

The historical evidence makes that a poor question.

The much cleaner question is:

> **Given the expertise surrounding this admission, why did a patient with painful abnormal feet, suspected PRKN and a gait expressly recognised as dystonic-looking pass through eight days in that university department without anybody returning to examine and integrate the feet after day one?**

And when the patient himself finally forced that exact unresolved problem back into the room through the Protest, **why did the answer transmitted forward remain “Not clear” + Podiatry rather than an explicit unresolved movement-disorders question?**

That should probably be the final paragraph of the team/department profile, because it places the patient profile and the institutional profile directly opposite each other:

> **On one side: an intelligent, observant patient who had lived with the feet for thirteen years. On the other: a university Neurology department with unusually proximate expertise in young-onset Parkinsonism, PRKN, dystonia, dystonic feet and the therapeutic consequences of parkin disease. Between them lay eight days in which the feet were examined on admission and never returned to. The Protest is where those two profiles finally collide.**

---
